=== Section 3 - Networking ===


--- Introduction to Docker Containers ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing about the basics of Docker, the advantages that the Docker offers,
which really makes it as one of the great technologies available and also a small demo before we conclude
the video.
So let's go ahead and understand more about it now before we go ahead and understand the advantages
that the Docker offers, let's look into a typical installation of software workflow.
I'm sure that most of you might have already experienced this with your workstation.
Now let's say that we have a Windows operating system and you want to install certain applications or
you want to install a certain game.
Now the first step is to download the installer.
So installer would be a typical dot exe file.
Now once you download the installer, you go ahead and double click on the exe file so that the installer
starts to run.
Now, during the middle of the installation, many of you might have already experienced that you get
an error message during installation, saying that certain files are missing or certain dependencies
are missing, etcetera.
So you start to troubleshoot the issue so that you understand the fix to solve this specific error message.
Now, once you troubleshoot it and once you install a specific dependency, you rerun the installer
and maybe you get another error, which might not be the same as the previous one.
Now again, you go back to the same cycle of troubleshooting it and rerunning the installer.
Now you still remember this type of workflow you typically see specifically when you used to install
the GTA game.
Now it always used to say that there are certain DLLs which are missing certain dependencies which are
missing and you had to go through this software workflow at least a time or two to make sure that you
have all the dependencies and all the requirements to install the game.
Now this workflow that we were discussing over here, this is the end user workflow.
Now there is also a workflow related to the development.
Now let's say that this is a programmer who works very hard developing a software, so he has developed
this specific software.
Now, depending upon the way in which he has created the software, it might or it might not work out
of the box in the operating system.
So let's say that he has created a software through Visual Studio, so it might work directly in Windows.
It will not work in Linux, it will not work in Mac.
And this is a pain for the programmer because if he wants that software to work in Linux, if he wants
to make it work in Mac, it is really a pain.
He might use Java, but if let's say he does not really wants to use Java out of the box, it really
becomes difficult for the developer as well.
So what he can do, he can create the software, he can put it in a Docker container.
Now he can deploy the Docker container in Windows, he can deploy it in Linux, he can deploy it in
Mac, and he can be 100% sure that the Docker container software that he has tested in his local environment
will work perfectly in Windows, it will work perfectly in Linux and it will work perfectly in Mac and
all the operating system which the Docker supports.
So this really becomes much more easier for the developer to push his software to a wide variety of
operating system and also all the dependencies which are required by this specific software.
Because during the second slide that we are discussing that a software installation typically fails
due to dependency issues.
ET cetera.
He can put all of these dependencies within the Docker container itself, so once he deploys the Docker
container to any operating system, it will work out of the box and the end user does not really have
to do anything other than running this specific Docker container.
So with this, let's understand what Docker is in a definitive terms.
So Docker is basically an open platform.
Now once we build a Docker container, we can run it anywhere.
We can run it in Windows, we can run it in Linux, we can run it in Mac, whether it is a laptop,
it is in a data center or it is in a cloud.
Now it follows build once run anywhere approach.
So that basically means that once the software developer has built his own Docker container, he can
run it anywhere.
He can run it in windows, he can run it in Linux, he can run it in Mac as well, in a perfectly working
condition depending upon the test that he has performed while he has created the Docker container.
So this is the theoretical perspective.
Let me quickly give you a demo so that we have a good understanding about this.
So for our demo purposes we'll be taking the example of Nginx.
Now, Enginex is a very popular web server in the market.
Now we'll consider Nginx to be this software which has been written by an organization.
Now if you look into the technical specification of Nginx under the supported distribution, it does
support Amazon Linux.
Then you have CentOS, DB and FreeBSD.
Then you have the Red Hat Linux, Susie, Ubuntu, etcetera.
But it does not really support Windows.
And this is the reason why I intentionally, I have used this specific web server for our demo purpose.
Now what we need to ensure is that if we try to install this in Windows, it will not really work because
you will not have a exe file out of the box.
So what you need to do is you can put this entire Nginx within a Docker container and once you have
done that, you can deploy the Docker container in Windows Linux as well as Mac.
Now again, Mac is not supported here, but if it is under the Docker container, it will work irrespective
of whether Nginx is supported out of the box or not.
Now the first thing that we typically need to verify whether the Nginx docker container is available
or not.
So this is the Docker Hub website and here you see there is a Nginx container which is available and
this is the official image.
So let me click over here and if we go a bit down, you see this is the Nginx official image and basically
here is the command to pull the Nginx image.
And if you go a bit down, you will see various commands which are available in which you can run the
specific image.
So generally there are two steps which are involved.
Generally when you use Docker, the first step is basically this specific Docker container image.
It would be in a specific website.
Now Docker Hub is one such website, so you have to pull this image or I would say you have to download
this image within your operating system where you want to start the container from it.
Now for that you have the command of Docker pull Nginx.
Now once the image is downloaded, you can go ahead and start the container from it.
So let's look into how exactly this would work.
Now let me copy this up and from my windows cli, I'll paste the Docker pull Nginx command.
So this command will go ahead and download the Nginx image from the Docker hub repository.
Now do remember that before this command works, you need to make sure that you have the Docker daemon
installed within your operating system.
Now Docker supports Windows Linux as well as Mac.
And basically if you see over here, I have the Docker daemon which is running and this is the reason
why my Docker command works perfectly.
So let's quickly wait for a moment for our image to get downloaded.
All right.
So our Nginx image has been successfully downloaded.
Now the next thing that you need to do is you have to start the container from that image.
So for that I'll write a simple command of Docker run.
I'll specify a port mapping, let's say 80, 80 to the 80 of the container.
Let me specify the container image, which is nginx.
So it has given us the long output over here.
But if we quickly do a Docker PS, then you should see that there is a Nginx container which has started
up.
Now in order to verify, I am in my ignito mode and I'll put 127001 colon 88 and now you see it basically
is showing me the index dot HTML page of nginx.
So that means my nginx is perfectly working in the Docker container and I'm able to access that within
my Windows browser.
So this is the great advantage of the Docker containers.
You don't really need to worry whether it is supported or whether the application is supported in Windows
or not, or whether the application is supported in Linux or not.
As long as you can put it under the Docker container, you will be able to run it in Windows Linux as
well as Mac or in fact any operating system where the official Docker daemon is supported.
So with this, I hope you understood at the high level overview on what Docker is all about.
Now one great thing in fact two advantages we just saw.
One was Nginx was not supported in Windows but through the Docker container.
It is perfectly working.
And second is we did not really have to worry about installation of Nginx and taking care of all of
the dependencies.
ET cetera.
All we did was we pulled this specific docker container of Nginx and that container itself had all the
required dependencies to run the Nginx web server.
And once we pulled it, we went ahead and started the Docker container and that's about it.
And this is the reason why Docker as a technology has become extremely popular.
And typically if you look into the roles of DevOps or SREs, having a good understanding of Docker is
a mandatory thing which a lot of organizations are looking for.
And even as a software developer, primarily because of such advantages that it offers, a lot of organizations
are also mandating that the software developers should also have at least basic knowledge about the
Docker containers.
So this is the high level overview about the introduction to Docker.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

=== Section 6 - Security ===


--- Creating Infrastructure for Docker Setup ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we'll be discussing about the infrastructure that will be creating for a Docker
setup.
Now, as we are discussing in the earlier video to begin with, in the initial sections, you can go
ahead and decide to install the Docker desktop directly within your laptop.
However, for this course, the preferred choice for Docker installation would be Linux.
Now do note that we'll be exploring both the Linux based installation method as well as the Docker desktop
based installation method in the upcoming videos.
Now, if you're using a Linux based installation method, then you will need a server.
So to begin with, what we'll be doing is we'll be creating a new server in the digital ocean environment
in case if you have a credit for other providers, you can go ahead and launch a new server in that
provider.
One important part to note here is that make sure that you choose the Ubuntu operating system primarily
because the video for installing Docker in Linux would be based on Ubuntu OS.
So once we have the server up and running, the next step would be to install the docker and then we
can go ahead and learn various features of Docker.
Now whenever you launch a new server, depending upon the cloud provider that you choose, there are
multiple authentication methods that are available.
The first primary one is a password based authentication, so this is a simplest authentication method
available.
So let's assume that you have a server up and running.
You go ahead and you log in with the username, so you try to connect to the server with the SSH username
and the server will respond back asking you for the password.
Now, although the password based authentication is supported by multiple cloud providers, this is
not a recommended approach specifically if your servers over the internet.
So the better approach is a key based authentication.
So in this type of authentication, there are two special types of keys that are generated.
One is referred as a public key, second is referred as a private key.
So if you look into this diagram, you have two keys, you have a public key and you have a private
key.
So what happens in this approach is that the public key that is created, that public key is stored
in the server that you launch, and in order to log in to the server, you will have to use the corresponding
private key.
So let me quickly show you this.
So currently you see that there are two keys that are created.
One is the private key over here and second one is the public key.
Now if I show you the contents of the private key, so this is just a test key.
You see the contents of the private key is much more bigger.
And this is the reason why specifically, if you have servers over the Internet that has a password
based authentication enabled, you will see that the attackers would launch a brute force attack and
they'll try thousands of password combination to connect to the Linux server with various usernames.
However, if you have a key based authentication and the key is so big, it becomes very difficult to
go ahead and run a brute force attack here.
And this is the reason why for the server that we will be launching over Digitalocean, we will be choosing
a key based authentication.
All right.
So currently I'm in the Digitalocean console and what we need is we need a new Linux server.
So in Digital Ocean the server is referred as a droplet.
Now again, depending upon the cloud provider that you choose, the name changes.
So in AWS it is called as the EC2 instance and so on.
So let's go to the droplets here.
And currently I have one droplet that is available for our Splunk course.
However, let's go ahead and create a new droplet so you can go ahead and click on Create.
You can select the droplet over here.
Now within the first thing that you will be choosing is the operating system.
You can choose the Ubuntu one.
Now the next important setting is to choose a specific droplet size.
So there are multiple droplet size that are available depending upon the Ram and CPU requirements.
We'll be using the basic one, which is the $5 per month droplet over here.
The next important part is to choose a data center region.
You can select the data center, the one that is closest to your location.
Since I'm based out of India, I'll be using Bangalore.
The next important part is the authentication.
Now do note that there are multiple authentication methods that are available.
As we were discussing, you have a password based authentication where you can go ahead and you can
type the password that you need for the server.
This works perfectly well.
However, this is not the best of the approaches.
The ideal approach is to go ahead and create an SSH key.
So currently you see I have multiple SSH keys that are available.
In fact, each ssh key is for a different droplet, for a different course.
So the Splunk keys or a splunk course console keys for our console course you have.
For our Kubernetes course.
So what we'll be doing is we'll be creating a new SSH key.
So let's go ahead and create a new SSH key and it will ask you to paste the contents over here.
Now currently I'm in my Windows terminal and I have the SSH related utilities that are already installed.
So if I just type SSH, you will basically get a prompt related to usage.
Now in case if you're using Windows operating system and if the SSH is not really present, then you
can go ahead and enable it as part of the Windows features.
Now let me quickly show you.
So in Windows, if you explore the optional features over here and within here, you have to make sure
that the open SSH related packages are enabled.
If they are enabled, then this command will work for Mac OS.
You do not really need to worry because SSH client comes by default.
So it is very straightforward in Mac.
Now in order to go ahead and create a new public private key, you'll have to run a command called as
SSH key gen.
It is asking you to select the location where this public and private key will be stored.
Let me go ahead and copy this up.
I'll paste it here.
The name of the key.
You can change it.
Let's say Docker underscore RSA, I I'll not enter any passphrase for the key.
I'll press enter multiple times and are public and private key is created.
So let's go ahead and explore this.
So within my C drive I'll go to users.
Let's go to zalora and you have a SSH directory here.
And currently you see I have a docker underscore RSA, which is the private key.
Do note that you should not share this key with anyone.
This is like a password and you have a docker underscore RSA dot pub which is a public key and this
is the key that you will be uploading it to the server.
So let's go ahead and open this public key.
You can open it up with any utility like notepad that you have within your operating system.
So this is the public key.
Let's go ahead and copy this up.
And within our digitalocean we'll go ahead and paste this key.
Let's just call it as Docker key.
All right, We'll add the SSH key here.
So now under the SSH key, you can select the Docker key once.
Then the next step is to choose a hostname.
Now you can go ahead and choose any name that you require for your droplet.
You can say something like Docker demo or any name that you intend to use here.
And optionally, if you have multiple projects available within the Digitalocean console, you can just
select the appropriate project.
If you do not have multiple projects, you can just use the default settings that is available once,
then you can go ahead and create a droplet.
Perfect.
So it took around two minutes and our new droplet is up and running.
Now, if you'll see, you basically get a public IP, which you can use to connect to your server.

--- Docker Image vs Docker Containers ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we'll have a very high level overview about certain terminologies like images
and containers with respect to Dockers.
Now, when you start to work with the Dockers, the Docker image and Docker containers is one of the
terminologies which will come pretty often.
So let's look into what exactly they mean at a high level overview.
Now the Docker image is a file which contains all the necessary dependencies and configurations which
are required to run an application.
So let's understand this with a below diagram.
So let's say that you have a Docker image for the Nginx web server.
So this image of Nginx would have the Nginx binary, it would have all the necessary configurations
related to Nginx.
It will also have all the necessary dependencies which are required for the Nginx web server to start.
All right.
So this image is basically all that is needed for the application to start in a high level overview.
Now this image by itself is not running.
So a Docker container is basically a running instance of an image.
So from image, we launch a container and this is the container which is running and where the execution
happens.
So let's understand this with a quick demo.
So before we go ahead and we can start the container, basically container is a running instance of
image is something that we discussed.
So before we can start the container, we need to have the image within our workstation or within our
servers.
Now within the Docker hub, which is Hub.docker.com, there are a lot of container images which are
already available.
Now you have image of Nginx, MongoDB, Alpine, Redis, etcetera.
So depending upon the use case that you have, you can go ahead and download the required image.
So let's go ahead and click on Nginx.
So first thing, as we already were discussing in the introduction video, the first thing before we
can start the Docker container is we need to download the image associated with that container.
So for our Nginx container we need to download the associated Nginx image.
So if I quickly copy this up and within my CLI, if I do a docker pull Nginx.
Now this command will go ahead and download the Nginx image which is there within the Docker hub.
Great.
So now within the status you see it states that it has downloaded the newer image of Nginx.
Now if you quickly do a Docker images, you should see that there is one image which is available and
the size is 109 MB Now this is just the image.
There is nothing which is running.
So if I just try to open this up and see whether the Nginx page loads in browser or not, it will not
load because this image is just a file and this is not running.
So if I can quickly do a docker.
PS you see that there are no containers which are running.
So now what I need to do is I need to create a new container from the specific image.
So in order to do that, I'll type Docker run, I'll say hyphen d t, I'll specify the port, let's
say the port is 8080, and you can specify the image ID, so I can specify the image ID here or you
can also type Nginx.
So when I press enter, it should give you a long string.
And now if you do a docker PS you should see that there is one container which has been created.
Now this container is basically what we were discussing, a running instance of an image.
All right.
Now in order to verify whether things are working or not, let me do a ifconfig.

--- Port Binding ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing about the port binding now in one of the Docker run commands
that we have been executing.
If you remember, we always used to give the hyphen P option and then we used to specify the ports over
here.
So it is important for us to understand on what exactly this means and what is the use of it.
Now, by default, Docker can make the connections to the outside world, but the outside world cannot
connect to the Docker containers directly.
Now, if we want the containers to accept the incoming connections from the world, we need to bind
it to a specific host port.
So let's understand this with a diagram here.
So we have a Nginx container which is running now.
Nginx container is running on port 80.
So by default someone from outside world will not be able to connect to my nginx on port 80.
All right.
Because this nginx container itself has a different ip address altogether.
So what we have to do is if someone wants to connect to my nginx, we have to bind the port 80 of my
nginx to one of the ports of the host machine.
So let's say you can bind it to a port 8080 of the docker.
Host Now after you bind it, what happens is that from the outside world, if someone tries to open
up the IP address of the Docker host followed by the port 8080, then the request would be directed
to the port 80 of the Nginx container.
So this is the high level overview.
Again, this would be little confusing.
So let's jump into practical and understand on what I mean by this.
So if I do a Docker, PS we have one container image which is up and running Now, this container image,
if you see over here, there is a bind.
So it is under the ports section over here.

the straight line.
So if I just copy it up in the notepad, you will see that under the ports section you have the port.
So this port that you see over here, this port is the port of my host and this is the port of my docker
container.
So this means that any request that comes to the port 80 of the Docker host would be sent to the port
80 of the Docker container.
Now, since this is the Nginx web server, it is listening on Port 80 inside the Docker container.
However, if you would like to see if I quickly do a Docker inspect, let's do a Docker inspect.
You see it is giving a lot of information.
Now one of the information is the IP address.

So one 7270 0.2 So this is the private IP address.
That means that from the outside network I will not be able to connect to the private IP address over
here.
All right.
So it's like the Nginx web server is listening on the private IP address.
So the Docker container has an IP address of 172 dot x.
Let's assume this and it is listening on port 80.
Now from the internet.
I cannot connect over here since this is a private networking that is being created.
So what I need to do is that I have to connect to the host.
This is the Docker host, so let's try it out.
So let me do one thing.
I'll do a Docker run command again.

So base command is you have the Docker as the base command and then you have various commands like attach,
build, checkpoint, commit, etcetera.

So if you see over here you have a Docker container.
Now let's click here and within the Docker container also you have commands like Docker container attach,
Docker container commit.
However you see on the base command, also you have something like Docker attach, then you have Docker
commit, etcetera.
So both of these commands does the similar thing.
However, Docker is now moving towards this specific command structure.
So for the individuals who have been using Docker since long time, they are still used to commands
like Docker attach, Docker build, however we should be.
Familiar with the Docker Container Command, because moving forward, this is the correct way in which
you can define things.
So let me quickly show you a quick demo.
So currently if I do a Docker PS here you see that there are multiple containers.
Now you can run a command like Docker Stop container zero one and this will work perfectly fine.
So our container is stopped.
However, you can also make use of Docker container stop and you can specify the other container which
is currently running, which is container zero two.
So you see it does the same thing.
Now if you do a Docker PS over here, both the containers are stopped.
So earlier we never had the Docker container.
We always used to make use of the Docker followed by the sub command.
However, nowadays Docker has started to add a lot of things under the Docker container.
So if you see you have Docker container attached, Docker container, CP, docker container create,
etcetera.
So this is the correct way.
And since you are learning Docker, it's better to get used with the newer approach of commands.

--- Importance of IT flags ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing the importance of the flag.
Now, if you remember during the Docker container exec video, we had basically run this specific command.
Now, while running this command, we had specified two flags.
One is AI and second is TI.
So in today's video, we will look into what exactly these flags are all about.
Now, one important part to remember is that any process that you create in a Linux environment basically
has three open file descriptors.
One is stdin, second is stdout and second is STD error.
Now let's say that there are two processes which are running over here.
One is bash and second is Unix.
So bash also has STD in std out std error.
Same with Unix.
You have std in stdout and stderr error.
Now std in is basically the standard input.
So any input that you specify to this specific process goes via STD.
So one of the ways in which you can put the input is through your keyboard.
So if you type certain commands, that is the command that goes to the STD in, then you also have various
pipe operators through which you can put the STD in.
So this is the STD in so this can be better understood.
So let's say this is your terminal so that the things that you type in it goes to the STD in and the
things that shows up in your screen.
It comes through the STD out.
Now hyphen I flag so let's go a bit up.
So hyphen I flag basically keeps the STD in open.
So let's go back to our practical and look into how exactly it might look like.
So let me do a docker.
PS here I have one container up and running.
Now let's say Docker container exec.
It will run the same command that we had run in the earlier video to keep the things straight.
I'll say.
Bash And now you see, as expected, we are logged in inside the container.
So here if I type various commands, I'll be able to get the relevant output.
So let's come out of the container.
Now let's do one thing.
Let's run without it Flag.
All right.
And now you see, I automatically got out of the container, so nothing came.
Now let's try one thing.
Let's specify the hyphen.
I flag over here, which basically is for standard in.
So now, after I ran this command, although it did not really get the T over here, but if I type ls,
you will see I'll be able to see the output.
If I type commands like PWD, I'll be able to see the output.
However, you do not really have a T, so let's press ctrl C and now let's put hyphen T, so when you
put hyphen t, you get a T.
So now if you type T, this is the T, and basically this is like a nice way of representation where

--- Restart Policies in Docker ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the restart policies.
Now, by default, Docker containers will not start when they exit or when the Docker daemon is restarted.
This is very important because let's say that you have this Docker host and there are two Docker containers
which are running.
One is of Nginx and second is of Apache.
Now, due to some reason, the Docker daemon got restarted now after the Docker daemon got restarted,
the containers which were running will not automatically start.
So one quick example is let's say you restart your server and after the restart of your server, your
Docker daemon, once it comes up, then the containers which were running previously will not automatically
start.
So in order to solve this issue, Docker provides us with the restart policies which allows us to control
various aspects related to containers, let's say, when specifically when they exit or when the Docker
restarts.
Now we can specify the restart policy by using the hyphen hyphen restart flag with the Docker run command.
So there are various restart policies which are mentioned over here.
Now before we go ahead and understand that, let me show you a quick demo.
So if I do a Docker, PS there are no containers which are currently present, so I'll just create one
container and if you do a Docker.
PS So this is the container of Nginx which is currently running now due to some reason, let's say our
Docker daemon got restarted, so let me restart it.
Alright, so the Docker daemon got restarted.
Now there can be various other reasons.
One can be the server itself got restarted or second reason might be that the Docker daemon got killed
due to some memory issue or various other factors as well.
So now if I do a docker, PS you see the Docker container did not automatically start.
So if you do a Docker PS hyphen A the Docker container is in the exit state, so now I'll have to manually
start this Docker container.
All right.
So although for testing purposes this might look fine, but when you run this in production and in production
due to patching activity, restart of server happens quite a lot.
So you want that your containers of production should automatically start when the server restarts.
So it is very important that we specify a appropriate Docker restart policy for our Docker containers.
Now the restart policy, basically there are four flags that we can offer over here.
One is no, that basically means that do not automatically restart the container and if you do not specify
the hyphen hyphen restart flag, this is considered as a default one because till the time whenever
we run the Docker run command, we never specify the restart flag.
And this is the reason why this no was considered as a default one.
Now the second one is on failure, which basically means that restart the container if it exits due
to an error which manifests as a non-zero exit code.
Third one, which is pretty important, and this is the one which is used in all the production, is
unless stopped.
So unless stopped basically means that restart the container, unless it is explicitly stopped or Docker
itself is stopped or restarted.
And the last one is always that basically means that always restart the container if it stops.
So unless stop is something that we are interested in.
So let's look into how we can specify this specific flag and what exactly this does.
So what we'll do is we'll run the same Docker container run command.
However, this time we'll have to specify the restart flag and I'll specify unless stopped.
All right, so now if I do a Docker, PS, this is the container which got started and it got started
with a restart policy of unless stopped.
So now let's go ahead and restart our Docker container and after restart, if I do a docker.
PS you see that my container automatically got started.
So anytime when the Docker daemon gets restarted or anytime when your server itself gets restarted,
you can ensure that your Docker containers will automatically get started.
So this is the high level overview about the restart policies in Docker.
Make sure that any time you create a Docker container in production, make sure you put the restart
of unless stop.
I do remember that in one of the organizations that I was working with, there was one system admin
guy like he was learning Docker and he created his application in Docker and he pushed it to production.
And during the monthly life cycle of patch management, after the server was restarted, his container
never came up and the monitoring team were after him saying that the Docker container is stopped at
the middle of the night when the patching was completed.
So then he had to start the Docker container manually and then he realized that there was something
called as restart policies.
So anyways, he could have improved upon that if he would had gone through this course anyways.
I'm just kidding.
So with this, we'll conclude this video.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

Hey, Aaron.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about the difference between the copy versus add instructions
in dockerfile.
So it is important for you to understand the difference because in exams you might get question related
to which one you should use for a specific use case.
So let's go ahead and have a quick demo post which will go ahead and understand more about this.
So I'm in my CLI and there are three files which are created here.
One is add dot txt, second is copy dot txt and third is the dockerfile.
So let's quickly open up the dockerfile and see the contents.
Now this is a very simple dockerfile So in the first instruction we have a from instruction.
So this will basically create an image from BusyBox.
Then we have a copy instruction here which would copy the file called as copy dot txt to the temp directory.
You have add instruction which would add the file called as add dot txt to the temp directory and you
have the cmd instruction which basically runs the shell.
So let's do one thing.
Let's quickly build this specific image.
So I'll say as Demo BusyBox.
Great.
So our image is built.
So if you can quickly do a Docker images, you will see that you have one image which is present over
here.
So we can quickly create a new container from this specific image.
I'll call it as Demo BusyBox and we can launch it from the BusyBox image.
Great.
So basically, before we go ahead and connect to the container, let's understand this Docker file.
Now, in the first instruction, if you see it, is basically copying a file called as copy dot txt.
So we have the copy dot txt file here and it is putting it inside the temp directory.
In the next instruction we are adding a file called as add dot txt.
We also have add dot txt in the same directory and we are moving it to the temp.
So in the container that we have created both copy dot txt here and the add dot txt here should be within
the temp directory.
So let's quickly find it out.
So I'll do a docker ps to verify if everything is running and yes it is.
So let's connect to the container.
Now, if you go to the temp directory here and if you do an ls hyphen L, you will see both the files
added dot txt and copy dot txt which are part of the temp directory inside the container.
Now in exams you might get quiz related to the difference between add and copy and which one you should
use for a specific use case.
So with the basic understanding set, let's go ahead and understand about each one of them.
Now the copy instruction and the Add instruction are basically serving a similar purpose.
Now both of them allows you to copy files from a specific location into a Docker image.
Now the copy basically takes in a source and the destination.
So basically, whatever file that you might have in the source, the copy will copy that specific file
in a specific source.
So source can be a local file or a directory and it will copy that file inside the container image.
So that is what copy does.
Now add also does a similar thing, which copy instruction does, but it supports two other kind of
sources.
So the first one is that Add will allow you to make use of URL instead of local file or directory.
Now copy can only copy a file which is present locally or within a specific directory.
Now Add can also do that, but in addition to that it can also fetch the file from the URL and it can
move it to a specific container directory.
Now the second important aspect of the Add command is that it can allow you to extract a tar file from
the source directly into the destination.
So if you have a compressed file within your source, then if you make use of Add instruction, add
instruction will decompress the file and it will store the contents of the decompressed file into the
destination.
Now, do remember that although you can make use of add instruction to fetch the package from the remote
URL, making use of that is strongly discouraged.
Instead of that, you should make use of curl or wget instead.
So if you see over here.
So in this case we are basically adding so you have a add instruction which copies this file.
So this is a compressed file and it stores the files inside the user C things directory.
Now after it has done that, you are making use of the run instruction to decompress the specific contents
of the compressed file.
And then you are again you have a run instruction which does the building part.
So if you are aware about the layering, then this will create three layers within the image.
Now instead of that, so what Docker says is that you should avoid this kind of a scenario because this
will add multiple layers to the image which will increase the size.
So instead of that, you can make use of curl and you can make use of the and an instruction over here.
So if you basically make use of the and an instruction not only it will help you have the lower layers
but it will also help you have the lower size of the image.
So this is one part to remember.
Now, the copy instruction here.
So whenever you have a local file which is currently present within your host and you want to copy that
file inside a container image, then you can make use of copy, avoid using Add there.
Now if you have a compressed file within your host and you want to decompress it and send it inside
the container image, in that cases you can make use of Add.
So let's quickly look into one of the supported sources of Add.
So let's do one thing.
I'll come out of the container and I'll create a file called as compress dot txt and let's go ahead
and compress this specific file.
All right, so now if you do ls hyphen l you will see that we have a compressed file which is present
over here.
Now let's change our Docker instruction here where I'll add one more add instruction.
I'll say compress dot tar dot GZ and I'll put it to TMP.

decompression on this specific file and whatever decompressed files which were present here, it moved
it inside the container.
So that is what Add is specifically used for.
So simple and short.
Let's say that you want to move a file like add dot txt or copy dot txt to the image.
You can avoid using the Add command.
You should make use of the copy command in case you have a compressed file and you want to move the
contents of this compressed file inside the container you can make use of Add.
Now Add also has the capability to fetch files from the remote URL and that is something that you should
avoid and instead you can make use of curl or wget.

--- EXPOSE Instruction ---

Hey, Ron.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video will be discussing about the expose instruction in Docker file.
Now the expose instruction, it informs Docker that a container listens on a specified network port
at a runtime.
So let's say that you have a Docker image and the service or the application within that Docker image
is listening on port number 5000.
So how will a individual who is running the Docker container know that a service is listening on Port
5000 because the service can listen on 8000, 9000 or any ephemeral port.
So the expose instruction precisely informs on that aspect.
Now, as we are discussing, the primary aim of expose instruction is not actually to publish the port,
but to inform on which port a specific application or a specific service is running.
So if you want to actually publish a port at a Docker level, then you will have to use the hyphen P
option.
So the third point, as it states that this expose instruction, it functions as type of a documentation
between the person who builds the image and the person who runs the container about which port are intended
to be published.
Now, generally what happens is that there are hundreds of images within the Docker hub.
Now a specific image as we are discussing it, can listen the service or the application inside that
container can listen on any ephemeral port.
So how will a person who is running that container know on which port that application is listening
to?
So anyone who builds their own image, they inform with the help of expose instruction that a specific
service or the application within the container image is going to listen on a specific port.
So let's better understand this with a use case diagram here where you have a service container on the
right hand side and when you perform a curl on the domain followed by the port, then this service container
basically returns a data block.
Now, you don't really know on which port this service container is listening to, so it might be listening
to 5000.
It might be listening to 5001, 5002, etcetera.
So unless and until, you know on which port this service container is listening to, it becomes difficult
to publish that port.
So that is where the expose instruction comes into the picture.
So when you look into the Docker file, you have apt get install service.
So this is a service which listens on a specific port and then you have a expose instruction with 9324.
So now what will happen is the image which gets built from this Docker file, from the image, a person

publish the port accordingly.

--- Dockerfile - HEALTHCHECK Instruction ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the health check Dockerfile instruction.
Now, the health check instruction basically allows us to tell the Docker platform on how it can test
whether our application is working as expected or not.
Now, typically whenever a Docker starts a container, it monitors the process that the container runs.
So we already know that because that process would run as a PID one.
Now if that process ends or it terminates, then the container also exits.
However, this type of check is a very basic check.
Sometimes it might happen that the process is running.
However, there are a lot of errors and it is not really responding properly.
So there is a need to have a proper health check which can verify whether the application is working
as expected or not.
So in order to do that, we make use of the health check instruction.
So let's quickly jump into the practical and understand on how exactly it might work.
So I'm in my CLI and let's quickly do one thing.

So basically it can monitor whether the container is up or not.

--- HEALTHCHECK Instruction Options ---

Hayden.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about the health check options.
Now, whenever you're making use of a health check instruction, there are multiple options that you
can combine it with to achieve a desired result.
So there are four important options that we need to be aware of at a high level.
Overview.
One is the interval, second is the timeout, third is the start period and fourth is the retry.
Now do note that if you do not specify all of these options while adding a health check instruction,
then the associated default values will automatically be used.
So for example, if you do not specify interval within the health check, then by default the interval
would be 30s and so on.
Now whenever you are making use of health check, do note that the commands exit status indicates the
health status of the container.
So for example, if you have the exit status of zero, that means success.
And the command did not throw any error if the possible value here is one.
So the exit status is one, then the container is not working correctly.
If it is two, which is basically for reserved and it has a description which states that do not use
the exit code.
So let's take a simple example here.
So you have a health check instruction where you have a curl on the localhost.
So in case if you have some kind of application running or if you have a web server running, then this
command will give you a result.
If not, then this command will fail.
So what this health check does, you have an interval of five minutes and the timeout of three seconds,
so it will check every five minutes whether the curl on localhost is working or not and the timeout
is three seconds.
So the results should be appearing within this specific timeout.
Now when you discuss about the health check, not only you can specify it as part of the overall dockerfile.
So if you see these are the various options that you can use as part of the Dockerfile, but you can
also make it available as part of the Docker run command.
So if you are doing a Docker run and if you want to specify the health check, that can also be done.

--- Layers of Docker Image ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing about the layers of Docker image.
Now a Docker image is built up from a series of layers.
Now each layer represents an instruction in the images Docker file.
Now this is very important to understand.
So typically, if you look into the diagram, this diagram has multiple layers over here.
And in the second point we discussed that each layer.
So there are multiple layers that you see over here.
Now each of this layer represents an instruction in the images Docker file so this can be understood
with this.
So this is a Docker file.
And when the Docker file is created and when you build an image from this Docker file, this image consists
of multiple layers and each of these layers is corresponding to the instruction which is present here.
So when you see the first instruction here is from Ubuntu 15.4.
So then the first layer over here, the most bottom one is basically created as an output of this specific
instruction.
Then you have the copy.
So then you have the second layer over here, then you have the run, then you have the third layer
over here and similar.
Now do you remember that there can be layer of type zero bytes, so that is perfectly fine.
Now the major difference between a container and an image is the top writeable layer.
Now all the rights that you basically make to the container that adds or modifies existing data are
stored in the writeable layer.
So this is something that we already saw.
So the image that is being built from the Docker file, this image is read only.
You cannot really modify this image.
On top of this image there is this read write layer and any modification that you make if you add a
file, if you remove this file, that modification is been written to this read and write layer.
So this can be understood with this diagram as well, where there are multiple Docker containers, each
and every Docker container can share a same image.
So you have a Ubuntu image over here and there are four containers which are using this Ubuntu image.
Now this is perfectly fine because this ubuntu image over here it is read only.
You cannot really modify that and each container will have its own read write layer and any changes
that they make.
It gets stored to this specific read and write layer.
Now if you look into this type of architecture, it is really great because even if you have ten containers,
all of those ten containers can share the base image.
They don't really have to create a completely new base image.
So this not only helps us save space, but also leads to better performance.
Now, if you want to see the layers associated with a specific image, you will be able to do that with
the help of Docker History Command.
So let's quickly look into how exactly it might look like.
So I'm in my CLI, so let's do a Docker images and there are multiple images which are present now if
you want to see the layer associated with the nginx image so you can run the command Docker history
and you can say Nginx and now you can see that these are the amount of layers which are associated with
the Nginx image.
And if you know the Docker file, you will be able to understand what each of these mean.
So you have an ad, you have a CMD, you have a label, you have a Env and various others.
All right.
So the amount of layer that each image has will be different on the image to image basis.
So if you typically do the Docker history on Ubuntu, you'll see Ubuntu has around five layers over
here, right?
And if you typically do a Docker history of MySQL, you'll see MySQL has a lot of layers.
So again it really depends on which image you use and the layers would be deferred accordingly.
So let me show you some interesting examples which will allow you to have a better understanding of
the image and layers.
So this is a simple Docker file.
If you see this Docker file, there are five instructions and this five instruction will correspond
to five layers.
All right, so you have.
From Ubuntu which might form the base layer which is layer one.
Then you have the run which would be layer two.
Then you have one more run layer three.
Then you have layer four and layer five.
So let's focus on the first three instructions of the Docker file.
Now the first three instruction is from Ubuntu.
All right.
So from ubuntu will basically pull the ubuntu image.
Now this image will have its own size.
Let's assume that the size here is 90 MB.

--- Pruning Docker Images ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing about Docker image Prune.
Now the Docker image Prune command allows us to clean up unused images which might be present.
Now, by default, whenever you run this specific command, the docker will only clean up the dangling
images.
So while you're working in Docker environment, you will come across the term of dangling images.
So it is important for you to remember on what exactly dangling image is.
Now dangling images are the one which does not have tag.
So basically images which do not have tag and the images which are not referenced by any container.
So this is the end condition.
So any image which satisfies this first requirement and it satisfies the second requirement is called
as the dangling images.
So let's quickly jump to the CLI and look into how exactly it might look like.
So I'm in my CLI.
Let's quickly do a Docker images and you will see that there are certain images which are present over
here.
Now there are certain images which do not have a tag which is associated with them.
Now dangling images are the ones which does not have a tag and the image which is not being referenced
by any container.
So when you do a Docker image prune, then all those dangling images will be removed.
So let's try it out.
So it is giving a warning that this will remove all the dangling images.
I'll press y and you see it has removed one image.
Now if I quickly do a docker images now you should see that the first image which did not have a tag
and it was also not referenced by any container was removed.
Now there is also a second command, so when you do a docker image prune hyphen A What this will do
is this will remove all the images which are not being referenced by any container.
Now this is very important because many times you will see that people just go ahead and pull the images,
but they never start any container from those images.
And slowly, slowly, the disk size would unnecessarily increase.
So when you do a Docker image prune hyphen A, it basically will remove all the images which does not
have a container which is associated with them.
So when I press y, you see it has deleted multiple images over here.
So now if I do a Docker images here, you will see that I only have one image which is available.
And the reason why this image was not deleted is because if I do a Docker ps hyphen a there is one container
which is associated with the ubuntu image.
All right.
Now I just wanted to show you one more thing.
So currently if I do an LS hyphen l I have a simple docker file over here.
All right, so this docker file is very simple from ubuntu.
You are basically running two commands.
Now if I quickly do a docker build, so this will go ahead and create an image.
So again, this specific image can be considered as dangling because not only it does not have a tag,
but there is no container which is running from this specific image.
Now if I start a container from the image, let's start a container, I'll call it as my demo and I'll
start it from the first image.
All right.
Now if I do a Docker image prune and if I do a yes and if I do a Docker images here, you will see that
the first image is not removed.
The reason why it is not removed because it is now no longer dangling, primarily because there is one
container which is associated with the image.

--- Flattening Docker Images ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.

Now, in a generic scenario, you should remember that most of the layers that an image has more the
size of that specific image.
Now, some of the images that I have seen, typically in production, it goes up to like from five GB
to ten GB.
So the image size increases considerably.
So it is very important for us to flatten an image to a single layer which can in turn help in reducing
the overall size of an image.
And this is the reason why it is important for us to understand on how we can modify the image to a
single layer.
So let's jump into the practical and look into how exactly this might look like.
I mean, let's quickly do a Docker images.

of the image.

me show you that.
Let's do a Docker pull on MySQL.
All right.
So now if I do a Docker image history on MySQL, you see the amount of layers that this image has is
quite huge.
And in fact, for some other images you will see much more higher layer than what you see for the MySQL
image here.
So for certain use cases it is very important that you merge all of these layers.
Otherwise your overall image size will be much more higher.
So if you do a Docker images, you will see that the MySQL image is of size 477 MB.
Anyways, let's take the example of Ubuntu for simplicity and we'll look into how we can modify the
layers associated with the Ubuntu image and also look into the associated size when all of these layers
are combined.

Let's do a docker run.

All right.
Now if you do a docker ps you should see that there is one container up and running and the name is
my ubuntu.

So we already saw that ubuntu image had multiple layers over here.

So let's look into how we can do that.

And from here let's create a pipe.
I'll say docker import and let's call it as my ubuntu hyphen latest.
All right.
So now if you look into the docker images, you will see that the my Ubuntu image that we have over

So you see there is a considerable size difference.

--- Overview of Docker Registries ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about Docker registry.
Now, understanding the concept of registry is very important and if we look into the analogy of registry,
it is something similar to a GitHub.
Now generally whenever you code for system administrator, it might be some kind of a configuration
management scripts like Ansible, etcetera.
So whenever you write those scripts it is recommended to put it in a centralized location where other
people can access as well as the code will remain safe there because if you store it in your local laptop
and due to some reason your hard disk fails or due to any other reason, if there is a issue with your
workstation, then your code will be lost.
And this is the reason why it is recommended that you store it in a centralized location.
Now, the same concept applies for the Docker registry.
If you are having a local images which are custom, it is not really recommended to store it in the
local server or in the local workstation.
We should store it in a centralized place where it can be accessible by others or it can remain safe
there.
So coming back to the definition of a registry, now a registry is a stateless, highly available server
side application that stores and lets you distribute the Docker images.
Now, generally, whenever you have your own custom docker image, it is stored in your local server
or in your local workstation.
However, that is not safe because if your server stops working due to some reason, then your custom
image will be lost and this is the reason why you should store it in a centralized place.
Now the place where the Docker images are stored is referred as the Docker Registry.
Now Docker Hub is one of the simplest example that we might have used because if you do a Docker pull,
then you basically download the image from a registry and that registry is stored in cloud, which is
referred as the Docker hub.
Now there are various type of registries which are available.
One is the Docker registry.
So this is the open source one, which has a very limited features, but it allows you to have the basic
functionality of storing your Docker images.
You also have a Docker trusted registry which is referred as DDR.
Now this is a enterprise variant for Docker Enterprise.
This has a lot of features.
When you compare it with the Docker registry, you also have various private registry services like
AWS, ECR, where you can store your Docker images.
And the last one is Docker Hub.
So Docker Hub is basically a cloud based offering where you can store your Docker images.
So let's quickly do one thing.
Let's jump into a certain demonstration as well as practical.
So the concept of registry is easily understood.
So I'm in my Docker hub and we are into the registry image.
So this Docker registry is a very basic registry application where we can go ahead and store our images.
Now this registry application offers a simple functionality.
If you want certain advanced functionality, then you can opt for the Docker Trusted registry, which
is which comes with the Docker enterprise.
Now if you go a bit down, the documentation already has the commands through which you can run the
registry.
So let's copy this up and I'll paste it within my CLI.
So what it is doing is it is basically running on port 5000, The name that it is giving it to the container
is registry and it is starting from the registry and with the tag of two.
So I'll go ahead and run it.
So the first thing it will go ahead and pull the registry image and then it will start the container
from the registry image.
So now if I do a Docker images, you should see that I have one Docker image which is available and
if I do a docker, PS you should see that there is one container which is up and running and it is listening
on port 5000 of the host and the traffic would be sent to the port, 5000 of the container.

application.

Oops.
All right, so our Ubuntu image is now downloaded.
Now if you quickly do a docker images, you should see that there is a ubuntu image.
Now one important part to remember is that whenever we do a docker pull it basically fetches the image
from the Docker hub.
Now we can also have images in various other locations as we were discussing.
So if you look into the slide, there can be various types of registry.
Docker Hub is one among them.
You can have various private registry also like Amazon ECR.

--- Build Cache ---

Haydon.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we'll be discussing about build cache or many times it is also referred as the
layer cache.
So by now we already know that Docker creates container images using layers, and each command that
is found within a Docker file creates a new layer as part of the image.
Now Docker also makes use of a layer cache to optimize the process of building a Docker image and makes
it much more faster.
So in this specific diagram, whenever we are trying to build an image called as Demo zero two, you
see what is happening is for many of the instructions, it is directly using the cache instead of building
it from scratch.
So that is what the caching does.
So let's do one thing.
Let's quickly jump into the practical so that this can be better understood.
So for today's demo we have two file that are available.
One is the Docker file and second is the Requirements.txt.
Now when you quickly open up the Docker file, this Docker file is loading this specific image of Python.
Then it is copying the specific file, which is the requirements.txt inside the container.
And then you have the run instruction which basically goes ahead and installs all the packages that
are defined under the Requirements.txt file.
And then you have the Entrypoint instruction.
Now if you also do a Nano on Requirements.txt, these are some of the packages that is being installed.
So let's go ahead and do a Docker, build the name, let's call it as without cache and let's start
it.
Great.
So our image is built.
So you see that you have the first layer here.
Then you have the copy layer.
Then you have the third layer, which is basically installing the packages from Requirements.txt and
then you have the fourth layer of Entrypoint.
So all the layers are built from scratch.
Now next time when you try to build a similar image, let's call it as with cash.
You see it got built within just a second or two.
It was extremely fast.
So here you see that a copy instruction is making use of a cache.
Then again, the run instruction of installing the Requirements.txt is also making use of the cache.
And also the step four of Entrypoint is also making use of the cache.
So basically if a specific instruction based on which the cache is already available with Docker, the
Docker, instead of building that instruction details from scratch, it can directly make use of the
cache.
And this way the overall building of image is much more faster.
Great.
So I hope now you understood the third point, which states that Docker makes use of the layer cache
to optimize the process of building the Docker images and also make it much more faster.
So before we conclude, let's look into one of the important pointers related to the build cache.
So the important pointer states that if the cache can't be used for a particular layer, all the subsequent
layers won't be loaded from the cache.
Now this can be better understood with this two diagram where you have an old docker file and you have
a new Docker file and there are three layers of each one of them.
So on the old Docker file, you have layer A, B, C, on the new Docker file, you have A, you have
C and for the B layer, you have made certain changes over here.
And on the right hand side you have a logic which states that if the docker will use a cache or not.
So for the layer one, since the layer is same, Docker will make use of the cache.
So a is equal to a so the cache will be used here for the second layer.
Since the instructions are different, you cannot make use of the cache.
So in terms of new docker file, this specific layer will be built from scratch.
And now since the layer two is built from scratch, all the other layers that are beneath the layer
two will also be built from scratch.
That means they will not use the cache.
Great.
So that's the high level overview about the build cache.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Understanding Bridge Networks ---

Hey, Ryan, and welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the bridge network driver in Dockers.
Now a bridge network basically makes use of software bridge, which in turn allows the containers which
are connected to the same bridge network to communicate.
It also provides isolation from the containers which are not connected to the bridge network.
So we can understand bridge network with this simple diagram here.
So let's say that there are three containers which are available now.
These containers will have the IP address depending upon the subnet which is associated with the bridge
network.
So you have the Docker zero Bridge, which is created over here.
And this acts as a gateway.
Now a container which wants to communicate with the Internet typically has to go through the Docker
Zero Bridge.
From there you have the IP tables and from there you have the zero interface.
So now here we are considering that this specific zero interface has the connection towards the Internet.
So you have the container container in turn has their own virtual interface over here.
Now they send the traffic to the Docker Zero Bridge.
If it is destined for the Internet, you have the zero interface which acts as the outbound over here.
Now, in this type of a bridge network, the containers which are created in the bridge network can
also communicate with each other.

--- Implementing User-Defined Bridge Networks ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the user defined bridge network.
Now, along with the user defined bridge network, we'll also discuss into some of the aspects related
to the bridge network overall.
Now, in the earlier video of bridge networks, we were looking that there was a Docker zero bridge
interface which was created.
So this acts as a virtual switch.
Now any container that you launch within the bridge network, that container has its associated interface.
Now this interface inside the container is called as the zero.
And from the outside it's something like v x x x.
All right.
Now each of this specific interface has its own set of IP address.
So let me quickly show you this specific aspect on how this might look like.
Now I'm in my CLI, so if I quickly do a show now, do remember that this command might not really work
in Windows.
So this is a specific binary that comes from a package.
So from a bridge show you will see that there is a bridge which is created.
The name is Docker zero and this is the bridge ID.
Now it is also showing you the list of interfaces which are part of this specific bridge.
So currently there are three interfaces which are part of this bridge.
So this is the reason why there is a intercommunication between the containers which are associated
with these interfaces.
So that communication is possible.
Now if you quickly do a Docker network inspect on bridge, you should be able to see that there would
be three containers which would be part.
So you see, you have the first container here, you have the second container here and you have the
third container here as well.
Now in case if you want to try and run this command in Linux, this is part of the package, let's do
a Yum!
What provides bridge.
So this would typically work on CentOS, Linux or Amazon Linux or RedHat based systems.
So this is part of the bridge utils.
So you can download this with the help of something like Yum install bridge utils.
So in my case, since it is already installed, it will say that this package is already installed in
case of ubuntu I believe it would be apt.
Get install bridge utils.
You can try it out now.
Whenever you install a docker it basically has its own default bridge which is created.
However, you can also create your own user defined bridge.
Now whenever you create your own user defined bridge, it comes with its own benefits.
Now some of these benefits includes the pointers that are part of the slide.

--- Understanding Host Network ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing about the host networks.
Now, the importance of host networks is a lot.
Specifically, if you are trying to deploy various kind of security services like IDs or various others.
So let's go ahead and discuss more about Host Networks posters.
We'll have a quick demo.
Now, the host driver basically removes the network isolation between the Docker host and the Docker
container so that the container can use the host networking directly so this can be understood with
the example.
So let's say that you are running a container which binds to port 80.
So basically an application in the container which binds to port 80 and you are using the host based
networking driver, then automatically that application which is running inside the container will automatically
bind to the port 80 of the host IP address.
Now when you compare this with bridge based networking, what we had to do is we had to publish the
port with Hyphen P in host based network that is not required.
Now there is second big advantage of host based network is that if you look into the bridge network
here now, every container in the bridge network has its own interface and the interface has its own
IP address.
So that means that if from the container, if you want to monitor the traffic of the eth0 interface,
that is not possible.
All right.
So let's say that you want to deploy a services like intrusion detection system.
You cannot install that in a container with a bridge network because if you do that, then the IDs will
monitor the traffic of the container.
It will not monitor that of the host.
However, if you want to monitor in such a way that the Containers application can monitor the traffic
of the eth0 of the host, you need to make use of the host network.
So let's quickly jump to the demo and look into how exactly it might look like.
So let's quickly do a Docker network.
LS And here this is the host network that we were discussing.
So let's quickly do a Docker container run.
I'll say it as my host network, I'll specify it Host and I'll run it from the Ubuntu image.
All right, so now if you do a docker ps we should see that there is one container which is up and running.
So this container is based on the host network.
So let's quickly log in to the container.
I'll say Docker container exec and we'll connect to the bash.
Great.
So from here, let's quickly download certain packages.
Great.
So our packages are being downloaded.
And before we go ahead and start our demo, let's also create a container call as my bridge will not
define any network over here.
So it will automatically start from the bridge network.
So if you do a Docker, PS We have one container based on bridge network and we have the second container
based on host network.
Now let's quickly log in to the My Bridge network.
Great.
So.
Again, I'll also install the base packages within this specific container.
Great.
Now, if I do a ifconfig here, you would see that this specific bridge has its own interface, which
is eth0 and it has the IP address of 17270.2.
Now if I do a netstat hyphen p over here, there are no ports or no services which are running on any
of the port.
So let's quickly do one thing.
I'll go ahead and I'll install Nginx in this specific container.
All right.
And once the Nginx is installed, we'll go ahead and start the Nginx.
Great.
So now if we do a netstat hyphen p, you will see that you have the nginx process which is connected
on port 80.
So this port 80 is inside the container.
Now if you come out of the container and if you do a netstat hyphen P, you will see that there is no
port 80 which is opened on the host level.
So that Nginx package and the port which it got opened, it was inside the container.
Now typically in the bridge network whenever we want, that outside world should be able to connect
to Port 80.
If you remember, we had to specify something like this where Hyphen P was published.
So you open up port 80 on the host and you forward the traffic to port 80 of the container.
So this is specific to bridge.
However, for host based networking, this is not required because it directly associates itself with
the host network.
So now let's connect to the container with the host based networking.
I'll say my host and I'll put it as bash.
Now, if I do a netstat hyphen P, you should see that it is showing you all the ports.
So these are the ports which are actually listening on the host server.
All right.
So this is actually the host server port.
Along with that, if you do a ifconfig, you will see that it is actually showing you all the interfaces
associated with the host.
So you see you have the zero interface, you have the Docker zero interface.
ET cetera.
And this is very important because let's say you have an intrusion detection system, so it might want
to monitor the traffic associated with the eth0 interface of the host.
And if you want to put the application inside the container, then you will have to put it in the host
based networking.
Because in the host based networking you will see that all the interface faces of the underlying host
are being exposed inside the container and this becomes very useful.
Now along with that, let's go ahead and install Nginx over here.
Great.
So now let's go ahead and start the Nginx service.
Alright, so now if you do a netstat hyphen p you will see that Nginx is listening on port 80.
So this port 80 is off the host.
Let's try it out from outside of the container.
So now from my host, if I do a netstat hyphen p, you will see that I have port 80 which is exposed.
So this port 80 is basically from the Docker container.
So for the host based networking, you don't really have to do something like hyphen, hyphen, publish
and publish the port.
This is not required for host based networking and although this is great, but only one disadvantage
is that you cannot really run two applications which are running on port 80.
Let's say that I have two applications in two different container, so both of the applications cannot
start on the same port because the port is associated with the host and you can only have one application
which can associate itself with a single port.
So this is the high level overview about the host based networks.

--- Publish All Argument for Exposed Ports ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about publishing exposed ports of containers.
Now, we have already discussed about how you can expose a port inside the container with the host.
So the command here is the hyphen P option.
So this is something that we have already discussed where with hyphen P we have the port 80.
So this is the port of the host that we want to expose to.
And the next one is the container port where the service would be running.
Now the small hyphen P is also referred as publish list, as it publishes only the list of ports that
are being specified with the command.
Now there is also a second approach to publish all the exposed port of the container.
Now this second approach is hyphen Capital P.
Now this is also referred as publish all.
And what happens in this approach is that all the exposed ports within the container are automatically
published to random ports of the host.
So let's quickly look into what exactly this means.
So I'm in my CLI and let's do one thing.
Let's quickly create a new container.
So I'll call it as web server and we'll launch it from the nginx image.
All right, so now if you do a Docker PS, you will see that you have a web server container up and
running.
Now this container is listening on port 80, so this port 80 is inside the container.
We have not really exposed it.
So if you want to verify if I can do a curl on 127001 on port 80, you see the connection is refused
because this port 80 is inside this specific container.
Now, in order to solve this, what we generally use to do, we use to specify the hyphen.
P And we used to say, let's say 80, 80, colon 80, and let's change the name to Web server zero one.
Now in this case, let's do a Docker.
PS You will see that the port 8080 of the host is mapped to the port 80 of the container.
And if you do a curl command again on port 8080, you will see that you are able to see the default
nginx page.
So this seems to be working and this is something that we already know.
So now let's try one thing.
Let's do a hyphen.
P All right, we'll do a hyphen.
P And let's change the name to Web server zero two.
So now what happens here is that you see automatically a random port got assigned.
So this is the random port which got opened and it automatically assigned itself to forward to the port
80 inside the container.
So if you want to verify here, I say curl 1270.0.1 on 32768 and here you are able to see the default
nginx page.
So generally whenever you make use of hyphen P option, what happens is that any port which is exposed
in a specific image, the hyphen P option will correspondingly create a random port in the host and
it will map it to the exposed port of the container.
So in this case you don't really have to explicitly specify the port mapping.
It automatically does it for you.
So that's the high level overview about the capital hyphen P option.
