--- Overview of Container Orchestration - New ---

Hi everyone, and welcome back.
Now in today's video at an overview, we are going to discuss about the basics of container orchestration.
Now again this video is going to be very important.
Specifically before you learn any orchestration tools like Kubernetes, swarm, ECS, etc. this video
will act as a base that will make things easier for learning any orchestration tool.
So with this, let's get started.
Now before we discuss about container orchestration, it is important for us to take a step back to
see what users and organization used to do before these tools became much more mature, to be ready
to serve production level traffic.
Now, during the initial times, users relied on manual container management tool to handle various
tasks like launching containers, managing containers, and so on.
For example, let's say that you have a server and you have a user, and user wants to launch some containers
for his application or his website.
So what he will do first, he will manually login to the server, and then he will run certain commands
like docker run to create the appropriate containers required for the application to run.
Now this is good from a smaller scale environment.
Generally, during earlier times, organizations were much more mature where they would have some kind
of an automation script, maybe through Python, shell scripts, etc. that used to take care of common
tasks like deployment, scaling, networking, monitoring, etc..
Now, irrespective of both the approaches that we are discussing here, when the environment becomes
larger, specifically in production environment, both the approaches are not really sufficient to handle
things at scale.
So let's take few common examples that almost all of the organizations use to face.
And then we'll see on how container orchestration comes into picture.
So first challenge here is related to manual scaling.
So let's say that you have a server one where there are two containers that are running now suddenly
as part of your promotion or maybe some kind of advertisement, you are receiving very high traffic
on your website.
And the issue is that there is only one container running for app, one container running for nginx.
So in the situation where there is an unexpected high amount of traffic that is coming to your website,
you want some form of scaling to happen.
Now you also have a second server that is available.
However, this scaling will not really happen automatically.
So now what will happen is the user or the developer.
He will have to manually create the container on the second server as well.
And then whatever traffic that comes to your website, it needs to be load balanced between the containers
in the server one and server number two.
So all of this is a manual process and it takes a lot of time.
Now let's explore one more very common challenge that organizations used to face specifically related
to fault tolerance.
So let's say that you have some containers that are running in the server one.
Now if a container has failed, it can be due to a wide variety of reasons.
It can be a crash, it can be due to resource exhaustion, etc. the container generally would not automatically
restart without manual intervention in most of the cases.
Now at this stage we are just taking an example where app container has failed.
However, what happens if the entire server one has failed?
How would you automate things in such cases?
So when you have a manual based approach, if the server one fails what you might do in the server two
again you will launch the app container.
You will launch the nginx container, redirect the traffic to the server two and so on.
So all of this is a manual process and it makes things difficult for production level environments.
So these are just two common challenges that a wide variety of other challenges also that organizations
were facing.
And in order to overcome all of these, you have a new technology of container orchestration that came
into picture.
Now at a very high level overview of container orchestration automates the deployment, management,
scaling and networking of the containers.
So now what will happen is you have a container orchestration tool which is responsible for all of this
task.
Now user let's say a user wants to launch certain containers in a fleet of servers.
So he will not manually log in to each of the server, manually run the docker run command to create
a container, and so on.
So that is the older approach.
So now in the newer approach where container orchestration is used, user will communicate directly
to the container orchestration tool.
And container orchestration tool will in turn take care of the responsibility of launching the containers
in the appropriate server, making sure that this containers are always running.
If the containers are not running, let's say if a server one has crashed, then container orchestration
tool has that logic to migrate that container or relaunch the container in another server to ensure
that containers are always running.
So most of the common tasks related to deployment scaling, networking, everything.
Container orchestration tool takes care of it.
So let's look into how container Orchestration tool allows us to solve some of the important challenges
that organizations used to face.
So let's say that you have in total of three servers that are running as part of the fleet.
As of now, the traffic is less.
So you have 25% of traffic, and the container orchestration tool has launched one container in server
number one.
Note that many of the mature container orchestration tool can automatically scale the containers up
or down, based on the defined policies and the real time traffic.
This is the important part to understand.
So let's say as of now the traffic is less.
You only have one container, but at a later stage you have increased the amount of traffic.
So you have 75% of traffic that is coming.
So this single container might not be able to handle all of the requests.
So now what container orchestration will do.
It will go ahead and launch multiple set of containers in multiple servers.
and also the traffic will be routed equally among all of the servers.
And this is a great benefit for organizations.
Along with that, we were discussing about the challenge related to fault tolerance.
Now when container orchestration is introduced, it can automatically look into the health check of
the containers that are running.
And let's say that the server one has crashed due to some reason.
Then container orchestration will go ahead and it has the appropriate logic to create one more set of
containers in another server and redirect all of the traffic to server number two so that the website
is always running.
So all of this important logic, you don't really have to create the manual scripts related to it.
The container orchestration tool automatically takes care of it.
And this is of great benefit and it saves huge amount of time.
Great.
So now that we understand the basic of it at an overview, let's have a quick demo to understand on
how things really work.
Now for today's demo video I have three servers that are created.
One is where you have a container orchestration tool that is available, and you have two more servers
which are going to be the worker node where the actual containers can run.
So let's go ahead and quickly connect to this server post, which will be discussing things in more
detail.
Great.
So currently I am connected to all of the three servers.
Now as we are discussing, when you have a container orchestration tool that comes into picture, user
will not really log in to each of the server.
Manually launch the container, route the traffic accordingly.
What user will do?
He will instruct the container orchestration tool that say I need five containers of nginx and container
orchestration tool has the appropriate logic to launch the five containers and ensure that they are
always running.
So let's take an example where I instruct that in total I need five containers of nginx that should
be running now.
Again, we are not interested in the command at this stage.
We are more interested to see on how things work at a high level.
Overview.
So when I press enter the container Orchestration tool, whatever is available has the appropriate logic
to take whatever instruction that you have provided, and it will act on it to ensure that in total,
five containers will be created and will always be running.
Now, as we are discussing that there are multiple worker servers that are running.
In fact, the containers can also run on the main manager node in case if you have configured it.
So let's look into it.
So in the main node over here, if you see when I run Docker PS there are two containers that are running
here.
Then in worker node one if I do a docker PS, you see I have one more container running here and in
worker node two if I run docker PS you have two more containers of nginx that is running over here.
Now as we were discussing That container orchestration tool also ensures that whatever instruction that
you have provided, it gets fulfilled all of the time.
For example, we have told the container orchestration that I need a total of five containers of nginx
that would be running now, let's say due to some reason the worker zero two server has crashed.
All right, so let me do one thing.
I'll manually stop the server.
All right.
I'll shut down this specific server and we'll see on how the container orchestration tool will ensure
that these two containers that were running in the worker zero two, which is no longer present, these
two containers are created in the remaining servers, whatever are running.
So now let's run the Docker PS command once again.
Within here you have two containers that are running now in the worker zero one.
If I run the docker PS.
You see, in total there are three containers that are running and this two containers are the new containers
that got created in the worker zero one.
Why?
Because worker zero two is no longer responding.
All right.
So this is one very simple example related to what container orchestration tool allows us to do.
Now one thing to note is that container orchestration tool is a lot more.
Now just for this video we had taken two examples related to manual scaling and fault tolerance.
However, there are a lot of important features that the container Orchestration tool provides from
the perspective of production environment, which is very helpful.
For example, load balancing and traffic routing monitoring of container health.
Along with that, one point to note is that there are many container orchestration tools that are available
in the industry, and based on the requirement, you can use one among them.
For example, Docker Swarm has been one of the very popular container orchestration tool that was initially
used.
Nowadays in the industry, if you speak, Kubernetes is extremely popular and in fact most of the organization
seems to be using Kubernetes because of the wide range of extensive features that it provides.

--- Initializing Docker Swarm ---

Hey, Ryan, and welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the initializing the Docker swarm.
Now, there are certain key terminologies that you need to understand for Docker Swarm.
One of them is a node.
So a node is basically an instance of Docker Engine, which is going to be participating in the swarm.
So in our case there are going to be three nodes and all of them are going to be part of the overall
swarm cluster.
Now to deploy your application to swarm, you must submit a service definition to a manager node.
So among all of these, whenever we initialize a swarm, one will act as a manager node and we basically
dictate to that manager node that I need.
Let's say for example, I need two containers of Nginx which should be up and running at all the time.
So we submit that specific service definition to a manager node.
So manager Node can be one among them that we can select.
And once we tell the manager node that we need two Nginx containers to be running up all the time,
then the manager node basically will dispatch the unit of work to the worker node.
So then once the manager knows that it needs to deploy two containers of Nginx, it will go ahead and
dispatch that to the worker node.
So you have the terminology of manager node and you have the terminology of worker nodes.
Now in order to initialize the Docker swarm, there are two commands that you need to run.
One would be in the manager node and second would be in the worker nodes.
So what we'll do is we'll select the Node zero one as the manager node and node zero to Node zero three
will act as the worker nodes.

that you also have connectivity between all of these three virtual machines.

--- Services, Tasks and Containers ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will look into some of the key terminologies related to the Docker swarm, which
are services, tasks and containers.
Now, a service is basically the definition of the task to execute on the manager or the worker nodes.
So let's say this is a simple command to create a service.
So it is Docker service create.
We give the name of the service as web server.
We specify the number of replicas, which is one and from which image our application container should
launch.
And this is enginex.
So once you run this command, what swarm will do is swarm will basically create one nginx container
in one of the nodes which are part of the swarm.
Now, once this container is created and due to some reason this container has stopped working, the
swarm will automatically restart that container.
If not, then it can also let's say that the entire node has failed and it is not responding.
So in this case it will go ahead and create the container in a different working node so that your service
is always operational.
So if you say replica of one, it basically means that at an instant of time one container would always
be up and running.
Now when you go ahead and start working with Swarm, there are certain terminologies like services,
task and containers that you should understand.
So service, we already discussed an example.
So let's say in this example we have three Nginx replicas.
So this is a service.
So in service we basically specify if you see Docker service create, we specify the amount of replica
that we want within our cluster.
So in this case, if we say that there should be three nginx replicas, then what orchestrator will
do?
It will create three tasks and each task is associated with a container.
So if you see there is one task in Node one, you have the second task in Node two and you have third
task in Node three and each task has the container.
So container is like an initiation of a specific task.

--- Draining Swarm Node ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will look into how we can train a node from a swarm cluster.
So let's take an example.
So currently you see that there are three nodes with the availability of active over here.
Now let's say that as part of the monthly patching lifecycle, you want to restart this specific server
so you do something like Yum y update to install the latest patches and then you want to restart the
Swarm zero three server or if you want to do some kind of a maintenance and you want to bring it down
for few hours, then what is the approach?
So in that case it is recommended that you drain the specific node so that whatever containers that
swarm might be running in this node would be switched to a different available node over here.
So let's go ahead and look into how exactly it might work.
So let's quickly do one thing.
Let's quickly create a service, I'll call it as my web server, and the replicas would be, let's give
it five and I'll start it from the Nginx image.
Great.
So now if we quickly do a Docker service PS on my web server, you will see that in total there are
five tasks which are running across various nodes over here.
So among this there are two tasks which are running in the Swarm zero three node.
So let's say that you have two tasks which are running over here and you want to restart or you want
to put this Swarm zero three server for maintenance for few hours.
So in order for you to do that, it is not really recommended to directly go ahead and stop the Docker
daemon or restart.
You can migrate all the containers which are running to a active node which you know that it is going
to run for a longer period of time and that can be achieved with the help of draining.
So let's quickly do it.
So I'll say Docker node update, you have to specify the availability.
So within the availability you have active and you have drain.
So I'll say drain here and you can specify the node name which is Swarm zero three.
All right, So now what you have told the orchestrator that this specific node might be going for maintenance
and you can go ahead and drain it.
And now if you do a Docker service, PS, my web server, you should see that the containers which were
running in the Swarm zero three, they are now shut down and a new containers have been brought up in
an alternative node.
So anytime if you increase the availability of containers.
So in this case we had increased the availability of containers to five.
So let's say we move it to ten.
So even if you move it to ten, Docker orchestrator will not create the new task within a node which
is marked as drain, it will only move it to the nodes which are part of the availability active.
So if you quickly do a Docker node ls over here, you should see that the Swarm zero three, the availability
here is drained.
So not only Docker orchestrator has removed the tasks which are running in the Swarm zero three, but
also anytime if you create new services, it will not be deployed in the swarm.
Zero three Now again, you can bring the mode of the swarm zero three from draining to active also.
So let's go a bit up.
You can change the availability from drain to active.
All right, so let's run the Docker node LS command yet again.
And now you see the availability is active.
So this is it about draining the node within the swarm cluster?
I hope you got the use case where the draining is required.
So with this we'll conclude this video.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Locking Swarm Cluster ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about logging the swarm cluster.
Now, a swarm cluster can contain a lot of sensitive information.
So some of the sensitive information includes the TLS key, which is used to encrypt the communication
among the swarm nodes.
It also contains the key used to encrypt and decrypt the raft logs on the disk.
Now, the keys that we are discussing over here, if these keys are unencrypted and if your swarm is
compromised due to some reason, then the attacker will be able to catch hold of the keys in an unencrypted
form and they can get a lot of sensitive information.
And this is the reason why Docker suggests you to lock your swarm so that this situation can be ignored.
So let's quickly look into how exactly this might look like.
So I'm in my swarm zero one server, so if I quickly do a Docker node.
LS There are three nodes which are part of the swarm.
So now if I quickly go to var lib Docker and within it there are a lot of directories.
Let's go to the swarm directory and within here let's go to the certificates.
Great.
So now you see that you have the swarm node certificate, you also have the swarm node key and you also
have the swarm root CA cert.
So if you quickly do a cat on Swarm Node key, you will see that this is the key, which is the private
key and it is available in plain text.
So if the attacker gets hold of this key as well as the certificate, he will be able to decrypt the
communication as well as various raft encrypted log files.
So in order for you to avoid such situation, the Docker suggests you to go for the lock feature of
swarm cluster.
So if you quickly do a Docker swarm update hyphen hyphen help, you will see that here you have the
feature of auto lock.
And here there are two options.
One is true and second is false.
So if you have the auto lock as true, then next time when you restart your swarm, the swarm will basically
ask you for the password whenever you want to perform some kind of a operation.
So let's look into how that can be achieved.
So I'll say Docker swarm update you have auto lock is equal to true.
So now it basically says that to unlock a swarm manager after it restarts, you need to run the Docker
swarm unlock and you have to pass this specific key.
So go ahead and save this key because this key is going to be important.
So I'll save this key within my text document.
So now that the auto lock feature has now been enabled, let's go ahead and restart our Docker container.
So I'll say systemctl, restart Docker.
Great.
So once you have done that, let's quickly verify the status.
I'll say systemctl status.
Docker, The Docker is up and running now.
If I do a Docker node.
LS You see it basically gives you an error saying that error response from the daemon swarm is encrypted
and it needs to be unlocked before it can be used.
And it is also saying that you need to make use of the Docker swarm unlock command to be able to unlock
it.
So let's go ahead and run this Docker swarm, unlock command and just specify the key.
Great.
So once you have done that, if you now do a Docker node ls, you will be able to see the nodes which
are part of the swarm cluster.
Now, along with that, once you have already unlocked your swarm cluster, if you say Docker swarm,
unlock hyphen key, you will be able to retrieve the key back again.
And last but not the least, you can also rotate the key with hyphen hyphen rotate command.
So in this case you see that the key is now rotated.
So in case if you want to rotate the key, you can go ahead and rotate it.
So once you've done that, you can copy this new key and paste it within your text file.
Now make sure that you put it in some kind of a password manager.
Don't put it in a text file because again, it would be in a plain text format.
So this is it about locking the swarm cluster.
I hope at a high level overview you understood what this meant.
And with this, we'll conclude this video and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Troubleshooting Swarm Service Deployments ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll discuss about troubleshooting the service deployments.
Now, a service may be configured in such a way that no node in the swarm can run the task.
Now, in such cases, the service remains in the state of pending.
Now, there are multiple reasons why service might go into the pending state.
So if you look into the below diagram over here, you see that there are three tasks which are available.
The desired state for them is running.
However, the current state is in the pending mode.
So that means that this specific service was not able to deploy the task.
The task went into the pending state.
Now, as we have discussed that, there are multiple reasons why the task within the service might go
into the pending state.
Let's look into some of them now.
The first common reason is that if all the nodes are drained and you create a service, then it will
go into the pending state.
Unless and until a node becomes available.
Now, the second reason is that you specify a amount of memory or a CPU which is required for a specific
service.
Now, if no node in the swarm has that specific amount of memory or CPU, the service remains in the
pending state unless and until a node is available which can run that specific task.
And the third common reason is that if you have imposed some kind of a placement constraints or some
kind of a constraints here, then also the service might go into the pending state.
So let's look into this in detail.
So I'm in my CLI and let's quickly do a Docker service.
LS here and you have a service called as demo Troubleshoot.
So let's quickly do a Docker service, PS Demo troubleshoot.
All right, so now here you see that the current state of three tasks are pending over here.
All of them are pending.
Now, in case if someone tells you that, hey, there are some tasks which are pending, can you figure
out what might be the reason?
So in such cases you can quickly do a Docker inspect and you can take one of the IDs over here and press
enter.
Now within here, within the status column, you will see an error.
So error says that no suitable node scheduling constraints are not satisfied on three nodes.
So that basically means that there was some kind of a constraint which was applied while creating a
service.
And due to that constraint, the containers were in the pending state.
So then you can ask the respective team to show you the Docker service Create command, which they had
used to create this specific service.
And then from there you will be able to figure out the reason for that.
So in my case, what I had done was in order to run or in order for us to have this demo, what I had
done was I had created a Docker service.
The name was Demo Troubleshoot.
However, there was a constraint here and the constraint was that the replica.
So three replicas of Nginx should only run on the node which has the label of region is equal to is
equal to one Mumbai.
Now any of the node which was part of the list here, none of the nodes had this specific key value
pair.
And this is the reason why all the tasks associated with the service went into the pending state.
So this is one quick way in which you will be able to identify on why the service went into the pending
state.
So that's about it for the troubleshooting of service deployments.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Control Service Placement ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the control service placement.
Now, if you remember during the video of the container orchestration overview, we had taken an example
of how Amazon ECS can place the containers based on various factors like the availability zones or even
instance types.
Now, similarly, Docker Swarm also has the capability to push the task or containers on the worker
nodes depending upon the specific constraints that we can specify.
So what are those constraints?
Are the things that we are going to discuss in today's video.
So basically the swarm services provides a few different ways in which we can control the scale and
placement of services on different nodes.
So these are some of the factors based on which we can control the way in which the containers or the
tasks are being pushed.
So the first is replicated and the global services.
Now this is something that we have already discussed.
So if you do a replicated, then you have to specify the replica count.
And for global, it basically will launch that task in all the nodes which are available as part of
Swarm.
Then you can also specify the resource constraints that a container should only be launched on a specific
node based on certain resources like CPU and memory.
You also have various placement constraints where you can say that the container should only be run
on the node, which has the label of PCI underscore compliance is equal to true.
And you also have something like placement preferences where you can spread the containers based on
label.
So let's do one thing.
Let's take one of the example over here and let's look into how exactly this works so that the concept
is easier for us to understand.
So for today's video, we'll take one example here based on labels.
So I'm in my CLI and if I can quickly do a Docker node.
LS You see that there are three nodes which are available now.

--- Overview of Overlay networks ---

Hey, Ron, and welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the overlay networks.
Now, the overlay network driver basically allows us to have a distributed network setup among multiple
Docker daemon host.
So in short overlay network allows containers connected to it to communicate securely.
So let's say that this is a swarm cluster and there are multiple nodes which are running and the containers
are in a different node.
So let's assume here that you have three containers in three different virtual machine and each virtual
machine has its own Docker daemon and they are part of the swarm cluster.
Now if a web server container of Node one wants to communicate with the web server container of Node
two, or if a web server container of node one wants to communicate with the web server container of
Node zero three, then this is not possible with the bridge or this is not possible with the host network.
So in order for the communication to happen, we need to make use of the overlay network.
So whenever you initiate a swarm, you will find that the overlay network driver has been initialized.
So let's quickly look into that.
So I'm in my CLI and let me quickly do a Docker network.
LS And here you see that there is a network which is created.
The driver here is overlay and the name here is Ingress, and the scope here is swarm.
So generally if you are going to work with Docker Enterprise, then this specific network comes by default.
And for default Docker installations, you don't really have a overlay network driver which comes.
So whenever you configure the swarm, you will see that a new overlay driver has been initiated.

do a Docker network, inspect my network.

--- Secure Overlay Networks ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about the security aspect of the overlay networks.
Now, during the introduction to Overlay Network, we already discussed that overlay networks basically
allows the containers which are distributed across multiple servers to be able to communicate among
each other.
Now, although that is good, but the problem here is the network.
So let's say you have Node zero one and Node zero two server and one of them has a container one and
you have a container two.
So overlay network allows the communication between the container one and the container two.
But the problem here is the security part.
So if in the middle so this is a network, the part from Node one to Node zero two is a network.
And if there is a malicious user who is sitting between them and if he can read the communication,
he might be able to get some sensitive info.
So this is the reason why the security aspect is important within the overlay networks.
So as we discussed that if the containers are communicating with each other, it is recommended to secure
the communication which is happening now.
This is especially important in overlay networks because the traffic will be sent over the network or
maybe even over the Internet.
Now to enable the encryption, when you create an overlay network, you have to pass the hyphen hyphen,
which is the option encrypted flag.
So this is how the command looks like.
So you have the Docker network create, you have hyphen, hyphen, opt encrypted, then you have the
driver overlay and then you have to give the overlay network name.
So this is something that you can define yourself.
So let's try this out.
So this is the command here.
So you have the Docker network create, you are passing the hyphen hyphen, opt encrypted flag.
Then you have the driver as overlay and then you are defining the overlay network name.
So let's go ahead and try this out.
And you see as the output, you got an ID, So now if you do a Docker network LS, you will see that
this is the overlay network which has been created now in exams.
It is important for you to remember on how you can supply a flag which allows the encryption of traffic
between the containers in an overlay network.
So do remember this specific flag which is hyphen hyphen, opt encrypted.
Now there are certain important pointers that you should remember.
The first is that when you enable the overlay encryption, Docker creates a IPsec tunnels between all
the nodes where the tasks are scheduled for services attached to the overlay network.
The next important part is that the tunnels also makes use of ease algorithm in GCM mode and manager
automatically rotates the key every 12 hours.
So as far as exam perspective is concerned, you don't really have to remember the GCM mode and all.
One primary thing that you have to remember is this option to create a encrypted overlay network.
Now one also important part that you should remember is that overlay network encryption is not supported
in Windows.
Now if a Windows node attempts to connect to an encrypted overlay network, no error is detected, but
the node will not be able to communicate.
So this is the high level overview about the secure overlay networks.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Split Brain & Importance of Quorum ---

Here on.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the split brain problem and the necessity of a write
quorum.
Now, let's go ahead and understand split brain problem with an example.
So let's say that you have a cluster where there are two Web servers which are currently up within the
cluster.
Now among this one among them is a master.
So the green Web server here, this is the master and the blue one is the slave.
Now you have a centralized disk over here and Master Node is responsible for storing and accessing some
of the files from the centralized disk.
So since the VM to the web server running under the VM two is the master one, its responsibility is
to connect to the centralized storage and perform certain operations here.
Now let's assume that due to some reason, the connectivity between the web server one and the web server
two has stopped, so the network is unreachable.
However, both the web servers are up and running.
However, they are not able to inter communicate between each other.
So now what will happen is the passive web server on the left hand side, this web server will assume
that the web server, which is in the VM two.
So this web server has certain issues.
So the web server in the VM one will assume that the other web server has went down and now it will
promote itself to master and then it will connect to the centralized storage here.
Now the problem that happened is both of them are master.
They are not able to communicate with each other and both of them are trying to connect and perform
certain operations on the centralized storage.
And if both of them are doing that, the chances of data corruptions are extremely high and this is
dangerous.
So this kind of a problem is referred as the split brain problem.
Now, in order for us to go away with the split brain, there is a concept of quorum.
So having a right quorum is extremely important.
So quorum is nothing but how many servers you have within a cluster.
So currently you see there are only two servers within the cluster and due to this you have a split
brain problem.
But if you have a quorum of three, so quorum of three means there are going to be three members which
are going to be part of the cluster.
So having a correct quorum will help you prevent the split brain problem.
So let's understand this with the example here.
Now in this type of architecture, there are three members within the cluster and as expected, the
web server running in the VM.
One is the master one and the other ones are the passive ones, or I would say the child ones.
Now here everything is working correctly.
All of them are able to intercommunicate correctly and the master one has taken the lead and it is writing
to the centralized storage.
Now in the quorum type of architecture, voting is extremely important.
So let's say that each of the server which is participating within the cluster has a vote.
So you have VM one.
So web server here has vote one, you have one more vote for the VM two and you have one more vote for
the VM three.
So each and every server which is participating in the Quorum has its own vote.
Now let's assume that the web server, which is currently the master in the VM one, has the connectivity
issue.
So this specific server is not able to communicate with the VM two as well as VM three.
Now, in such cases, what will happen is this web server will determine how many votes it has.
So currently, since this server is not able to communicate to the VM two, nor it is able to communicate
with the VM three so it only has vote one and hence it will lose.
However, you have the M2 and M3 connectivity working perfectly, so both of them has higher vote because
the M2 has vote one, VM three has vote two.
So in total both of them has in total two votes.
So VM two one vote, VM three has one vote.
So total two votes.
So then this will win the right hand side part will win.
And thus since the VM one has only one vote, it will remove its connectivity from the centralized storage
here.
And the vm3.
So there will be an algorithm which will choose a new master between these two.
So let's say that the VM three has been chosen as the master.
So now this master will go ahead and connect to the centralized storage.
So this is the reason why having the right quorum is extremely important.
Now, once the connectivity between VM one to the other, VMs has been established, again, we already
know because of two votes, the VM three had taken the role of master, so then the VM one would step
down and it would act as a passive node.
Now do remember that in this type of architecture, in this type of quorum, where there are three cluster
nodes, you can have a failure of up to only one because let's say because we already discussed that
if there was a failure of one, the other two has the vote of two.
And this is the reason why they were able to choose the master.
Now, in the quorum of three, if two server went down, then again it is going to be the same issue.
So you have to make sure that the quorum is always the right one.
So few important parts to remember for the quorum.
First one is that you should always maintain an odd number of the nodes within the cluster so this can
be understood with the diagram here.
So let's understand this table.
So here you have a cluster size of one.
Now since you have one, you have majority of zero and fault tolerance of zero.
So that means if this server goes down, then the cluster goes away.
Now you also have a cluster size of two where you need a majority of two and there cannot be a fault
tolerance because we we already took an example of a split brain in a typical scenario of two servers
here.
So if one server due to some reason there is a network connectivity issue between both of these servers,
then that can create an issue.
Now, for the example of cluster size of three, you need the majority of two and you can have one server
down at a moment of time.
This also we understood here that if there are three servers here and if one server is not able to connect
to the other two, then the other two can take the majority because they have the vote of two over here.
And this specific server has only one vote.
And this is the reason why it can disconnect itself to the centralized storage.
So for the quorum of three, for the cluster size of three, the majority of two servers are needed
all the time.
And this is what we can see over here.
The majority of two servers are needed at all the time and up to one server failure can be sustained
and same goes for four, five, six and seven.
So always remember you should have an odd number whenever you are creating a cluster.
So that's the high level overview about the split brain and also about the quorum.
So with this, we'll conclude this video.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- High Availability of Swarm Manager Nodes ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the high availability with respect to the swarm manager
nodes.
Now, the manager nodes are basically responsible for handling the cluster management task.
So as of now, what we had been doing was we had created one manager node and there were multiple worker
nodes.
Now the problem with this approach is that if this manager node goes down, then all of the cluster
management operation will not work.
So for example, let's say that you have one manager node, it is connected to two worker and this manager
node has stopped working.
And tomorrow you have to scale up your service because a traffic is going to increase a lot.
So in that case, since the manager node is down, you will not be able to perform the scale up service
or scale down service or any cluster management service with the swarm.
And this is the reason why it is very important that you have multiple manager nodes for high availability.
Now the manager nodes within the swarm has a lot of responsibility.
Now some of these include the maintaining of cluster state.
It includes scheduling of services, it includes serving swarm mode, Http API endpoints.
Now, with the help of raft implementation, the manager maintains a consistent internal state of the
entire swarm and all the services running on it.
So if you see over here, you have the internal distribution state store.
Now this is achieved with the help of raft implementation.
Now again, for the exam perspective, you will not be challenged anything with the help of how you
can implement Raft.
It's just that at a high level overview you should know that raft is basically used for maintaining
a consistent internal state.
Now coming back to high availability because here we were discussing that high availability is important.
Now the question is how you can achieve high availability.
So that is something that we'll be discussing at the later stage of the video.
However, do remember that swarm comes with its own fault tolerant feature.
So this is something that you can go ahead and configure.
Now, as we were discussing during the video of Split Brain and Quorum, Doctor recommends you to implement
an odd number of nodes according to your organization's high availability requirement.
Now, do remember here that this is a formula that you should remember that an end manager cluster tolerates
the loss of, at most n minus one by two managers.
So let's say that you have the total of three managers.
So three minus one is equal to two.
Two by two is one.
So if you have total of three managers, then it can tolerate a loss of up to one manager node.
So this is the formula that you can use.
So if I can quickly open a new slide.
So this is part of the quorum.
So if you will see if you have a cluster size of three, then you can have up to one node failure.
You cannot have something more than that.
And this is derived from the formula here, which is n minus one by two.
So let's do one thing.
Let me actually put it within the text document and let's calculate.
So for three cluster nodes you have n So n here is basically determines the number of nodes.
All right.
So for three cluster node n minus one, so is equal to three, minus one is two and then you divide
two by two.
So now you divide two by two is equal to one.
So you can have a maximum of one node failure for three cluster nodes.
So in case of even cluster nodes here, let's say we have four cluster nodes.
So now four minus one is equal to three, and now three divided by two is equal to 1.5.
So now the question is how will this work?
So now you have to round up to the lowest figure.
So 1.5, so you round it up to one, which is the lowest one.
So the four cluster node can support failure of one node.
So if you look into the diagram here, so four can support the failure of up to one node.
So this is the formula that is important for you to remember, both for the real world as well as for
the exams.
So now let's do one thing.
What I have is I have three servers which are.
Created.
The first server is called a swarm manager zero one second is a swarm manager zero two and third is
a swarm manager.
Zero three And in today's practical, we'll look into how we can have the high availability between
multiple manager nodes.
Now, I already have Docker installed in all of these servers, so let's do one thing.
Let's quickly do a Docker swarm in it.
So this will give a little warning message saying that you have to advertise the address because there
are multiple address.
One is public as well as private.
So let's advertise the public address here.
I'll use the public address.
Great.
So now it has given a message.
Basically, this message has the token for the worker nodes.
So this specific thing is something that we were using in the previous videos.
So when you run this command, any node in which you run this command will act as a worker node.
However, for our practical, we want the node to be the manager node.
And this is the reason why you should read the last line here, which states that to add a manager node
to this swarm, run the Docker swarm, join token manager command so you have to run this within the
current node itself.
So basically as of now, since you only have one node here and you have initialized this node as the
swarm, this will act as the manager.
So this acts as a leader.
So if you want other nodes also to be part of the swarm manager for high availability, you can run
the command which is mentioned over here.

Great.

--- Introduction to Kubernetes - New ---

Hi everyone, and welcome to the introductory video to Kubernetes.
Now, before we proceed further, one thing that I wanted to share is that personally, I have used
a wide range of container orchestration tools that are available in the industry.
It can be Docker Swarm, it can be Amazon, EKS and various others that are available.
But by far one of the best ones that is available in the industry is Kubernetes.
So we are going to discuss about Kubernetes in great detail step by step.
So with this base set let's go ahead and understand the basics of Kubernetes.
Now at a high level overview.
One point to note is that Kubernetes is an open source container orchestration engine developed by Google.
So although it was originally designed by Google, but now it is maintained by CNCF, which is the cloud
native computing Foundation.
Now with this base set, let's go ahead and look into the architecture of Kubernetes at an overview.
There are two important components of the Kubernetes cluster.
First, very important one is the control plane.
And the second one is the worker nodes.
Now control plane is the actual Kubernetes cluster.
So all of the main components that are part of Kubernetes, they are referred to as the control plane.
And the worker nodes that you see on the right.
These are the actual servers where the container applications run.
All right.
So let's also look into the workflow at a high level overview.
So discussing about the workflow the user communicates to the control plane and provides the necessary
instruction to run the containerized applications.
For example you have a user user will have some requirements.
For example user might want to run two containers of nginx.
Or user might want to run an application container, but not on the smaller server on the largest server
that is available here.
So all of these requirements, the user will submit it to the control plane, which is the main Kubernetes
master node.
Control plane will interpret the requirement, and it will go ahead and launch the appropriate container
in the nodes that are available as part of the overall cluster.
All right.
So this is a very basic high level workflow.
Now before we proceed further, let me also quickly show you on how exactly it looks like so that things
are much more clear.

So Kubernetes by itself has multiple components.
So when you are doing things manually like you have to configure each and every components, you have

So this is one of the approaches that you will take once you understand Kubernetes pretty well.

So one of the advantages of managed Kubernetes service.
So let's say you are learning Kubernetes the first time you don't really want to do things hardware,

So you use managed Kubernetes service, you learn Kubernetes, you learn various commands that you can
run against Kubernetes, various features.
And once you are comfortable with Kubernetes, then at the last you can go ahead and configure your

So generally whenever you're configuring Kubernetes, there are few important things that you need to

First is you have to install the kubectl.
So kubectl is basically the client side because during the Kubernetes video we were looking there that

every agent.

against the Kubernetes cluster.
So let's say that you have a Kubernetes cluster up and running and you want to go ahead and create resources
like Pods or any other resources in the Kubernetes cluster.
So what you need is you need a command line utility.
In this case, it is kubectl.

And based on your instruction, the resources would be created or deleted accordingly.
Now, one important part to remember here is that in order for kubectl to connect to the Kubernetes
master, it needs various information like the DNS or IP of your cluster, the authentication credentials
and so on.
And this is the reason why you also have one more resource called as Kubeconfig file.
And this kube config file in turn contains various information like token DNS and other information
of the Kubernetes cluster, which your kubectl will refer to in order to go ahead and connect to the
Kubernetes cluster.

and the cube config resource file.

here.

followed by the name.
The name of the container is my web server, followed by the image from which the container would be

So you have kubectl run followed by the name, which is my web server and followed by the image which
is Nginx over here.

by the image of Nginx and that's about it.
So at a high level overview, just remember that in Kubernetes terms a container is referred as a pod.
So if you do a kubectl get pods here, you will see that we have one pod called as my web server and
it is running great.
So let's explore a second command, which is basically exec into a container.
So for example, if you want to connect inside a container, what you will generally do in Docker is
you will do a Docker exec hyphen t followed by the name of the container and the bash over here.
In a similar way for the Kubernetes, you do a cube Ctl exec hyphen t followed by the name of the container.
So in Kubernetes terms as we are discussing containers is also referred as a pod.
So this is name of a pod followed by a hyphen hyphen and bash.
So now you see that the commands are very similar.
And if you are comfortable with the Docker set of commands learning Kubernetes set of commands will
be much more simpler, specifically related to pods.
So let's try it out.
So when you do a Docker PS over here, as we are discussing that, we have one container.
Let's go ahead and connect inside this container with Docker Exec.
It followed by my web server and we will make use of bash.
So immediately here you see we are connected inside the container in a similar way.
We already know that we have a pod that is available with the name of my web server.
So in order to connect it, you can do a cube Ctl exec hyphen T followed by the name of the pod, which
is my web server, followed by hyphen, hyphen and the bash.
Great.
And now you see you are connected inside the container.
So this hyphen hyphen separates your argument that you want to run as part of your container as well
as the name here.
For example, let's say you want to go ahead and run a command of LS hyphen L inside this specific pod
in Kubernetes.
So you have a hyphen hyphen followed by the command.
So this command will run inside your Kubernetes pod.
So when you do enter here, you see you are getting the output associated with your root file system
of this specific my web server port.
And one more thing I wanted to show you.
In fact, let's say that you want to go ahead and remove this specific container so you can do a docker.
RM Followed by the name of the container, which is my web server.
And immediately here you basically get an error primarily because you have a running container here.
So what you generally do, you will do a Docker stop followed by the name of the container, which is
my web server, and then you can go ahead and remove your container here.
So in a similar way, if you do a kubectl get pods.
So if you want to go ahead and remove this specific pod, you can directly go ahead and do a kubectl
delete pod, my web server and immediately your pod will be deleted here, even though if it is running
now with this base set, let's go ahead and discuss more in detail about the term of a Kubernetes pod.
So pod in Kubernetes represents a group of one or more application containers and some shared resources
for those containers.
So this can be better explained with this diagram.
So you have a pod one and within the pod one you have one container that is available and that container
has a specific IP address.
In a pod.
Two you have a one container and you have one storage volume.
In pod three you have multiple containers.
So you have two containers.
You have a single storage volume, and in a pod four you have three containers and you have two storage
volume.
And this is what it means when it states that a pod represents a group of one or more application containers.
So do note that not only there can be a single container in a pod, you can also have multiple containers
that are part of a single pod.
Now let's look into some of the benefits that this type of approach provides.
So many applications might have more than one container that are tightly coupled in a 1 to 1 relationship.
So, for example, let's say that you have an application and that application requires two containers
to be up and running.
First is the container named function zero one and second is a container name function zero two.
So what you will do first, you will do a Docker run and you will create a container from function zero
one and then you'll go ahead and create a container from function zero two.
And only after both of these containers are up, your application would be up here.
So in case if one of the container goes down, then at the end what would happen is your end application
will also go down.
Second part is that in this approach we are managing at an individual container level.
So for example, let's say that you have functions zero one function zero two things are working fine
tomorrow.
There is good amount of load that is happening, so you need to know the 1 to 1 relationship here.
So you need to have a sheet which states that function zero one is dependent on function zero two and
so on.
So in production application, this can also be quite complex.
Now one of the ways to deal with a tightly coupled application in Kubernetes is to make use of Pod.
So what you will do in Pod, you have two containers function zero one and function zero two.
So essentially what will happen is all you do, you go ahead and create a pod, and pod in turn will
go ahead and create multiple containers here.
So you do not really have to create individual containers similar to what we do in Docker.
Things become much more simplified at a pod level.
So for example, you have a pod one which consists of functions zero one function zero two things are
working quite well.
So tomorrow there is good amount of load in your website.
A lot of users are visiting and things are slowing down.
So what you can do, you can go ahead and create one more pod.
This pod in turn will go ahead and create a container of function zero one and function zero two, and
that's about it.
You do not really need to know about 1 to 1 relationship and other details.
Everything.
Kubernetes deals in the background.
Now, in the later set of videos, we will be discussing more in detail on how a pod can have multiple
set of containers.
And there are a lot of interesting things that you can do with Kubernetes.
So, for example, if something is not really working, so let's say Function zero two is not really
working in Pod one, Kubernetes can go ahead and automatically create one more pod so that your application
can go ahead and remain available all the time.
Or for example, let's say that you have two pods that are serving the production traffic.
However, the load is increasing.
Kubernetes can automatically go ahead and create more pods that can receive the production traffic so
that the load is evenly distributed.
So if you have worked with the auto scaling feature in AWS, Kubernetes can do something very similar
to that.
That's great.
So before we go ahead and do a lot of exciting things with Pods and various other Kubernetes objects,
let's go ahead and quickly revise our concepts.
First, is that a pod always runs on a node.
So for example, when you do a cube, Ctl get nodes over here, you see that you have one node available
and all the pods that we are creating, they are being created in this specific worker node.
Second important point is that a node is a worker machine within the Kubernetes environment.
Then you have each node is managed by a master, so you have a Kubernetes master which goes ahead and
manages nodes.
And fourth is a node can have multiple pods.
So for today's video, we just created one pod, but you can even create 100 pods and 100 pods can run
in a node which you have available as part of your cluster.
Now at a high level overview, some of the points might not be very clear, but as soon as we go further
down the course, each and every point you will understand in great detail.
However, with this video, the primary aim was just to give you a high level overview about the Kubernetes
pod so that we can go ahead and start with our exciting journey into the Kubernetes.

--- Introduction to Labels and Selectors ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about labels and selectors.
Now, labels are basically a key value pair that you attach to a Kubernetes objects such as pods.
So let's understand this with a simple example where you have two distinct set of environments here.
The first environment consists of a server, a database and a load balancer.
And in a similar way, a second set of environment consists of a load balancer, a server and a database.
Now if someone tells you that, Hey, can you go ahead and stop all the resources associated with a
dev environment.
Now if you look from here, it is very difficult to see which server or which database belongs to a
dev environment.
The reason why it is difficult to know is because there are no appropriate labels which are associated
with all of these resources.
Now, in a second example here, what we have done is we have added label to each and every resource
here.
So the first server has a label where the key here is name and the value is CP Labs Gateway, and it
also has a second label where the environment is production.
Now in a similar way, the second resource has a label where name is Cablelabs hyphen DB and the environment
is production.
Similarly, if you look into the second environment here, the name is CP Hyphen DB and the environment
is Dev.
Now from this label which is associated with a individual resource, you will be able to quickly find
out that hey, the lower three resources which are created, they belong to the dev environment and
now I can go ahead and stop all of these resources right away.
Now in order for you to know about this, having a label associated with this resource is very important.
So let me quickly give you an example in terms of perspective so it becomes easier for us to understand.
So I'm in my console here, and here you will see that there are three servers which are currently running.
Now let's say that someone tells you go ahead and stop a dev server.
Now from here it is very difficult for you to know which among these three servers is a dev server.
The reason why it is difficult for us to know is because there are no appropriate labels which are associated
with these three servers.
And this is the reason why attaching a label is very important.
Now here, do remember that depending upon the technology that you use, the name label will change.
So AWS might call it as tag.
Now in the Kubernetes environment, you will call it as label and so on.
So although the naming convention is different, but the overall concepts remains the same.
So if you have worked with AWS, labels are very similar to tags so that it becomes easier for you to
grasp.
So let's do one thing.
Let's go ahead and add a tag.
What I'll do, I'll add two tags.
Now if you would look into this diagram, there were two labels which were attached.
One was associated with the name and second was associated with the environment.
So let's go ahead and do that.
So here I'll call name as CP Labs hyphen production and we'll add one more tag or one more label called
as Env.
And the env here is production.
I'll go ahead and save this.
Now in a similar way for the second server, let's go ahead and add one more tag, I'll call it as name
and let's call it as payment gateway.
And for this I'll add a tag.
The tag is dev.
All right, Now if you go ahead and refresh it, the first server again, you are not able to identify
from here, but if you look into the tags here, you will see that there is a tag which is associated
with the server where the name is CP Labs hyphen production and the Env is production.
So if you click on any of the server, it is very easy for you to see that this server belongs to dev
environment or the first server belongs to a production environment.
So this is what labels are now.
Definitely you can make it much more easier so that within a year it can appear.
So let me give you one more example.
So if I add a tag with a capital name and let's call it as App Gateway, and if I click on Save, you
see it appears within the console itself.
So if you do it this way, this way also it works well because ultimately the thing that you see over
here is nothing but the value associated with the key.
So if you look into the first slide, labels are nothing but a key value pairs.
So within here also you have a key value pair where the key is name and the value is app gateway.
And similar to that, within the second slide you'll see you have key value pair.
So the key is name, the value is key labs hyphen, gateway and so on.
So this is what labels are.
Now the next important part is selectors.
Now selectors basically allows us to filter objects based on labels.
So generally in Kubernetes, whenever you are using labels, you will be using selectors as well in
a lot of occurrences.
So let's take an example.
So the first example is show me all the objects which has label where inv is equal to prod.
So now what will happen is you will see all the resources here which has the environment as production.
So this is one simple use case.
Now the second use case here is show me all the objects where environment is equal to dev.
So now it will show you all the resources where the environment is dev.
So this is the importance of selectors.
So through selectors you can look or you can filter the resources based on labels.
So again, let's quickly understand this with the terminology, because here we are working with UI,
so it becomes much more easier.
So let's take the first example where we were discussing show me all the objects, where the label is
production.
So in AWS you can easily do that with the filter here.
So you can consider this filter as a selector.
So let's do one thing.
Let's go a bit up here and within the tag keys here you have a key of env.
So I'll click on Env and I'll say production.
So now what will happen is it will only show you all the resources which belongs to the production environment
or in short all the resources which has a tag where env is production.
So this is one way.
Now you can also modify it to show you all the resource which belongs to dev environment.
So again, it will show you only one resource which belongs to the dev environment.
So this is what selectors are now through selectors irrespective of how many instance you have.
So in current environment we have three instances.
However, in production there can be hundreds or even thousands of instances.
So through the selector you will be able to select or you will be able to view the right resources based
on the labels which are associated with them.
Now, from the Kubernetes perspective, there can be multiple objects which can be part of a Kubernetes
cluster.
Now some of the objects are pods.
You have services, secrets, namespaces, you have deployments, and so many of them.
So now what you can do is you can attach a label to the objects, which are part of the Kubernetes cluster.
Now, in the example, we had attached a label or a tag associated with the EC2 instance.
Now you can even attach a label to a load balancer, a RDS instance and so on.
Similar to that, even in Kubernetes you have various objects like pods, services, secrets, and you
can attach labels to all of these objects.
So let's understand the selector part again.
So in Kubernetes perspective, as we were discussing, there can be multiple objects.
So in this diagram we have a total of six pods which are currently running now by the name of this pod.
As we were discussing, it is difficult to identify what this pod does, whether it belongs to dev environment,
whether it belongs to prod environment and so on.
So the next thing that you do is you assign a label to your Kubernetes object.
So to every pod here you assign a label where the envy is the key and dev or prod is the associated
value.
So from the label now you can easily identify that the first pod belongs to dev, the second pod belongs
to prod and so on.
So coming back to the selector, let's say that you want to have a selector where you want to list all
the pods from dev environment.
So in order to have this use case you will have to make use of selector.
So what you do, you write a selector.
So what selector will do?
Selector will remove all the pods which does not have envy is equal to dev.
So now you will only be able to see the pods which has this specific environment of dev.
Now, in a similar way, let's say now you want to list all the pods from the production environment.
So now what selector will do?
Selector will look into the labels which are associated with the objects and all the objects which do
not have the environment of prod.
Will be removed from the list.
So if you see now select, I will go ahead and remove all the objects which has the envy of dev or others
which are not prod.
So that's what selectors are.
So I hope you understood at a high level overview about the labels and selectors.
Now before we conclude, let me give you a demo for Kubernetes so that this concept can be understood
in a better way.
So let's do one thing.
Let's do a kubectl get pods here.
Now you see that there are two pods which are running now, these two pods from the name itself.
It becomes difficult to understand which of these pods belongs to dev or which of these pods belongs
to production.
So now what you can do, you can filter it out.
So let's say kubectl get pods and I'll specify by label where n is equal to dev.
And now you see it will only show you the pod which belongs to the development environment, or in short,
the pod, which has a label where the key is env and the value is death.
In a similar way you can even do env is equal to prod.
And now you see you see one more pod which has the environment is equal to production.
So in this way you can select the appropriate pods depending upon the use cases.
So let's do one thing, let's do a cube, Ctl describe Pod and before that let's do a cube.
Ctl get pods yet again and let's do a cube.
Ctl Describe Pod and I'll specify the name of one of the pods here.
Now, within the details which have been presented, if you look here, one of the details here is the
label.
Now one of the label here is env is equal to dev.
So this is the label which is associated with this pod.
This is very similar here where you have a pod and you have a label where N is equal to dev.
So that is what labels are.
And through selectors you will be able to filter the Kubernetes objects depending on the labels which
are associated with them.
Now, attaching a label to your Kubernetes objects is very important.
I'll show you one real world use case.
So in one of the organizations where I had recently joined what they used to do, they never had any
of the tags or any of the labels which are associated with the servers there.
So it was very difficult to find which server belonged to which environment.
And in one of the email thread, one of the developer requested the team member to go ahead and delete
the development EC2 instance and what the team member did.
He deleted the EC2 instance which belonged to the staging environment and all of the QA team came running
at the DevOps team asking why did they delete the staging environment?
And this was one of the reason, the reason why this mistake happened was because there was no label
which was associated with the resources in AWS.
And this is the reason why having labels is extremely important not only for the Kubernetes objects,
but also for the non Kubernetes environments like AWS.
So that's the high level overview about labels and selectors.
I hope you understood the concepts of label and selectors with respect to Kubernetes objects.

--- Overview of ReplicaSets ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we will be discussing about replica sets.
Now, the purpose of a replica set is to maintain a stable set of replica pods running at any given
moment of time.
So we can understand this with a simple diagram here where you have a replica set and within the replica
set you have two configuration settings.
One is the desired state and second is image.
So here the desired state is equal to three and the image is enginex.
So now what?
Replica set will do is that it will maintain three pods of the nginx always running within your Kubernetes
cluster.
So that is the purpose of replica set.
It always maintains the desired amount of state of the pods from the images that you have specified
to the replica set.
Now within here there are two important terms that you need to remember.
One is the desired state and second is the current state.
So the desired state is basically the state of pods which is desired at any given moment of time.
And the current state is the actual state of Pod which are currently running within your Kubernetes
cluster.
So in this diagram, the desired state is three.
That basically means that you would like to have three replicas of a pod from a nginx image to be always
up and running.
Now the current state is also three because currently there are three pods which are running.
So let's say that due to some reason the pod two has stopped working.
So now what will happen is the current state will change from 3 to 2 because currently there are only
two pods which are actively running.
However, the desired state is three.
So now what your replica set will try to do is it will go ahead and it will launch a new pod so that
your desired state matches the current state.
Now do remember here that replica set will always try and maintain the desired state is equal to current
state.
So let's do one thing.
Let's jump into the practical and look into how exactly it might look like.
So I'm in my CLI and let's quickly do one thing.
Let's do a kubectl get pods.
Now currently you will see that there are three pods which are currently running.
Now this is very similar to what we were discussing where you have a replica set and there are three
pods which are currently running as part of the replica set.
So now if you do a kubectl get replica set, you see that there is one replica set.
The name of that replica set is frontend, the desired is three, current is three and ready is three.
So this is part of the replica set.
Now as we were discussing that if your desired state is three and if due to some reason one of the pods
stops working or two pod stops working and similar replica set will launch new pods so that it always
maintains the desired state which has been configured.
So let's do one thing.
Let's go ahead and do a kubectl get pods again.
And what we'll do is this thing we'll go ahead and do a kubectl delete pod and we'll delete one of the
pods which have been created through the replica set.
All right.
So the pod is now deleted.
So now when you do a kubectl get pods again, you see that one more pod was automatically launched here.
So the age of this pod is 17 seconds.
So this pod was automatically launched by the replica set because in the earlier command we had deleted
one of the pods and the total count did not match the current set.
And this is the reason why the replica set automatically had created one more pod so that it matches
the desired state with the current state.
So that's the high level overview about replica sets.
I hope at a overview you understood what replica sets are all about.
So that's about it in today's overview video.
In the next video we'll go ahead and implement replica sets and look into the configuration options.
So I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.
