--- Introduction to Deployments ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we will be discussing about deployments.
Now, before we go ahead and understand deployments, you need to understand the challenges that you
might face when you just use replica sets.
Now, in replica sets, you get a basic functionality related to managing pods, scaling pods and similar.
So let's say that you have a replica set here which has three pods.
So currently the desired state is three.
Now tomorrow you might want five pods.
So now what do you do?
You go ahead and change the desired state of your replica set and replica set will launch two more pods
so that the current state becomes five.
So for such basic functionality you can go ahead and make use of replica sets.
However, if you want certain important functionalities related to rolling out changes, rolling back
changes and so on, then you need to go with deployments.
Now deployment provides a replication functionality with the help of replica sets along with various
additional capabilities like rolling out changes, rolling back changes and so on if required.
So you have a deployment set which sits at the top.
Now, deployment set in turn.
It makes use of replica sets to achieve the replication capability related to pods.
So this is the basic functionality.
Now along with that deployment can also do various things like rollback, it can maintain the application
revisions and so on.
So let's understand some of the benefits of deployments.
The first one is rolling out changes.
Now what happens here is that you can roll out new updates to your application using deployments.
Now let's say that you are managing your Kubernetes within your organization.
So it might happen that every few days there will be a new version of your application which has been
tested and you want to roll out that new version of your application.
So what would happen is let's understand this.
So you have a deployment, deployment uses replica set and basically you have one pod.
Now tomorrow there is a new version of your application which has been released and you want to make
sure that your production has that new version.
So now what deployment will do?
Deployment will create one more replica set.
It will launch a pod with the latest version of your application here, and then it will go ahead and
connect to that replica set.
Now, once your new version of your application is created, then deployment will go ahead and remove
the older version of your application.
All right.
So this approach makes sure that whenever a new version of your application is deployed, the application
is not down because application not being down is a very important aspect.
And whenever you update, you have to make sure that your application does not go down and deployment
takes care of that.
Now, the second important benefit of deployment is rollback of changes.
Now what would happen many times is that let's say you have deployed a new version of your application
and due to some reason, the application is giving a lot of issues to your clients or to the end facing
users.
So now what you would like to do is you would like to rollback from the latest update to the previous
working update, which was working perfectly.
So in this case, you can easily do it with the deployments now deployment behind the scenes.
Ultimately it uses replica sets.
However, it provides a lot of additional capabilities like rolling out changes, rolling back changes
and so on.
Now let's do one thing before we conclude this video.
I'll quickly show you a small demo.
So that it can be better understood.
So I'll do a cube.
Ctl get deployments.
Now you will see that there is one deployment which is currently available.
Now let's do one thing.
Let's look into the rollout history associated with this deployment.
Now you will see that there are multiple revisions which had been made to this specific application.
So you have a revision.
Two, you have a revision, three, you have revision four and you have revision five.
So let's say that you have switched to revision two and due to some reason there are a lot of errors
which are happening in this specific revision.
So now what you can do is you can roll back to revision one or you can even change your application
revision two, version three, version four and version five and similar.
So that is one of the advantages of deployments that you can not only look into the rollout history
to see on what are the updated.
Changes that have been happening to your application, but you can also roll back to a specific revision
in case if your latest version of your application is giving you an issue.
So that's the high level overview about deployments.
Again, we will be discussing in great detail in practical aspect in the upcoming videos related to
rolling out changes, also rolling back and few other important topics associated with deployments.
So with this, we'll conclude this video.
Now, this video was aimed primarily to give you a high level overview of what deployments are all about.
However, the practical is kept for the upcoming videos.
So I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Creating our first deployment ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we'll be creating our first deployment and we'll look into some of the functionalities
which deployment offers.
Now, in the introduction video to Deployments, we already understood the basics about deployments
and how it uses replica sets and some additional capabilities to have features like rolling updates,
rollback changes and others.
So let's jump into the practical and understand on how deployments would work.
Now, if you would have gone through the video associated with the replica sets, you would already
know the structure and the importance of every field within the Yaml file.
So what we'll do.
We'll make few changes here.
Now, since we are working with deployments, the kind here would change to deployment.
And along with that you can also change the name so that it is easier for us to identify, I'll call
it as KP Labs hyphen deployment.
So this is the only thing which is required.
Now let's do one thing from the terminal.
Let's go ahead and apply the deployment dot yaml file.
So here it says that you have a deployment called as KP Labs hyphen deployment, which has been created.
Now when you do a kubectl get deployments here, you will see that you have one deployment which is
created.
The name is Labs Hyphen Deployment.
Now we already discussed that deployment makes use of replica sets to make sure that there are always
a specific number of replicas which are running now even within your deployments dot yaml you see within
the spec field you specify the replicas and here you have specified five.
So what would happen is deployment would create a replica set which would have a desired state of five.
So in order to understand that you can do a cube, Ctl get replica set and you will see that there is
one replica set which is created which has a desired number of five.
And if you do a kubectl get pods, you should see that there are five pods which are currently running.
So these five pods which are currently running, these five pods are managed by replica set and replica
set in turn is managed by the deployments.
So now that we are aware about basics on how you can create a deployment, let's look into one of the
major features of deployment which is rolling out of changes.
Now we already have the architecture here where we have a deployment, we have a replica set and we
have n number of pods within the replica set.
So the next thing, let's say you want to update the application.
So now what deployment will do?
Deployment will create a new replica set.
It will launch all the pods within that replica set and that replica set will be connected to the deployment.
Now once this pods within the replica set is running perfectly, then deployment will go ahead and it
will remove the pods associated with the previous application.
So this is one of the ways in which you can roll out changes with the deployments.
So let's do one thing.
Let's go ahead and try this out and look into how exactly it might look like now within the deployments
dot yaml file.
Within the spec section, you see the image through which the pods are launching is enginex.
So let's assume that this is the version one of the application.
So tomorrow let's say you want to specify a custom enginex version here.
Now if you look into the Nginx official Docker image, there are a lot of versions which are available.
So let's say that now in the second version of our application, we want to use the Nginx version one
point 17.3.
So what I can do, I can say one point 17.3.
All right.
So I have updated my application here, so I'll go ahead and I'll save this.
So now let's do one thing.
Let's go ahead and apply the changes within the deployments dot yaml file.
Now when you do a kubectl get replica set you will see that there are two replica sets which are created
here.
Now this is very similar to the architecture where we were discussing that in order for a new roll out
change to happen, deployment will create a second replica set and this second replica set will have
the new version of your pods running.
Now, along with that, it will go ahead and it will also start to remove the pods which are present
within the older replica set in a roll out fashion.
So let's do a kubectl get replica sets yet again and you would see that the older replica set there
are zero pods which are currently present and the newer replica set, it has all the pods which are
currently running.
So this new replica set has the latest version of the Nginx image which is running.
So in order to understand this in a better way, let's go ahead and do a cube.
Ctl Describe deployment followed by your deployment name.
So this will basically give you information about your deployment.
Now, one of the important section here is the event section, because this section will basically give
us more detail about how the rollout of changes happen.
Now, the first step that it had done was it had created a replica set which had five number of pods.
So this was the first event when we had created our first deployment.
After this first event, what we had done, we had updated the Nginx image to one point 17.3.
So now what deployments did It created a new replica set.
So if you would see here, the replica set name here starts with five, five, eight.
However, the replica set name earlier was 95.
So it's the second event occurred after we updated the image within our deployment dot yaml file.
So now what it did, it created a new replica set and it launched two pods within that replica set.
Now once both of these spots started to run, then it scaled down the older replica set here.
So if you would see it scaled down one of the pods which were running in the older replica set.
Now once this scaled down event happened, it again launched a new pod within the new replica set.
And after a new pod got launched in the new replica, set it again, scaled down one pod in the older
replica set.
And at the last what happened?
You had all the pods from the older replica sets which were scaled down and all the pods in the new
replica sets were launched.
So this is the new replica set where you have five pods which are currently running.
However, the older replica set where the older version of your application was running, that replica
set has zero pods.
So when you do a kubectl get replica set, you would see that deployment basically maintains both the
replica sets here.
Now one important part to remember so how this would happen.
So one question that would come is whether you can change the way this would work.
And the answer is definitely.
So basically, if you go a bit up here under the rolling update strategy, it says 25% maximum unavailable.
So you can change the way in which the scaling up and scaling down of replica would happen.
So if you change the rolling update strategy here.
So before we conclude, let me quickly show you one thing.
Now, when you run a Kubectl rollout history command, it basically maintains the revision associated
with your changes.
So earlier, if you remember, we had a simple nginx image and that was revision one.
Now later we change the nginx image and that was associated with the revision two.
So if you want to get more information, you can say revision one and it basically will tell you that
here the image was nginx.
Now if you do revision two here, you can see the exact changes which were made.
So within the revision two you see the image has been changed from Nginx to Nginx 1.7.3.
So this revision is a great functionality because what can happen is let's say that you have shifted
to revision two and there are a lot of issues which are happening in revision two.
So then you can go ahead and even roll back to the previous revision, which was known to be working
well for your customers.
So coming back to it, let's look into some of the important pointers that you need to remember associated
with deployments.
The first important pointer here is that deployment ensures that only a certain number of pods are down
while your application is updated.
Now, by default, it ensures that at least 25% of the desired number of pods are up.
That basically means that 25% maximum of the pods would remain unavailable.
Now this is very important.
So let's let's say, for example, that you have a deployment where there are three pods.
Now you are updating your application.
So deployment can depending upon the strategy and your configuration deployment can at a time bring
down all the three pods of your application.
So at that.
Moment of time, your application would lead to 100% of downtime.
All right.
So you do not want that.
You do not want deployment to.
Immediately terminate all your pods of your running application because that would lead to your downtime.
So what you might want that it let's say that there are three pods which are running deployment can
go ahead and terminate one pod.
So two pods are still running which will serve your traffic.
Now along with that deployment, will go ahead and launch one more pod of your new application.
All right.
Now, one more part of your new application is launch.
It is working perfectly.
Then maybe deployment can go ahead and remove one more pod of your previous application.
It can go ahead and launch one more pod of your new application.
Then remove the older pod and launch the third pod associated with the new application.
So it all depends upon the strategy and your configurations which you do to your deployment within your
environment.
And along with that, the third very important point here is that deployment keeps the history of the
revision which has been made.
This is something that we have already seen.
So that's the high level overview about deployments.

--- maxSurge and maxUnavailable ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we are going to discuss about the Mac's search and Mac's unavailable options
in deployments.
Now, whenever you perform an update in deployments, there are two important options that you need
to know.
First one is the Mac's search, and second one is the Mac's unavailable.
So the Mac's search basically defines the maximum number of ports that can be scheduled above the original
number of ports.
So let's take an example.
Let's say that you have a deployment and that deployment has a total of five ports.
So basically are starting a deployment with the replicas of five.
Now you have gone ahead and you want to set a different image.
So let's say that the deployment with five replicas is running based on engine X image and now you set
to the Apache image.
So when that update happens to the deployment during the update process, what generally happens is
a new set of ports are created on top of the existing ports.
So let's say the existing port is five.
For an example, two more ports will be created.
So let's say then at the end of time there will be seven ports and after the two new ports based on
Apache image are running to older ports based on engine x image would be removed.
Similar would be as part of the rolling update.
So this is just a high level example.
The second one is the max available, which defines the maximum number of ports that can be unavailable
during the update.
Now, again, this is also an important option because let's say that you have a deployment rate of
two and depending upon the number of visitors that are visiting your website, you need at least two
replica ports to be available at an instant of time.
Now let's say that you modified the image associated with the port deployment and now what will happen
is one of the port will be removed for a few seconds during the update process.
So during that few seconds only one replica would be running.
So that would mean that half of the visitors who are visiting your website, they will either get an
error or the connection would become very slow.
So depending upon the use case of your organization, you can go ahead and configure both of these configuration
parameter accordingly.

number of ports that will be running at an instant of time or there will be a little less number of
ports that will be running at an instant of time during the deployment process.

--- Kubernetes Secrets ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about Kubernetes secrets.
So before we go ahead and discuss more about Kubernetes secret, let's look into a typical challenge
which almost all of the organization has to go through.
So let's say that you have an application which basically runs on a pod and it needs to connect to a
MySQL database to initialize so that it starts to work properly.
So you have a pod and you have your MySQL database.
And this app basically needs to authenticate to the MySQL database.
Now, in order for the application to start working, it has to authenticate.
So what in a typical organization generally happens is that the developer hard codes the credentials.
All right.
So what would happen is that you have a pod.
So this pod basically launches from a specific Docker image and the developer would hardcode the credentials.
Like here you see you have a DB underscore user as admin and db underscore password as my password.
So these are the credentials which are hardcoded within the Docker image by itself.
All right.
So since these credentials are hardcoded, once your application starts, it can directly authenticate
with the username and password with the MySQL database.
So this kind of approach is something that you will see in a lot of organizations, which is still happening.
Now there are a lot of risks if you work with this type of approach.
So let's look into some of them.
First, is anyone having access to the container repository can easily fetch the credentials.
Now, since these credentials are basically within the Docker image itself, any user who has access
to the container repository where the Docker image is stored, he'll be able to read the credentials
from the Docker image.
Second is, developer needs to have credentials of the production system.
So if this is a production MySQL database, then since the developer would be building his own Docker
image, the dev team will have to give developer the username and password in plain text.
So again, this is a big no no.
And third is updating of credentials will lead to a new Docker image being built.
Now generally what happens is that let's say that you have a compliance policy where you have to rotate
your DB credentials every 90 days.
So every 90 days you will have to rebuild your Docker image considering that the credentials are hardcoded.
So these are the three important things that needs to be considered.
So what happens is that instead of hard coding the credentials, what you can do is you can store the
credentials centrally.
So what you have now is you have a central secret store, and within this central secret store, you
store your credentials here and your application does not have any hardcoded credentials.
So whenever a pod gets launched, what your application can do is that it can fetch the credentials
from a central secret store.
And once it fetches the credentials, it can use that credentials to authenticate with your MySQL database.
All right.
So the first step is you put your credentials to a central secret store.
Second step is that app can fetch that credentials and step three is the authentication part.
So there are three advantages here.
First is that you do not really store the credentials within the pod.
All right.
So your pod does not really have the credentials hardcoded.
So even if someone goes ahead and reads your Docker image, he will not find anything.
Second is the developer will not have the credentials.
All the developer will have to do is that they will have to write a logic to pull the credentials from
a central secret store, and that's about it.
So the developer will not have access to credentials.
So that is the second big advantage.
The third advantage is that since the credentials are not hard coded, even if the credentials are changed,
your Docker image will not have to be rebuilt.
So that is the third advantage here.
Now the question is who will put the credentials here?
So if let's say that you have a DB credentials, then you can assign a DB team member who can put the
credentials within the central secret store.
All right.
So the DB team can put the credentials within the central secret store and they can manage it.
So let's say after 90 days, if your password is changing, then they can go ahead and update a password
within the central secret store.
And that's about it.
All right.
So if you look into the app code here, so you have a two scenario.
First scenario is where the credentials were hardcoded.
So if you look here username as admin and the password is within the plain text here.
All right.
So you have a hardcoded credentials and in the second approach here within the password, you have a
logic here.
So this logic can fetch the credentials from a central secret store so you no longer have the plaintext
password within your application code.
Now the question that comes is what exactly is this central secret store?
Now, from the name itself, we can identify that this is a place where the credentials can be stored
centrally and Kubernetes secrets is exactly what it does.
Now, Kubernetes secret is not the only one which provides this solution.
There are various other solutions which can also be used.
So one of the famous ones is AWS Secrets Manager.
So Secrets manager again, is a very mature solution.
And a lot of organizations, they make use of this.
Then you also have the parameter store, which can also be used as a central secret store where the
credentials can be stored.
Now, another mature solution here is the Hashicorp vault, where your central secrets can be stored,
and it can be fetched by the App Pod to do various operations like authenticating against the database.
So there are a lot of solutions which are available.
Now, since we are preparing for the Kubernetes certification, our solution to understand would be
the Kubernetes secrets.
Now, a secret is basically an object that contains a small amount of sensitive data such as a password,
a token or a key.
Now, as expected, basically it allows customers to store the secrets centrally to reduce the risk
of exposure.
So it can be better understood with this diagram where you have a Kubernetes secret and now you are
basically storing your credentials within the Kubernetes secrets.
So anyone who wants to fetch the credentials, they can directly get it from the Kubernetes secrets.
Now, in order to create a Kubernetes secret, as far as the is concerned, the command is pretty straightforward.
So you have a kubectl create secret, then you have a type.
So there are three types.
One is generic, second is Docker registry, third is TLS.

--- Mounting Secrets Inside Pods ---

Hey, everyone.

Now, till now, we have been looking into the basics on how you can create a secret.
And we also looked into how we can access the secret with the help of Kubectl Command.
However, generally, whenever you create a secret, the purpose of the secret is to make it available
to your application, which is running within your cluster.
And there are several approaches in which you can make your secret available to a container.
So one of the primary method is by making use of volumes, and second is by making use of environment
variables.
So let's do one thing.
Let's jump into practical and understand both of these approaches.

Now within this Yaml file, if you look here, the first section is pretty straightforward.
We are just creating a pod.
Now this is something that we already know.
Now the second aspect here, the second section is associated with the volume mount.
So what we are basically doing, we have a volume of foo, the mount path is e.t.c. foo.
So this is the directory and this is a read only directory.
And we are associating our secret call as the first secret within this specific mount path.
Now again, we'll be discussing in detail about the volume mounts during the section of storage.

And now our pod is within the running state.
So now let's do one thing.

All right.
So we have gone inside the container.

So if you go to foo and do an LS, you will see that you have a file called DB Pass and the output of
the file will basically be the password which was associated with the key of DB pass.
So this is how you can mount your secrets inside a specific container.
So now application can use this specific password to authenticate against the specific database.
So in this approach, since you have your secret mounted in a specific directory, it comes under the
category of volumes here.
Now you also have a second approach in which you can share the secret inside a container, and that
is through the environment variables.
So I have one more file called as secret hyphen env dot yaml.
Now this file is also quite similar.

And this environment variable will basically have the value associated with the secret.

and your application can basically fetch the value associated with a given environment variable to get

Now, do remember that in both the approaches that we were discussing in Volume Mount as well as within

it is within the decoded format.

--- Understanding ConfigMaps ---

Hayden.
And welcome back.
So in today's video, we'll be discussing about config maps.
Now, config maps is a pretty important topic.
Specifically if you're working as a developer and even if you're working as an administrator, config
maps are something that you will be extensively using within your Kubernetes environment.
So let's go ahead and understand config maps with a simple use case.
So let's understand the use case here.
So let's assume that we have an app, a container and depending upon the environment, the configuration
settings associated with this app container will need to be changed.
So let's look into some example properties.
So let's say that you have a dev environment, so you have two pods or you can consider two containers.
Now for the dev container of app A you need certain environment like app dot env is equal to dev app
dot mem is equal to 2048M and app dot properties is equal to dev dot env dot url.
Now when this container goes to prod then you do not really want these values.
You want a new values like app dot env is changed from dev to prod app dot mem is 8096 and app dot properties
is prod dot env dot url.
Now generally what many of the organizations they do is they create two different container images.
Now in these two container images, although the application is same, but the configuration file which
has these parameters are different.
So just for that single configuration file, they have multiple container images and this is not the
best practice.
So let's say that tomorrow you want this dev dot environments configuration of app dot mem to be changed
from 204 8 to 3 096.
So what you will have to do is you will have to rebuild the entire container image.
So this is a tedious task and this is the reason why config maps proves to be very useful.
Now what config maps allows us to do is it allows us to centrally store data.
So what happens now is that instead of hard coding these things within the container image, what you
do is you centrally store this data.
So currently you're centrally storing the data associated with the dev properties and you're centrally
storing the data associated with the prod properties.
And from here you are mounting or you are referencing this central data from your container image.
Now one of the second advantages of centrally storing data is that you can dynamically change the values.
So let's say that tomorrow you want to add one more parameter within your configuration file.
If your configurations are stored centrally, then you can easily do that.
However, if your configuration files are hard coded within the container image, then if you want to
change even a certain parameter, you will have to completely rebuild that specific image.
All right, so this method of centrally storing the data is achieved with the help of config maps within
the Kubernetes.
So as you see, you have this config map object and within config map there are two config maps which
are available.
One is associated with the dev and it is mounted within the dev pod and second is associated with the
prod and it is mounted within the prod pod.
Now as we are discussing within the first slide that if you already have all of your configurations
which have been stored centrally, then you no longer need to have a separate container image.
So what you can do is you can have a single container image and depending upon which environment your
image is being deployed, let's say that you are deploying your container image within the dev environment.
Then automatically you can reference the dev config map here.
If you are pushing your container image to the prod environment, then you can directly push this prod
properties to this container image.

--- Overview of Service ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about a very important topic called as Kubernetes service.
So let's get started.
Now, we already know that whenever you create a pod, you will have a corresponding IP address associated
with that pod.
So let's take an example that there are two pods here.
One is front end and second is backend.
So the frontend will have a specific IP address.
In this case it is 10 to 44 0.5 and the backend also has an IP address which is 1044 0.6.
So if frontend has to communicate with the backend, it can directly connect it with the IP address
which is associated with the backend pod.
So generally in organizations, what you will have is you will typically have a front end application
and that front end application will connect to the back end application to execute certain logic.
So in order for both of them to communicate, the front end would typically need the IP address to connect
to the back end.
So let's assume that within the front end configuration file you have specified the back end underscore
URL configuration parameter as 10 to 44 0.6, which is basically the IP address associated with the
back end pod.
So the front end application which is running it will refer to this configuration parameter to connect
to the back end, to send the appropriate data which would be computed by the back end servers.
Now let's assume that due to some reason, the back end pod has stopped working.
All right.
So there are some issues and this pod has stopped responding altogether.
So now if we have deployment, what would happen is deployment will go ahead and launch a new pod.
And this new pod will have a new set of IP address, which is 1040 40. ten.
So now the problem that is happening here is that the application which is running in front end within
the configuration parameter, it still has the IP address associated with the older pod, which is 10
to 44 0.6.
However, the new pod which got created, that pod has an IP address of 10 to 40 40. ten.
So this basically means that the front end application will error out.
It will start to send a lot of Http 500 back to the client.
So that basically means the front end application, although it is working, it will go ahead and it
will send a lot of error back to the client even though there is a right back end here.
So you have a new back end pod which is created here, although this back end pod is perfectly working,
the front end will error out primarily because the configuration file does not have the updated IP address
associated with the new back end pod which got created.
So this is one issue that you will typically face specifically whenever you are hardcoding the IP address
or even the DNS name associated with the pod.
Now the second issue that generally you might face is that let's say that you have a front end pod and
you have multiple back end pods.
So we have already discussed about replica sets and deployments.
It might happen that there are three back end pods now, now tomorrow, since there are a lot of requests,
this back end pod might scale to ten.
So the question is how will you update the IP addresses of all of these pods in the front end configuration
files?
Because if you're running Daemonsets, then your replica sets can scale a lot and currently there are
three tomorrow.
If there are ten, how will you constantly keep updating the configuration file of your front end?
So that is a second issue.
So in order to overcome both of these issues that we were discussing, you can make use of Kubernetes
services.
So what happens in Kubernetes services is that you create a gateway.
So here let's call this as a back end gateway.
Now this gateway can connect to all of the back end pods.
So if there are five new pods which are created, all of these five new pods will be associated with
this back end gateway.
And now only thing that front end has to do is that it has to send the request to the back end gateway.
So let's assume that this specific pod has stopped working.
So now you don't really have to worry about it because the back end gateway is present.
It can simply go ahead and not send a request to this specific back end pod.
However, the requests are still being sent to the other two pods which are running, or if there is
a new pod which gets created, it can also automatically be associated with the back end gateway.
So this back end gateway here, which acts as an intermediary layer.
So this is called as a service.
So a Kubernetes service can act as an abstraction, which can provide a single IP address and a DNS
through which pod can be accessed.
So this specific back end gateway over here, this back end Gateway, can have a IP address and the
DNS name.
So that IP address associated with this back end gateway can be configured within the configuration
file associated with the front end application.
And that back end gateway will act as a single point through which all the requests would be routed.
So this also can be understood.
With this diagram here where you have a service here so you can consider service as a back end gateway.
So this service is associated with three pods over here, similar to what we were discussing here,
where you have a service and this service can associate it with the multiple pods.
Now, since this service is associated with the multiple pods, there are a lot of things that you can
perform.
One among them is load balancing so you can load balance across multiple pods.
You can easily scale your pods.
So today you have three pods.
Tomorrow you need ten pods.
You can easily scale your pods without having to change anything at the front end.
And this is a very important use case.
So these are some of the important functionality associated with a Kubernetes service.
Now let's do one thing.
Let's jump into the demo and look into how exactly this might look like.

So in this case, if Port One fails, then service can detect and it can only route the traffic to the
port which is currently running, or if there is a new port which is created, that port can automatically
be associated with the service.
So you do not really have to change the configuration file associated with the front end application.
And the important part, as we discussed, that your service can also allow you to do load balancing
between multiple pods so that no single pod can have a huge amount of load.
So that's the high level overview about the Kubernetes service.
Now there are a few important points to remember that there are several types of Kubernetes services
which are available which you can make use of.

--- Service Type: ClusterIP ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about one of the service types, which is cluster IP.
Now, whenever a service is of type cluster IP, what happens is that an internal IP address of the
cluster is associated with that specific service.
Now, since an internal cluster IP is assigned to the service, that specific service can only be reachable
from within the cluster.
Now, one important part to remember here is that the cluster IP is the default service type.
So that basically means that if you create a service and if you do not define a service type by default,
it will create a service with the service type of cluster IP.
Now, we already know this specific diagram where you have a service with the name of backend gateway,
and this service has three endpoints, and these three endpoints are nothing but the IP address of the
backend pods.
Now the service which is created over here, this service also has an IP address.
Now we have already seen this in the previous video where we had done a curl on the IP address associated
with that service.
So if you see this service has an IP address of ten dot 244 .0. 20.
So this IP address is nothing but the internal IP address of the cluster.
So this is the reason why it is called as the cluster IP.
Now the reason why, again, it is called as the cluster IP is because the IP address of this service
is one of the IP address of the cluster, which is internal.
Now, since this is an internal cluster IP, that basically means that only the objects which are within
the cluster will be able to access this specific IP address.
I from the internet cannot directly send a request to this IP address, although if there is a port
which is running, it can definitely connect to this IP address because this pod also has the IP address
of the internal cluster which it has received and also the appropriate routing.
Now in the previous video, we had created a service.
Now if you look into the service Yaml file, we never specified the type of service here and this is
the reason why it has received the default service type as cluster IP.
Now when you do a cube, Ctl get service over here you have a service of K Labs hyphen service which
was created in the previous video.
Now if you look into here, the type is cluster IP and it is also assigned a cluster IP here.
So this IP address, which is assigned to this specific service, this IP address belongs to the cluster.
So that's the high level overview about the service type of cluster IP.
Again, this is a very simple service type and we have already seen about how we can deploy it in the
previous video.
However, we understood what this cluster IP by itself means.
So with this, we'll conclude this video.
I hope this video has been informative for you and look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Using Selectors for Service Endpoints ---

Hey, you, and welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we look into how we can integrate the labels and selectors while configuring
a community service.
Now, at this stage, we have been creating a service.
We have been creating the endpoint manually.
And in the endpoint we have also been manually defining the IP address of the port that we want this
service to redirect traffic to.
So although for one or two IP, it is fine, but let's say in a production cluster data say 500 points.
Now in case if you are using this manual approach, then you'll have to add 500 IP addresses in the
endpoints.
So this is something that you do not really want, and this is also something that is not really scalable.
So manual approach of adding the IP address in End Point is something that we want to avoid for production
environments.
And in order to improve the overall functionality, communities has allowed the integration of selectors
with services.
So what really happens here is that whenever a client creates a service, they can define that.
Add all the boards with a label of app is equal to end innings.
So now whenever a service will be created, the end point of that service will be associated with the
IP address associated with all the ports that has this specific label.
So this type of approach is much more simpler.
So by only adding the appropriate selector as part of the service, you can go ahead and select all
the boards.
It can be a hundred, 200, 500 boards, etc., and added as part of your overall service endpoints.
So this is what is referred to as integration of service with selectors.
So let's go ahead and try.
Don't.

--- Kubernetes Networking Model ---

Hayden.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about the Kubernetes networking model.
Now, we already know that Kubernetes was built to run on a distributed systems where there can be hundreds
of worker nodes in which the pods would be running.
So you will have a Kubernetes master and there can be hundreds of worker nodes where everything would
be running.
So in such type of architecture, networking is one of the primary component that you have to work with.
So by understanding the overall networking related aspects that Kubernetes defined and mandates, it
allows administrators to properly run monitor as well as the troubleshooting related aspects for the
applications which are running within the Kubernetes clusters.
Now, when you discuss about the networking model, one important part to remember is that Kubernetes
imposes the following fundamental requirement on any networking implementation that takes place.
So there are three requirements.
First one is that ports on a node can communicate with all the ports on all the nodes without requiring
of a additional Nat.
Second is all nodes can communicate with all ports without an additional Nat, And third is the IP that
a port sees itself as is the same IP address that others see it as it is.
So these are the three important requirements that Kubernetes mandates.
So depending upon these requirements, there are certain challenges and solutions that needs to be implemented.
So in total, there are four different networking challenges.
First one is related to container to container networking.
Second is port to pod networking.
Third is port to service networking, and fourth is Internet to service networking.
So let's quickly have a look into this.
So the first one is container to container networking.
So we know that container to container networking primarily happens inside a pod.
So a pod can have multiple containers within it.
So Pod can contain a group of containers with the same IP address and communication between the container
inside pod happens via localhost.
So let's consider the outer layer to be pod.
And within the pod there are two containers.
First one is the application container, second one is the database container.
So the application container can communicate with the database container via the localhost, followed
by the port in which the application is running.
So it can be localhost followed by 3306.
In a similar way, if a database container wants to communicate with the application container and if
the application container is running on port 80, then database container can do localhost colon 80
and it will be able to connect with itself.
So these are the container to container networking related aspects.
Second is port to pod networking.
So primary aim here is to understand at a high level overview about how port to pod communication works.
So you have one pod, you have second pod.
Each pod has the interface Eth0, which will have the pod IP over here.
And then it also connects to one more interface.
In this case it is Eth0 Port two has V one, and both of them goes through a bridge through which the
inter communication happens between them.
So you don't really need to go into detail if you just understand this at a high level overview, that's
quite good enough.
So currently I am connected to the CLI and if you do a kubectl get pods you see that there are two pods
that are running over here and when you do an output of wide over here, you will also see the IP address
that are associated with each of the pod over here.
Now let's go ahead and quickly connect to one of the pods.
And inside this, if you do a ifconfig, you will see that you have a et0 interface and you will also
see the IP address, which is one 7217 0.4 similar to the output that we had seen.
All right.
So now we know that each pod has the Et0 interface over here and it has an associated IP.
Now apart from that, you will have multiple interfaces like V, zero, v one, and that in turn connects
to a specific bridge.
So if I quickly do a ifconfig on the host, you will see that there are multiple interfaces that are
available and they should also be one bridge, primarily because when communication needs to happen,
there has to be a bridge that would be involved.
So in this case you can quickly find that out.
So when you see this specific destination, which is one 7270 zero zero, so any communication that
goes towards this destination, the interface here is the Docker zero.
So in this case, the Docker zero is basically the overall bridge that is used for communication.
The next point is associated with port to service communication.
So Kubernetes service can act as an abstraction, which can provide a single IP address and DNS through
which the pods can be accessed.
So basically, we already know that when you create a deployment, there can be multiple amount of pods
and tracking the IP of each pod is something that is difficult.
So what you can do is you can create a gateway which basically acts as a middleware, and this gateway
keeps the track of each and every pod that are running.
And this gateway is also called as the service.
So all you have to do is you have to direct the traffic to the service, and service can load balance
the traffic across the pods that are running inside it.
The next point is Internet to service networking.
So let's say that you have two different service example service and lab service, and each service
is directing to a single port, and someone from the Internet wants to communicate to this specific
port via the service.
So how can you do that?
So that can be achieved with the help of ingress and ingress controller.
So Kubernetes Ingress is basically a collection of routing rules which governs how external users access
the service, which is running within the Kubernetes cluster.
So for example, whenever the user type example.com, then you want the traffic to go to the example
service.
If a user types say labs in, then the traffic should be routed to labs, service and so on.
So that rules can be defined as part of the Kubernetes ingress.
And you can also have a ingress controller, which is basically it can be a load balancer, which in
turn will forward the traffic to the underlying services that are running.
So that is basically an Internet to service networking.
So in most of the organizations, you will see that a load balancer is used.
The traffic from the client goes to the load balancer and from the load balancer, the traffic goes
to the underlying services that are running within your Kubernetes cluster.
So that is the very high level overview about the Kubernetes networking model.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Understanding Liveness Probe ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about the liveness probe.
Now, generally what happens is that many a times when you are running your application for a long period
of time, eventually the application will transition to a broken state, and that cannot be recovered
except by when it is restarted.
So what organizations they generally use to do is that they use to use some kind of external tools like
Monit.
So what Monit does is that whenever it sees that the application has been broken or it is not running,
it will automatically restart that application.
And that used to be quite a time saver because at the middle of a night suddenly you see that your application
is not working and the DevOps team or the Sysops team manually has to log in to the server and restart
your application.
So that used to be a big pain.
So a third party tool helps here which automates the restart if your application is in a broken state.
Now, similar to that, Kuverit is also provides us with a feature of liveness probe which can detect
and also provides a remedy for such kind of a situation.
So within this image you can see that you have a Nginx and Nginx might be running for many months and
suddenly sometime you will see that Nginx is in a broken state.
So if this nginx is in a broken state most of the times if you just restart it, it works pretty well.
So that restart of your application automatically when it breaks can be achieved with the help of liveness
probe.
So let's understand this with a simple diagram here.
Now you have a Kubernetes cluster, and the first step here is basically it verifies whether your application
is running.
So in the step one, it is able to verify that your application is running.
So if you look into the table here in the column one here, your status is healthy and there is no action
which is taking place Now in the step two, the Kubernetes verifies, let's say, with the liveness
probe that the application is not working.
The application inside your container has stopped working.
So now within the status you see it has marked it as unhealthy.
And what happens once it marked it as unhealthy is that it will restart it.
So once the Kubernetes cluster verifies that your application is unhealthy in the step three of the
table, if you see that the application is again healthy.
However, within the step two, under the status, since it is unhealthy, there is a corresponding
action of restart which has been taken place.
So once restart has taken place, your application is up and running yet again.
So this entire procedure of monitoring and also having an action can be achieved with the help of liveness
probe.
So I'm in my atom editor here and we have a simple file of liveness probe dot yaml and it basically
contains two sections here.
One is the liveness probe and second is the section which basically creates a pod.
So the first section here, it creates a pod from the image of Ubuntu.
And in the second section we have defined the liveness probe.
So this is very similar where you have a Kubernetes cluster.
Now we are defining a pod in the first section.
So in the first section we are creating a pod and in the second section we are telling Kubernetes to
regularly send a liveness probe to the container to verify whether the application is up and running
or not.
So within the second section here, you see that within the liveness probe I have specified a command
called as service Nginx status.
So this command is basically will be run by the liveness probe to verify whether the application is
running.
If it is not running, then the Kubernetes will restart your container.
So let's quickly have a demo and look into how exactly this might look like.
So before we go ahead and run that, let's do a cube.
Ctl run from the image of Ubuntu.
So this will create a simple pod here.
All right.

So currently you see it does not really have a nginx.

So let's quickly do a kubectl exec into this pod and let's specify the command service Nginx status.
So now it is giving you the same error as earlier.
It is saying that no Nginx service is present and it also is saying you that the command terminated
with the exit code of one.
That basically means that this command gave you an error.
Now something similar to this.
If I replace this, let's do a ls on temp directory.
You will have no output because there is no file within the temp directory.

cases.
First case is when nginx is not installed at all.
Second case is when you have nginx installed but the nginx is not running.

So now, oops, I'm running quite a lot because of confusions.

So this is a high level overview about echo status.

And as expected, because when you do a cube, Ctl exec into the liveness probe and when you do a service
nginx status, this command has failed because there is no service nginx which is currently running.
So when the liveness probe runs this same command service engine status, it will get an error over
here.
And when it receives an error, what liveness probe will do is that it will go ahead and restart the
overall container.
So basically it will kill and start the container which is running.
So now let's go ahead and do a cube.
Ctl get pods yet again.
And currently you see within the restart you have a count of one.
So it will continuously keep on doing this unless and until it receives an eco status of zero.
That means the command has been successful.
So that's the high level overview about what liveness probes are.
Now, do remember here that there are three types of probes which can be used here.
One is Http, second is command and third is TCP.
Now in this demo we have taken an example of a command type where we specified the command which liveness
probe had to execute to verify if the application is running or not.
Now you can also make use of Http probe where if your application, let's say web server is running,
you can send a Http get request and verify the response.
Whether it is receiving a Http 200 response or a Http 400 response and something similar.
So there are various ways in which you can verify whether your application is running with the help
of liveness probes.
So that's the high level overview about this video.

--- Understanding Readiness Probe ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about the readiness probe.
Now it can happen that an application is running, but it is temporarily unavailable to serve the traffic.
So we can understand this with an example.
Let's say that an application is running, but it is still loading its large configuration file from
an external vendor.
So in such cases even the application is running.
But since it is still loading the configuration file, it is not ready to serve the production traffic.
So in such cases, if we use the liveness probe, liveness probe will consider this application to be
running and it will start to serve the traffic if it is under the load balancer.
So we do not want that, nor we want it to kill the container since it is not ready.
So in such cases we make use of the readiness probe.
So let's understand this with a simple example here.
Let's say you have an antivirus application here.
Now this application is currently running, but it is receiving its updates from a centralized server.
Now generally what happens is that let's say in Windows you have a exe file.
So exe file can be two year old, three year old, four year old.
So when you double click on that exe file and when you install it within your windows, that will basically
lead to your antivirus being running.
But antivirus being running does not mean that it is updated with the latest patches.
So what you want is once your antivirus is running, it should fetch the latest updates, the latest
virus signatures, and then it should be considered as ready.
Otherwise, even though it is running, it should not be considered as ready.
So for such kind of a use case, readiness probe proves to be quite useful.
So we can understand this with a much more high level overview diagram where you have two pods which
are currently running.
Now.
The first pod is of antivirus and it has downloaded all the latest updates from the Central Antivirus
server.
And once it has downloaded the latest updates, the status is now ready.
So now you have one more antivirus pod which is just created.
However, that antivirus pod, the update is still in progress.
So even though the antivirus service by itself is running, but it is not ready since it is still updating.
So this is the reason why whenever you make use of load balancer, then you can have a ability to surf
all the traffic towards the application whose status is ready.
So in the second pod here, since the status is not ready because it is still updating progress since
the status is not ready, no traffic will reach this specific pod.
All the traffic will only reach the pod.
Who has the status of ready.
So this is one example where readiness probe proves to be quite useful.
Now when you make use of liveness probe, as we were discussing, liveness probe can basically let's
say when we take an example of command, it can check whether the antivirus service is running and if
it is running, then liveness probe will consider your antivirus to be perfectly ready and then load
Balancer will start to send the traffic to your antivirus.
So since the antivirus is still update in progress, and if you are sending virus signatures, it can
happen that the signature might not be detected.
So in such kind of a use cases, readiness probes proves to be very useful.
Now syntax of readiness probe is quite similar to the liveness probe where you have readiness probe.
Again, within the exec section, you have the command where you specify the command that needs to be
checked in order for it to determine whether your application is ready to serve the traffic or not.
Now, definitely, if you see over here, the command is cat temp healthy.
So that basically means that your readiness probe will verify whether this file is present or not.
If it is present in the readiness probe will pass as successful.
If this file is not present, then it will fail.
Now, in such cases, you have to make sure that once your antivirus update progress has completed,
then it should go ahead and create this specific file.
So let's do one thing.
Let's take a quick demo to understand on how exactly this might work.
So I'm in my atom editor here, and similar to the liveness probe, we have two sections.
First section basically creates a pod of ubuntu and second section basically is associated with the
readiness probe.
So what we do in readiness probe here, this readiness probe basically has a exec and it will run this
command which is cat temp healthy.
So this command will be successful if this file is.
Present.
And if this file is not present, then this command will fail and hence your readiness probe will also
fail.
So let's quickly jump into the demo and see on how exactly it might look like.
So let's quickly do a kubectl apply on readiness probe dot yaml.
All right, now let's go ahead and do a kubectl get pods.
Now if you look into the status of the pod, the status of the pod is running.
However, within the ready, it says that it is not ready.
So you see this zero basically means that it is not ready.
Now if I can do it once again, even though it has been 25 seconds, this specific pod is in the non
ready state.

--- Understanding DaemonSets ---

Hey, everyone.

a pod in every worker node.
So this can be better explained with a diagram where you have three worker node over here and you want
to run a single copy of a pod.
Let's assume web server in every node over here.
Now, if I have to ask you this question, what would be your outright answer?
So the common answer that you will see is create a deployment with a replica set of three, which will
go ahead and launch three pods, mostly in each and every node.
So this is the ideal case, but this can not always be true.
It can happen in replica set and deployments that two copy or even three copy of a pod can be launched
in a single node.
So it all depends upon a lot of factors based on which the scheduling happens.
So with the typical use case, we cannot directly make use of replica sets and assume that scheduler
will launch a single copy of pod in every node.
So in order to achieve that you can make use of demon set.
Now a demon set basically ensures that all nodes run a copy of your pod.
Now one of the question that comes is why do you need to run just a single copy of a pod?
Why can't I run just two copy of a pod in a single node?
And what would be the difference if I do not run a pod in a single node here?

scans for all the files within the node to verify if it has some malicious data or not.

want to run multiple copies of your antivirus pod.

--- Taints and Tolerations ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
So now in today's video, we'll be discussing about taints and tolerations.
Now, taints are generally used to repel the pods from a specific nodes.
So let's understand this with a simple example.
Now, on the left hand side, you have a worker node zero one and you have the worker node two.
And along with that you have pod one and you have pod two.
Now when you apply a taint, so currently you see that there is a taint applied.
So you can consider this to be a boundary.
So there is a taint applied to the worker node one.
So now whenever there is a taint applied to a specific node and if a pod is scheduled within that specific
node by default, it will be blocked.
So if a scheduler tries to schedule a pod on a node which has been tainted, then the effect would be
blocked.
However, if you look into the worker node to the worker, Node two does not have that taint.
And this is the reason why if the same pod is being scheduled to the worker node two, it will be scheduled
in that specific node.
So this pod will be running in the worker node two.
Now the question that comes is how can you go ahead and schedule a pod in a worker node which has a
taint applied to it?
So the answer to that is tolerations.
So in order to enter the taint worker node, you need a special pass.
All right.
So I call it as special pass.
And this special pass is called as the Toleration.
So let's take the same scenario where you have a taint applied to a worker node.
The pod is trying to schedule itself to this specific worker node, and it has also brought its special
pass.
And since it has this special pass, this pod will be allowed to be schedule within this worker Node
one.
So now this pod can go ahead and run in the worker node one along with that.
One important part to remember is that pod can also run in worker node two if it is scheduled over there,
because worker node two does not really have the taint.
So let's go ahead and do a cube.
Ctl get nodes and there are two worker nodes which are currently available.
One is called as the Worker zero one and second is called as the worker zero two.

is cube worker zero one.

All right, so now this specific node is tainted.
Now we'll be discussing about this effect in the later videos, but just understand that what we have
done is we have painted a specific worker node zero one.
So if you do the Cube Ctl describe once again within the paint over here, you see you have a taint
which is applied to this specific worker.
Zero one So coming back to the scenario that we were discussing, that as soon as you add a taint to
the worker node, if a pod gets scheduled over here, it will be blocked.
So let's try that out.

So this is very similar that if a pod has a path, which is the toleration, not only it can run in

--- Requests and Limits in Kubernetes ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will be discussing about the resource requests and limits.
Now, if you schedule a large application in a node which has a limited resource, so by limited resource,
it can mean limited memory, limited CPU, then it will lead to a out of memory exceptions or others,
and ultimately it can also lead to a downtime.
So let's understand this.
So you have a scheduler and you have three nodes which are available Now, if you look the size of the
nodes are different here.
So the node one has a smaller size, Node two is medium and the Node three is the largest among all
of them.
And you have this app pod which you want to schedule.
Now, if you go ahead and run this app pod, then it can happen that your scheduler might assign that
app pod to the Node zero one, which was empty.
All right.
So if you look here, this node zero one is empty.
However, Node two and Node three are filled so it can happen that the app pod is assigned to Node zero
one.
And since app Pod requires much more higher resource than what Node one offers, it can lead to a downtime
over here.
All right.
So here within the first point, when I mean large application, that basically means the application
which needs huge amount of resources.
So in order to overcome this, you can manually specify that this specific pod requires, let's say
512 MB of Ram to run, and occasionally it might need a maximum of one GB of Ram.
So once your scheduler knows that this specific pod needs 512 MB of guaranteed Ram, then it might not
assign the node zero one because it can happen that the node zero one does not have that much amount
of memory.
Instead it can assign it to the Node zero three, which has that amount of sufficient Ram capacity which
is available.
So all of these can be achieved with the help of requests and limits.
So a request and limits are two ways in which we can control the amount of resource that can be assigned
to a given pod.
So a resource like CPU and memory is what is being considered here.
So there are two things.
One is request and second is limits.
Request basically means that a pod is guaranteed to get a specific amount of Ram or CPU which is defined
and you have limits.
Limits basically limits the amount of resources which a specific port can take.
So it makes sure that a container does not take a node resource above a specific value.
So this can be better understood with the diagram here where you have a green section which is associated
with the request.
So within the request you have 256 MB of Ram.
So when you define a request within your pod spec, then your scheduler will assign your pod to a node
which has 256 MB of Ram, which is available, which it can guarantee to a pod.
And you also have a limit here and within the limit you have specified 384 MB of Ram.
That basically means that Pod will be assigned a maximum of 384 MB of Ram.
It cannot take Ram more higher than 384 because what happens many times is that if the application gets
rogue, it takes huge amount of Ram and CPU, which is unexpected, and during that time you might want
to restart your application.
So limit basically helps in achieving a specific limit for your pod so that it does not affect the node
resource and the other pods.
So that's the high level overview about requests and limits.
So let's do one thing.
Let's jump into practical and understand more about it.
Now I'm going to add editor and we have a file of request hyphen limits dot yaml.
Now this is a simple template based on pod, so I hope you already know what this is.
Now the new thing here is the resources section where we are specifying a request and we are specifying
a limit.
All right.
So I hope you understood.
We have a request section and we have a limit section.
Now, within the request section over here we are specifying a memory of 64 and the CPU of 0.5 over
here.
And within the limits we are specifying a memory of 128 and the CPU of one.
So that basically means a single core CPU at a high level overview.
All right.
So this is a simple definition.
So now let's go ahead and run this.
So within the terminal, let's do a cube.
Ctl apply on the request hyphen limit dot yaml so it has created our pod and if you do a cube Ctl get
pods on output of why this pod is being assigned to one of the nodes over here.
So what scheduler does is that it looks into the request section over here and it checks about the memory
and CPU and it will schedule it to a node which has these specs which are available and where it can
guarantee this much amount of memory and this much amount of CPU to a given container.
Now let me show you a few interesting things here.
So when you do a cube, Ctl get nodes.
So you have three nodes which are available.
Let's go ahead and do a cube.
Ctl describe Node.
And let's add a node name over here.
Now, if you look into this specific section here, you can see that we have a labs hyphen pod which
is available.
And for all the pods which are running on that specific node, you have a section of CPU request, CPU
limits, memory request and memory limits.
So by now I hope you understood what this means.
Now we also have a pod called as CPU Labs Pod which is currently running on this node.
And if you look into the CPU request, it is 500m or 50%.
So we had defined 0.5.
So all of these nodes are basically based on a single core.
So 0.5 basically means 50%.
The CPU limit is one, which basically means 100%.
So a specific container can take 100% of a CPU of a single core.
Then you have a memory request, which is 64 MB and that constitutes to be 4% of the total host memory.
And the memory limit here is 128%.
So that basically means that my container cannot take a memory much more higher than 128, which is
the 8% of the total memory over here.
All right.
So now if you look into the different pods here, all of the pods have a different CPU request and memory
request and memory limits which are currently available.
So this constitutes to be a great factor in scheduling your pod on a specific node.
So I hope you understood at a high level overview what requests and limits are now in production.
It is considered as a best practice to manually specify requests and limits specifically to the pods,
which are going to take huge amount of memory and huge amount of CPU and also make use of a pod affinity
and pod anti-affinity to schedule your other pods to not run on the same node where a pod with a larger
resource is running.
So a few important pointers to remember that Kubernetes scheduler decides the idle node to run the pod
depending upon the request and limits.
Now if your pod requires, let's say, eight GB of Ram, however, there are no nodes within your cluster
which has eight GB of Ram, then your pod will never get scheduled over here.
So you have three approaches.
One is guaranteed.
Guaranteed is when let's say that you have a request and limits are equal.
So this is one of the approaches.
So guaranteed basically means that let's say you have a request which is specifying 64 MB of Ram.
So that is a guaranteed resource which will be provided by the scheduler, provided that a node is available.
Burstable basically is when your limits and your requests are not equal and best efforts is where you
do not really specify anything off requests and limits.
So let's do one thing.
Let's quickly look into the second point in the practical aspect so that we understand it.
So let's do one thing.
Let's go ahead and change the guaranteed memory to six GB of Ram.
Now currently we do not have any node which has six GB of Ram which is available.
So let's look into how scheduler will handle it.
Now before we do that, let's go ahead and delete the earlier pod which was created.

Now, this will fail.
So currently you see it gave an error saying that invalid value your requested should be greater or
equal to your memory limit.
Now what you have done here is that you have said that you need a guarantee of, let's say six GB.
However, you have limited it to 128.
So that's the reason why it's failing.
So let's change the limit to 12 GB.
Approx here and let's go ahead and apply your Yaml file.
Now if you do a kubectl get pods, currently you see that you have the labs hyphen pod and the pod is
within the pending status.
Now if you quickly do a kubectl describe pod.
Let's specify the port name.
And within the events here it says failed scheduling and it says zero out of three nodes are available
and it says insufficient resources.
All right.
So I hope you understood more in detail about requests and limits.
Now, one important part to remember is that your scheduler will schedule based on your request.
It will not really look into the limits.
Limit is something like a best effort.
However, request is the consideration based on which your pod will be scheduled to a given node.
So let's quickly do one thing.
Let's go ahead and delete the changes.
Now, as we were discussing that, your scheduler basically looks into this specific section.
It does not really look into this specific section of limits while scheduling.
So since your scheduler needs to guarantee that this much amount of memory and CPU are available, this
is the primary consideration.
Limits are taken into consideration, but not while selecting a specific node.
So now let's do one thing.

It find it found out that there are hosts which are available, which has 64 MB of Ram available as
well as the CPU and it scheduled here.
So although the limits here was 12 GB, it was taken into consideration in the aspect that your container
will not be able to take a memory and CPU consumption more higher than what is specified within the
limits.
However, for the scheduling aspect is concerned, the request is taken into the primary considerations.
So that's the high level overview about the resource requests and resource limits.

Now, one important part to remember is that the backup command doesn't really cause downtime for DDR,
so you don't really have to take down DDR before you take the backup so you can go ahead and take frequent
backups without affecting your users.
Now, in order to perform the backup, you need to run a specific command.
So this is the command that we need to run.
So basically if you see it's Docker run, then you have you have hyphen, I you have hyphen hyphen.
RM Then you have Docker slash DDR.
So this is the image and we are using the backup command here.
We are specifying the URL.

Then we are specifying the UCP insecure TLS.
Now this is important because we don't really have a trusted CA here.
Then we are specifying the UCP username, which is the admin, the UCP password.
You can supply your password here and then we are sending it to a file called as backup.
So this is where the contents of the backup would be stored.
So let's go ahead and run this command and see on what exactly happens.
So this is my CLI and basically I have pasted the command here.

Great.
So now you see it says that the backup is now completed.
Now, if you quickly do an ls hyphen L, you will see that there is one file called as backup.tr.
Now if you do a do hyphen on backup dot tar to see the size.
The size is just 2.6 MB.
Now the question is how come?
So basically if you go to TR within my repository, you will see that there are multiple images over
here.
One image has a size of 67 MB second image has a size of 61 MB.
However, the backup which was taken is only of size 2.6 MB.
So let's go ahead and understand on what exactly this command does.
Now the backup command only creates the backup of the configurations and the image metadata.
Now it does not really backup the users and the organizations.
So if you want to backup the users and organizations, you can take the backup.
Now, it also does not really backup the Docker images, which is stored within your registry.
So this is important part to remember because in exams you might get a question where they'll show you
that an administrator has run this command.
So what exactly will be backed up or what exactly will not be backed up?
So I hope you know, on what exactly will not be backed up.
So any users or any organizations or any images which are part of your data will not be backed up.
Now, let me quickly show you one part here.

Now within the Detr, if you go to user, you will see that the same user is appearing over here and
this is the reason why it is recommended that anytime if you want to take a backup of users and organizations,
you can directly take that backup from the UCP.
You don't really have to take it from the Detr, and this is the reason why the backup command does
not really backup the users and the organizations over here.
So that's the high level overview about the backup.
