--- Overview of Swarm Routing Mesh ---

You.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the routing mesh in Swarm.
Now, we already know that there can be multiple nodes participating within a swarm cluster.
So when we create a service, the task can be assigned to any of the nodes depending on various factors
like replica sets, labels and other constraints.
Now, routing mesh enables each node in the swarm to accept the connection on the published ports for
any service running in the swarm, even if there is no task running on the node.
Now this is very important.
So basically similar concept you will see even in Kubernetes.
So if you understand the routing mesh here, while you are learning Kubernetes, this will become much
more simpler.
So let's go ahead and understand what exactly the third point mean.
So here you see that you have two containers which are running in server, one in server two, and there
is no container which is running in server three, which is the node three.
So this container is basically listening on port 80.
So this is inside the container.
However, on the server side it is listening on port 8080.
So any request which is being made to port 8080 of of the node one will be redirected to port 80 of
the container.
So what routing mesh really does is that.
So here in Node three you see that there is no container which is running.
So even if there is no container which is running and if there is a request which is made on the port
8080 of the Node three, the request will automatically be redirected towards the node where the container
is actively running.
So this is what the routing mesh is all about.
Routing mesh basically it does not really need that.
The container should be running all the time, even if the container is not running and even if the
request is being made to the node where the container is not present, the node will automatically send
the request to other nodes where that container is active.
So let me quickly give you a demo on how exactly it might look like.
So this is my swarm node, so if I can quickly do a Docker node LS, you will see that there are two
nodes within the swarm and this specific node which ends with the IP of 211.
So if you see this is 211.
So this is acting as a master node and the other one acts as a worker node.
So if I can quickly show you.
So this is master and this is the worker node.
Great.
So now if I can quickly do a Docker service.
LS you will see that I have one service which is created here and if you do a Docker service PS on my
web, you will see that this service basically has one container which is up and running and this container
is created on the server which has the IP address of 211, which is the present server.
So if I do a Docker PS here, you will see that I have one container up and running now within the Docker
service.
LS command Now in fact let me clear the screen.
So within the Docker service LS command, you will see that I have port 8080, which is mapped to the
port 80 of the container.
So this container so this specific container is running Nginx.
So now what happens is that any request which is made to port 8080 will be redirected to port 80 inside
the container.
This is something that I'm sure that everyone would know by now.
So if I do a curl on 127.0.0.1 on 8080 you will see that you are able to see the Nginx default page.
Now this works perfectly because this request is currently made on the node where the container is running.
However, from the worker node if I do a docker PS here you see that there are no containers which are
running.
And even from here, if I do 127.0.0.1 on 8080 you will see that you are still able to see the Nginx
default.
Welcome page.
So now from this diagram, we came to know that one node had the container running.
One node did not have the container running.
So even in the node, which did not have the container running, when we made a request to port 8080,
that request was redirected to the node where the container was running and the response was served.
And this is what the routing mesh is all about.
So that's the high level overview about the routing mesh in Swarm.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Implementing Swarm Routing Mesh ---

Huron.
And welcome back.
Now, in the earlier video we discussed about the routing mesh in Swarm.
So in today's video, we will look into how exactly we can go ahead and create it.
Now, for our demo purposes, I have two servers which are created and I have the communication on all
ports which are open between both the servers over here.
Now the first server which has the IP ending with 211 acts as a swarm master node and the other one
acts as a worker node.
So if I do a Docker node LS, you will see that there are two servers.
One is acting as the master, second is acting as a worker.
So now let's go ahead and create a service.
So I'll say Docker service create, I'll give it a name of web server.

--- DTR - High Availability ---

Hey, Ryan.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about the high availability aspect of DDR.
Now the Docker Trusted Registry is designed to scale horizontally as your usage increases so you can
add more replicas to your DDR to scale to your demand as well as for the high availability.
Now, this can be better explained with this diagram where there are three worker nodes where the UCP
is configured.
Now on all the three worker nodes, you also have the DDR and there is also an overlay network for the
communication between all of the DDR replicas that are configured for high availability setup.
Now also note that all the DDR replicas runs on the same set of services and changes to their configurations
are automatically propagated to other replicas.
So any change in terms of configuration that you do, they are also automatically propagated.
Now one of the very important part to remember here is that in a single DDR based deployment, you can
make use of a local file system to store your Docker images.
However, whenever you have multiple DDR replicas similar to what we see in this specific diagram for
the high availability, you will need to ensure that all replicas are using the same storage backend.
And this is the reason why if you see within this diagram, you have a common object storage where all
the three DDR replicas will be referring to.
So this common object storage can be S3 or it can be Azure and others.

--- DTR - Caching ---

Hayden.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we'll be discussing about the DDR cache at a very high level overview.
Now, before we go ahead and discuss more in detail about the DDR cache, let's quickly look into a
typical challenge post which will understand more about it.
So this diagram is an easy way to understand a challenge.
So on the left hand side, you have the DDR cluster.
So this cluster is running in the US region.
Now within this cluster you have a repository of website slash UI, and within the repository there
are multiple tags that are available associated with the Docker images.
Now let's also assume that the organization has the Asia Office where a lot of users are working now
for the users to pull the images from the DDR they have, they can do a manual docker pull.
However, since there is a great difference between us and Asia in terms of distance, the overall operations
like Docker Pull, Docker push will take a lot of time, so this network can be a huge bottleneck.
So the farther away you are from the geographical location where the DDR is deployed, the longer it
will take for you to pull and push images.
So this primarily happens because the file that are being transferred from the DDR to your machine needs
to travel a longer distance across multiple networks on the line.
So in order to overcome this, one can make use of the DDR cache.
So what happens in the DDR cache is that you have a cache that you configure at the location where you
know that users will be doing a Docker pull and Docker push.
And in the back end you can go ahead and configure the images that are part of your primary cluster
to be cached into the clusters that are configured at the Asia office.
So next time when a user wants to do a Docker pull, the images can be fetched from this cache location
and this will be much more faster.
Now remember that the cache are transparent to users since user still needs to log in to pull the images
making use of the DDR URL address.
So basically what will happen is once the authentication happens, the DDR primary cluster will automatically
redirect to the appropriate cache which is running near them.
Now, depending upon the architecture, you can go ahead and configure the cache location accordingly.
So here you have a primary DDR cluster in US region.
However, you have to DDR cache that are available.
One is in the Asia region and second in the Europe region.
Now at a high level overview, also understand the architecture on how exactly this would look like.
So on the primary data center, you have the DDR that are running now.
They are making use of a shared storage.
So this is basically the US data center.
Now you also have the Asia Data Center where you have configured the DDR cache over here, which will
basically pull the data from upstream.
Now the architecture is very similar to the primary data center.
However, one important part that you need to know is that in the secondary node, which is acting as
a cache, you do not really need a UCP agent here.
This is very important part to remember.
UCP agent is not really required on the server, which is acting as a cache, so that's the high level
overview about DDR caching.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Setting Orchestrator in UCP ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we'll be discussing about setting up an orchestrator in UCP.
Now, when it comes to the orchestration, Docker, UCP supports both Docker Swarm as well as Kubernetes.
Now, whenever you add a node to a UCP cluster, the nodes workload are managed by default orchestrator,
so it can be Docker Swarm as well as Kubernetes here.
Now when you install Docker enterprise, new nodes are managed by Docker Swarm, but you can change
this specific default orchestrator to Kubernetes from the administrator settings.
So if you see under the scheduler, there are two options that are available.
One is for Swarm and second is for Kubernetes.
So you can use one among them.
The default one is the Docker swarm.
Now for the nodes that are already joined to the UCP, you can also go ahead and change the current
orchestrator for them.
Now the available orchestrator types are Kubernetes Swarm as well as mixed Mix basically enables workload
to be scheduled by both the Kubernetes and Swarm orchestrator engines within the same node.
Now here also remember that whenever you change the orchestrator type for a node, existing workload
will be evicted and they are not migrated to a new orchestrator automatically.
So this is one important part to remember.
So let's do one thing.
Let's quickly go to the UCP and understand how exactly this might look like.
Now I'm in the UCP console, so from the admin, if you quickly go to the admin settings and under the
orchestration tab here you see under the scheduler you can make use of either Swarm or Kubernetes,
whichever you intend to do.
So this is one way.
Now you can also set a specific orchestrator at a node level.
So under the shared resource, if you go to nodes currently we have one node available.
Let's open up that worker node.
And if you edit this node under the orchestrator type, you see you have Swarm Kubernetes and mixed
similar to what we were discussing here.
So available orchestrator type Kubernetes swarm and mixed.
So if you have mixed, that basically means that the node can be used both for Swarm as well as for
Kubernetes.
So here also remember that using a mixed orchestrator type is not recommended for production use.
So in case if you are running it in production, make sure to either use Swarm or use Kubernetes.
Never use mix approach over here.
Now apart from that, also remember that we can also change the orchestrator type of a given node from
swarm to Kubernetes via CLI.
So the CLI command looks something similar to this.
So you have Docker node update, you're adding a label which is com Docker, UCP orchestrator.
Kubernetes is equal to true, followed by the given worker node ID and post which the node will be used
for only Kubernetes specific workloads.
So that's the high level overview about the orchestrator type settings in UCP.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Overview of Container Security Scanning ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we'll be discussing about the container security scanning.
In fact, this is one of my favorite features in DDR, or I would say in terms of container security.
This is one of the very important features that you should be implementing within your organization
if you're working into the security or similar role.
Now, Docker containers can have various security vulnerabilities.
Now if you just blindly pull the container and just run it within the production environment, and if
that container has some high level security vulnerabilities which can be exploited, then your website
or whatever web application that you're running, it can be breached and hence it is very important
for you to scan your containers for security vulnerabilities.
Now, DDR allows us to perform the security scan for the containers which you push to the DDR.
Now these scan can be performed in the on push model or even manually.
So let's say that someone is pushing a specific container image to your DDR.
Then the DDR can automatically scan that image for vulnerability.
Or if someone has already pushed it, you can even scan it manually.
So if you look into this image over here, there are two container images here.
One has vulnerabilities, status as clean, and second has the vulnerability status where there are
five critical vulnerability 31 major and 18 minor.
So having the container scanning functionality is extremely important for your organization if you're
implementing containers in your production environments.
So let's quickly have a demo on how exactly this might look like in DDR.
So this is my Docker trusted registry.
And in fact the image that I have added here, this is the one that I had taken from the DDR.
Now if you see over here that there are two images here.
One has the image tag as version one and the second has the image tag of Ubuntu.
Now the version one does not really have any vulnerability and hence it is shown as clean.
Now you also have ubuntu.
Ubuntu is been detected with a lot of vulnerabilities.
Now if you want to see what are the type of vulnerabilities you can click on view details here and it
will show you the layer wise vulnerability associated with the container.
So these are all the vulnerabilities which are associated.
Now if you just click on one of the vulnerabilities here, you will get more information related to
what this vulnerability is.
You can even see what are the layers which are being affected by a specific type of vulnerability.
Now, if you just click on the CVE ID here, you will be redirected to the page, which gives you a
lot of details related to that specific vulnerability which is detected.
So as we were discussing, that, Docker trusted registry basically has two ways in which you can scan
for container vulnerabilities.
So if you go into the security here, you will see that I have enabled scanning over here and basically
you have to make sure that your scanning database is in sync with the online database, whatever it
might be.
So that's the high level overview about container scanning.
Now do remember that the container security scanning is a feature.
It is not a proprietary of Docker trusted registry.
So you can use various other solutions which has the capability for container security scanning.
You don't always have to rely on the DDR, but as far as the certification exam is concerned, your
knowledge about DDR is important.
So that's about it for this video.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Overview of LDAP ---

Hi, everyone, and welcome back to the Knowledge Portal Video series.
So in the previous lecture we spoke about delegation and how a user from one account can do a assume
role to the second account.
So that is what delegation was all about.
Today we will look into very similar concept called as Federation.
Again, this is a very, very important concept and federation is generally used in most of the enterprises
based companies.
So let's understand what Federation is with a very simple use case.
So let's assume that there are 500 users within an organization and your organization are using three
services.
So one, you have Jenkins.
Jenkins is generally used for CI CD.
Second is AWS and the third is HR management system.
Now as a solutions architect and a system administrator, you have been assigned a role to give users
access to all of these three services.
So now the question is there are 500 users and you need to give access for the 500 users to all of these
three services.
So how will you go ahead and approach this particular use case?
Now, one very simple way of doing is you add 500 users first in Jenkins, then you add 500 users in
AWS via Iam, and then you add 500 users in HR management system.
So this is one way.
Now the problem with this kind of approach is that let's assume that tomorrow ten more users join in.
So what you have to do is you have to add ten users here in Jenkins then and then in HR management,
which is very clumsy.
It takes a lot of time and it is not an ideal way of doing this.
So this is again a hard work.
And for lazy people out there like me, this is not a very ideal approach.
So what we can do over here is we can have a central directory.
So here we have a Ldap in Ldap.
All the users will be stored.
So administrator has to store the user only in Ldap.
Once the users are stored in Ldap, depending upon what are the federated settings that you have done,
user will be able to log in to Jenkins and HR Management system directly.
So no need to create user in Jenkins or or HR management system.
Just create a user in Ldap, establish a trust relationship between Ldap and all three services and
then user will be able to seamlessly connect to all of these three services.
And if you see, it becomes very simple.
So now let's say that tomorrow ten users come to your organization.
All you have to do is you have to add all of those users in the Ldap directory and it will do the job
for you.
So this is a very simple way and most efficient way of doing this, because generally if you look into
a critical organization like organization who are following Pci-dss standards, you need to immediately
revoke the access once they leave the organization, like once they are either resigned or terminated.
So again, you going to each and every service and disabling the account will take time.
Instead of that, if you are using Ldap, all you have to do is just disable the user in the Ldap and
that user will not be able to log in anywhere.
So again, very simple and efficient for using and this is one of the reasons why most of the enterprises
they use Ldap or something similar.
So there are various solutions which can be used for storing central users like Microsoft Active Directory
is something which is very famous in most of the organization.
And as far as Linux is concerned, you have Red Hat Identity management or Free IPA, which does the
similar things.
So let me do one thing.
Let me actually show you the demo of Free IPA on how exactly it looks like.
So I am logged in over here.
This, this is a free IPA and if you see there are a lot of users which are present over here.
So if you go to the different screen, let me actually I think it got logged out.
Let me log in once.
So generally you all you have to do is you have to store the user in this identity store and then you
do a federation from here to your AWS.
So we have a lot of users which are present over here.
You can do something like within the user group, you can create a user group called as Production.
So this is very similar to what we did in the delegation.
So let's create a user group called as Production.
And once you create a user group production, you add the users within this particular group.
So let me just add some random users.
Ldap user five, Ldap user nine and manager, I'll add over here.
Okay, So these are the three users which are created now in the site.
You can create a role which does the federation and you can establish a trust relationship like only
the users which are present within the production group can log in to the production role which is present
within your account.
So this is one of the basic on how you can go ahead with Federation.
So this is a very small chapter about federation.
We will go ahead and understand Federation in great detail in the upcoming lecture.
So I hope this has been useful for you.
And again, if you have any doubts, feel free to connect at instructors, at labs or connect to us
on social media.
Thanks for watching.

--- Linux Namespaces ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about Linux namespaces.
Now Docker extensively makes use of the Linux namespaces and hence at a high level overview, It is
important for us to understand what Linux namespaces are and how Docker makes use of those namespaces.
Now, a Docker uses a technology called as namespace to provide the isolated workspace called as the
container.
Now the namespace provides a layer of isolation and each aspect of the container runs in a separate
namespace.
So let's understand this.
So let's say you have two namespaces which are available over here and this namespace has a different
components which are available.
So in this namespace you have a separate component and a component in the namespace B also you have
a separate PID and a separate component.
Now at this stage it becomes a little confusing.
So let's quickly go to the practical and understand what this is.
So I'm in my CLI.
Let's quickly do one thing.
Let's quickly create a new container.
I'll say Docker container run, let's call it as my BusyBox.
I'll start it from the BusyBox image.
Great.
So now if you do a Docker PS, you should see that we have one container which is up and running.
Now from the host, if I do a PS hyphen f, you will see that there are a lot of processes which are
running over here.
Now within the host, the first process would typically be the systemd.
So in the previous version it is the init subsystem which runs as the PID one.
All right.
So these are the amount of processes which are running.
Now when you do a Docker, PS, let's quickly connect to the container.
It seems I gave the name of my burst box.
Anyways, so currently I am connected to the container and from the container if I do a hyphen f command
you will see that it is only showing you three processes.
And that too there is a process id of one and you have the SSH command which is running.
So now the question is how come the container is not able to see the processes which are running in
the host?
How come the container is not able to see that?
And that is precisely what the namespace does.
Now if you basically look here, let's quickly log into the container yet again.
So if you look over here within the container, the command is running as a PID one.
So this mapping is inside the container only.
However, if you do PS hyphen F here, you will see that outside of the container it is running with
a different PID altogether.
Now it cannot really have a PID of one in the host.
So all of those isolation that happens behind the scenes is the feature of Linux namespaces.
Now this is what Linux namespace is.
Now there are various other components to the Linux namespace.
Now the component that we are discussing here is the PID.
So Linux namespace also has various other components like UTS, like IPC and various others.
So PID.
I hope at a high level overview you understood on what this is all about.
So it basically allows you to have a separation in terms of process so that the containers or whatever
container which is running here, it cannot really see any other processes which are running within
the same operating system.
So by any chance, if this specific container has to be breached, so let's say this container is hacked,
so attacker will not be able to connect or not be able to know the wider picture on what exactly is
running.
So let's come out of this and let's type one command.
I'll say Unshare, say Mount Proc and I'll call it as bin Bash.
Now, again, in order to have a namespace, you don't really need a docker.
So Docker makes use of the feature of namespace which is available in the Linux environment.
So let's do it with Unshare.
So when I did a Unshare, so we are giving a P, so P is basically the PID component of the namespace
which the Linux provides.
So again, within here, if I do a PS hyphen f you will see that you only have two processes which are
being listed over here.
So this is also a namespace kind of a environment.
So this is not something specific to Docker.
This is a Linux feature which the Docker makes use of.
Now let's take one more example.
So within here I have a host name which is Docker hyphen demo.
So you also have a namespace of UTS which basically allows you to set hostname as well.
So I can say uncheck, let's say hyphen UTS and we'll say bin bash.
All right, so as expected, within this namespace you have a Docker hyphen demo, however you can change
the host name over here.
I can say it as my namespace.
All right, so whatever host name that you have added inside a specific namespace is associated with
only that namespace.
It is not associated with the underlying host.
All right.
So now from the host, if I do a host name, you will see the host name still remains to be Docker hyphen
demo.
Same goes when you connect to the container.
And if you type a host name, you see the container has a different host name.
However, when you come out of the container and you see the host name over here, the underlying host
has a different host name altogether.
So if you are running ten containers, ten containers can have its own host name.
Ten containers will have its own namespace in terms of process.
In terms of.
Network in terms of et cetera.
So speaking about namespaces which are available in Linux, as of now, there are six different types
of namespace which are available.
One is the IPC, which is the Inter-process communication.
Second is the PID, third is the net, which is the network, fourth is the user ID, fifth is mount
and sixth is UTS.
So at a high level overview, you understood what PID does as well as what UTS does.
Now, as far as the certification is concerned, it is not really required to go in depth about each
one of them, but you should be knowing that Docker makes use of namespaces and at a high level overview
what namespaces are and how exactly they might work at a very high level overview.

--- Control Groups (cgroups) ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the control groups.
Now, control groups, which are also referred as the C groups, is a Linux kernel feature that limits
accounts for and isolates the resource usage like CPU, memory disk, IO network, etcetera for a collection
of processes which are running within the Linux system.
So this can be better understood with a diagram here.
So let's say you have a Docker host and within the Docker host you have two containers which are running.
You have container A and you have container B.
Now this specific Docker host has one core CPU and it has one GB of Ram.
Now both the containers which are running over here, you see they are green, so they are running perfectly
and everyone is happy over here.
Now the problem that happens is let's assume that container a suddenly started to take huge amount of
resources.
It might be due to some kind of a denial of service attack or some kind of a poor implementation of
the application which is running inside the container.
Due to some reason, the container A has suddenly started to take huge amount of CPU as well as Ram.
So now what happens here is that the other containers will not really get appropriate amount of CPU
and Ram and so go with the processes which are running on the Docker.
Host So this is little dangerous and this is the reason why it is very important that we control the
amount of CPU, we control the amount of Ram that we assign to the container so that even though the
container acts rogue or even though the container is attacked, it cannot really take more amount of
CPU or more amount of Ram then what we have assigned to it now.
Do remember that control groups by itself is a Linux kernel feature.
Now this is very similar to the Linux namespaces, so Docker makes use of control groups to control
or to limit the ram and CPU that can be assigned.
Now control group can not only limit by Ram and CPU, it can do a lot like disk IO network etcetera.
However, Docker does not support each and every implementation of what control group support, so the
primary ones are the CPU and memory.
So let's go ahead and look into how we can achieve this.
So I'm in my Docker.
Host Let's quickly do a docker.
PS Currently there are no containers which are running, so if we quickly do a Docker run hyphen hyphen
help, let's go a bit down.
You will see that there is a option for memory and we can specify the memory limit over here.
So this is one of the options which if you specify the container, will have the limited memory based
on whatever limit that you might want to specify.
So let's try this out.
So I'll quickly do a Docker container run, let's call it as my memory.
Now I'll specify hyphen M and you can specify how much memory that you want to allocate.
So in my case I'll put 256 M and I'll start it from the BusyBox container.
All right, so now if you do a Docker, PS, you will see that we have one container which has been
created.
So let's do one thing before we go inside the container.
Let's look into the amount of memory which the Docker host has.
So it has the memory of one GB.
So currently the total is 991 MB.
Great.
So now let's do a Docker PS and let's connect to the Docker container.
All right.
Now, within the container, if you do a three hyphen m, you will be able to see the exact same contents
over here, which is 991 MB.
Now, this is a problem.
The problem is because although the containers memory here it is with the help of free hyphen M or even
if you do a top.
So even with top or free hyphen M you will be able to see the memory as one.
GB So this one GB is not true because the container has the maximum limit of 256 MB.
So if there is a application which is running inside the container and let's say that the application
detects that this container has one GB of Ram, then maybe it might try to create a lot of other child
processes to work more efficiently.
However, in reality this container has a memory limit of only 256 MB of Ram.
So if you make use of this conventional tools, it will create a problem.
Now basically this tools gets the information from the Proc meminfo.
So this is where the tools like top or free basically they get the information from.
Now the problem is these tools that were designed, they were designed way before Cgroups was really
implemented in Linux.
So they are also referred as the tools which are not really cgroup aware.
And this is the reason why the content that they'll show will not always be accurate as far as the cgroup
is concerned.
So the ideal way you can do a quick cat on sysfs cgroup you can go to memory and here there are a lot
of options.
Let's look into the memory limit over here so this can give us a proper output.
So if you do a memory limit in bytes, so this will show you in bytes and you see it is basically showing
you 256 MB of Ram.
So this is basically in bytes and this is the reason why you are getting a long output.
So this type of architecture is the right way because if you do not basically fetch the contents from
the cgroup and memory limit in bytes, you will not get a very accurate information.
This is very important part to remember.
So let's go back to the host and let's do a Docker run hyphen hyphen help.
And if you go a bit up and here you will see that there are even for CPU, there are a lot of options
which are available.
So you have CPU period end which limits the CPU and there are various other.
So maybe you can go ahead and explore these.
But at a high level overview, I hope you understood what the Linux control groups are all about and
how we can limit the amount of memory that we can assign to a specific container.
Along with that, I also hope you understood the limitations of tools like Free that they are not a

--- Reservation vs Limits ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we will be discussing about the resource limitation factors in Docker containers.
And meanwhile, we'll also discuss about the difference between reservation versus limit.
Now, by default, a container has no resource constraint and it can use as much resource that is available
within a specific host.
Now generally this can lead to a problem because many times what happens is that within production environment
you have multiple containers which are running in a single host.
And if one container due to some reason is taking a huge amount of Ram or it is taking a huge amount
of CPU, that means that the other containers which might belong to a different application will not
have a good amount of CPU and Ram due to which it will function slowly.
So this is the reason why it is important to not allow container to consume too much amount of memory
as well as CPU.
Now on a linux host, if a kernel detects that there is not enough amount of memory which is available
to perform certain amount of critical functions, then it will throw an OAM which is also referred as
out of memory exception and then kernel will automatically start to kill the process, which is taking
a good amount of memory because for example, let's say that you have one GB of Ram for your server
and you have one application which is taking a huge amount of Ram.
Now kernel does not really have good amount of Ram which is required to have the operating system to
be running efficiently.
So now what it will do is kernel will throw an out of memory exception and it will kill or it will start
to kill the processes which are taking a good amount of memory.
And once the process is killed then you will see that there is an exception within the log files.
Now in terms of resource limitation, there are two pointers that you need to remember.
One is the reservation and second is the limit.
Now limit imposes an upper limit to the amount of memory that can potentially be used by a Docker container.
Now reservation, on the other hand, is less rigid.
So if you are aware about the Linux terminology, limit is like a hard limit and reservation is a soft
limit.
We'll be discussing about this in practical as well.
So basically whenever the system is running low on memory and there is a contention, so reservation
tries to bring the container's memory consumption at or below the reservation limit, which has been
set.
So let's go ahead and understand both of these things practically.
So I'm in my CLI and let's quickly do a Docker container run hyphen hyphen, help.
Now let's go a bit up and here you see there are two important options over here.
One is the memory limit.
So if you look into the description, you have the memory limit over here and second is the memory soft
limit.
Now if you associate memory soft limit, it is associated with the memory reservation over here.
And memory limit is not associated with anything, it's just associated with hyphen.
M So let's do one thing.
Let's try with memory Reservation over here.
So let's do a Docker container run, let's give it a name as container zero one and I'll say memory
hyphen reservation, and let's put it to 250 MB All right.
And I'll launch it from the BusyBox container with the shell of SS.
All right, So now if you do a Docker, PS, you have one container which is created.
So basically if I can quickly do a free hyphen.
M This specific server where we are running this command has a total of one GB of memory.
So now let's run the same command multiple times.
Let's do a container zero two as well.
I'll do a container zero three.
Let's do a container zero four and also container zero five.
So basically, we have spawned five containers and each container has a memory reservation of 250 MB.
So now if you do a Docker PS here you have five containers.
Each has a soft limit of 250 MB.
Now do remember that this is a soft limit, so container can go above that as well.
So let's say that this is a container zero one which has a soft limit of 250 MB.
So it is not necessary that application within the container zero one will be restricted to 250 MB.
It can go beyond that as well.
So this 250 MB is a soft limit.
It is not a hard limit.
So when it comes to hard limit.
So let's say you have set up a hard limit of 500 MB.
So in terms of hard limit container cannot go above 500 MB.
So that is the hard limit.
It cannot go above that.
Now in terms of soft limit, container can go above soft limit as well.
So that's the difference between a hard limit and the soft limit.
Now you can specify the hard limit as well with the hyphen M option.
So when you do a hyphen M option, you can say 500 M Oops, let's change the container name here.
Alright, so now if you do a Docker PS here you see you have a container.
Zero six.
Now this container zero six has a hard limit of 500 MB and it has a soft limit of 250 MB.
So that basically means that container cannot go beyond 500 MB of limit.
Now, in case if there is a contention which is happening due to low amount of memory within the system,
then the reservation tries to bring the container's memory consumption at or below the reservation limit.
So let's say that we have a reservation limit of 250 MB and there is a contention of memory which is
happening within the system.
Then Docker will try to bring the container's memory consumption within the reservation limit.
So that's the high level overview about the reservation versus limit.
I hope you understood the difference between both of them.
Reservation is a soft limit container can go beyond the reservation as well.
The limit is a hard limit.
Once you have set the limit, container cannot go beyond what you had set with the limit value.
So that's the high level overview about the reservation and limit.
I hope this video has been informative for you and I look forward to see you in the next video.

--- Swarm MTLS ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the Swarm Mutual.
TLS also referred as the TLS.
Now the nodes, which are part of a swarm, makes use of the mutual transport layer security, also
referred as the TLS to authenticate, authorize and encrypt the communications with the other nodes
which are part of the swarm.
Now, by default, whenever you initialize a swarm, the node in which you initialize the swarm becomes
the manager node, and the manager node generates a new root certificate authority along with a key
pair, which is used to secure the communication with the other nodes which joins the swarm.
Now we'll look into what exactly this is in a moment, but do remember that you can also explicitly
generate your own root key with the help of hyphen hyphen external key flag.
Now to summarize the things that we were discussing.
So each time a node joins the swarm, the manager issues a certificate to that node.
So if you see over here, let's take an example of the manager node and the worker node.
So manager node, basically when you initialize a swarm, there is a root certificate authority.
Now if you see the worker node also has a TLS certificate here.
So the communication that happens between the manager node and the worker node and the communication
that happens between the manager node and the manager node is completely encrypted.
So this is quite useful because if a attacker is trying to see the traffic over here, he will not be
able to do that.
And that is possible with the help of mutual.
TLS So before we proceed further, let me actually show you on what exactly this is and where can you
find the root certificate authority?
So I'm in my CLI and if I can quickly do a Docker node.
LS So basically I only have one node as part of the swarm.
So basically I had done something like Docker swarm in it.
I am sure that you already know on what exactly this does, so once you do a Docker swarm in it, the
node in which you do this command automatically becomes the manager node.
So this is the current manager node.
And as we were discussing by default, if you look into the second point, by default, the manager
node generates a new root certificate authority along with the key pair which are used for secure communications.
So now if you go to var lib Docker and if you go to Swarm, you have a directory called as certificates.
Now within the certificates you see, you have a swarm root, CA certificate and also the swarm node
key.
So this is the certificate which gets created automatically.
However, you can also let's say you do not want to use the default certificates, you want to use your
own certificates.
So you can also do that with the help of hyphen hyphen external CA flag.
Now let's understand more about the token process.
So the manager node we already know, like whenever we initialize the swarm manager node generates two
tokens.
One is for the worker nodes and second is for the manager nodes.
So this can be easily explained with this diagram.
So let's say that this is a manager node.
Now whenever you initialize a swarm, there are two types of tokens which are generated.
One token is for the other manager node to join the cluster and second token is specifically for the
worker node.
So there are two different tokens which are generated now.
Each token includes the digest of the root certificate authority and a randomly generated secret.
This is very important part to remember.
Now to complement this point, whenever a node joins the swarm, the joining node uses the digest to
validate the root CA certificate from the remote manager.
This is important and we'll just look about this in detail in terms of practical in a moment.
So do remember that whatever token that gets generated, whatever worker token that gets generated,
that worker token basically contains the digest is something like a hash of the root.
CA So that basically means that if the root CA changes, let's say there is a attacker and he has modified
this root in the manager, then the digest also changes.
And then if you try to make use of that worker token, it does not work.
We'll just look into that.
So let's look into the rotating the.
It as well.
So basically we can make use of the command Docker swarm k hyphen hyphen rotate to generate a new CA
certificate.
And the key now this can happen because if sometimes if you think that your certificate has been compromised,
you can easily rotate your certificate.
Now do remember that since if you we already discussed here that each token includes the digest of the
root CA and if the certificate changes, then the joint tokens also changes accordingly.
So let's say you have rotated your certificates in the manager node, then you will not be able to make
use of the joint tokens as well.
In the longer term, you will have to make use of the newer joint tokens.
Now one of the question that comes is do I have to update the joint tokens of the existing worker nodes?
And the answer is no, because whenever you run this command, whatever new certificate that is generated,
it is a cross signed certificate which is signed from the previous root.
CA So it's something like let's assume that your you think that your previous root C has been compromised.
So what you do, you quickly do a Docker swarm, CA hyphen hyphen, rotate command so this will go ahead
and rotate a new certificate.
So the new root that gets generated, it is signed from the certificate which was previously rotated
and this signed certificate will only be trusted till the time the worker node also rotates the certificate.
Once the worker node rotates their own certificate, then the swarm will remove the older CA certificate.
Now, again, I'm sure that this is sounding a little confusing, so let's jump into practical so that
we start to understand on what this points really mean.

--- Managing Secrets in Docker Swarm ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we will discuss about managing secrets in Docker Swarm.
Now, Docker secrets basically allows us to centrally manage the secret data and to also securely transmit
those secrets to only the containers that needs access to it.
Now, centrally storing the secrets can be achieved with the help of a lot of solutions, which includes
Hashicorp Vault, it includes secrets, manager, parameter store, etcetera.
However, one great thing about the Docker secrets is that you can transmit that secret only to the
specific containers which are needed now.
Secrets are encrypted during the transit as well as at rest in the Docker swarm.
Now do remember that a given secret is only accessible to those services which have been granted explicit
access to it, and only while those service tasks are running.
So let's do one thing.
Let's quickly jump into practicals and understand on how this might work.
So I'm in my CLI and if we quickly do a docker swarm init here.
So let's quickly initialize our swarm cluster and I'll put it the public IP address of my server.

Or you can also pass the secret from the command line.
But I have a file called as db underscore dot txt and if you quickly open up this file, it basically
contains a random username and a random password.
So let's say that whatever containers that would be started, the applications inside those container
would need to connect to the database and you have to give the username and password of the database
to that application.
Now Hardcoding those database credentials is not the best approach.
So in this case you can make use of the secrets feature.
So in order to create a secret, you can just say Docker secret, create, just give the name, I'll
say DB secrets and pass on the file name where your secrets are stored.
All right, so now if you do a Docker secret ls, you should see that there is one secret which is created.
Now do remember that this secret that is being created, it is completely encrypted.
And if you do something like Docker secret inspect on DB secrets, you will not get the secret out of
this specific inspect command.

access those secrets.

In our case, the name is secret.

--- Docker Content Trust ---

Huron.
And welcome back.
Now in today's video, we will be discussing at a very high level overview about the Docker Content
trust.
Now, when we are downloading the images from network or from the Internet, the integrity becomes a
true concern.
So let's say that there is a registry over the internet and you are trying to download a image from
that specific registry.
Now if there is a middleman who is sitting in between and he has modified the image, then the integrity
changes.
Let's say if he has added some kind of a malicious thing within the image, then if you are running
that image in production, it will hamper your security and hence integrity becomes a true concern.
Whenever you are downloading a image from the network.
Now, the Docker Content Trust gives us the ability to verify both the integrity and the publisher.
Of all the data received from the registry over any channel.
Now, this specific use case can be achieved with the help of digital signatures.
So this is very similar.
If you are aware about how the public key infrastructure works.
This is quite similar to that.
Let me in fact quickly show you.
So currently this is a Docker trusted registry.
Now at the top of the browser, you see it gives a warning here which says that it is not secure.
So the reason why it is not secure is because the certificate with which it is signed, it is not really
trusted by the browser.
So let me in fact show you this.
If I enter this, you see it basically gives you an error saying that this site is not secure.
Basically, it says that there can be a man in the middle attack due to which the contents of the site
can be modified.
Now, this is very similar because this certificate is not trusted.
The website can be modified, your credentials can be leaked.
Similarly, even in Docker, if you are downloading a image and the certificate is not really trusted,
then the integrity can be the issue.
So what really happens is that let's say in a repository there can be multiple tags.
So what you can do is you can sign a specific image tag.
So here you see the latest image tag is signed.
The tag with 1.0 is signed.
However, in the second repository, although the test tag is signed, the production tag is not signed.
So with Docker content trust, what you can say is any tag which is not signed or which is not trusted,
you cannot download that specific image.
Only the tag which is signed can be downloaded.
Now, in case, if you are wondering like how does the browser know whether this is secure or not?

are a lot of root CA which are trusted over here.

Now only the websites which are not signed with the trusted root CA or if you have any custom CA which
you have configured within your browser, then those websites which are not signed with them will get
this specific warning or a specific error.
Now same goes with the signed image.
Any image which are signed will get downloaded perfectly.
Any images which are not signed.

within the Sudoers file, but that is not really recommended because then he'll be able to perform various
operations.

you have a group called as Docker, so you can go ahead and add a user to this specific group.

user does not have any sudo privileges.
So do remember that if you have any unprivileged user who wants to perform certain operations on Docker,
the quick way is to add that user within the Docker group and that should be perfectly fine.
Now there is one more way in which you can do that.
Let's do one thing, let's call it as Docker user here.
Oops, let's add the Docker user.
All right?
And now you can do a user mode, I'll say the Docker group and I'll specify the user, which is Docker
user.
All right.
So if you basically look into the Etsy Group now, you will see that the Docker user is automatically
added.
So among both the approaches, this is the recommended way.

--- Privileged Containers ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about privileged containers.
Now, by default, Docker container does not have many capabilities assigned to it.
Now, during the video of Linux capabilities, we already saw that by default, when you create a container,
container does have certain set of capabilities which gets associated with it.
But there are various other capabilities like Linux immutable and others which does not get assigned
to the containers by default.
Now, along with that, the Docker containers are also not allowed to access any devices which are that
of the host.
Now, due to these reasons, by default, Docker Container cannot perform various use cases like running
Docker container inside Docker container.
Now if you're wondering if this is possible, the answer is yes.
Definitely it is possible, but not when you just run a default Docker container.
If you want to run Docker inside Docker, then your Docker container needs to have various capabilities
assigned to it, including the access of the underlying host devices.
Now the next important concept is privileged containers.
Now privileged containers can access all the devices on the host as well as have the configuration like
AppArmor or SELinux to allow your container to have nearly the same access to the host as a process
running outside the container of the host.
So when you typically run a privileged containers, the Docker will assign all the capabilities to that
Docker container.
Along with that, these host devices, which were not allowed to be accessed from the container, can
be accessed from your privileged containers.
Now, along with that, whatever limits that you might apply, they do not really get followed by the
privileged containers.
So if you see over here, you have a normal container.
Normal container does not really have much of the access.
So there are a lot of restrictions over here.
But in terms of privileged containers, you do have access nearly as same as a process which is run
directly on the host level.
So I made my Linux server and let's do one thing.
Let's go ahead and create a container, let's call it as Amazon Linux.
We'll launch it from an Amazon Linux image and we'll make use of a bash process.

And this is the reason why if you do not really run a privileged container, it is considered to be
much more safer because many of the devices are not directly connected with your Docker container.
So let's come out of it.

--- Overview of Docker Storage Drivers ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about the storage drivers in Docker.
Now, we already know that a Docker image is built up from a series of layers.
Now whenever we create a container from an image, let's say that this is a Ubuntu image and we create
a container from this specific image.
Now after creating a container, when we log in to the container and we run a command, you will be
able to see that multiple files which are spanned across the layers are merged together and they are
shown to you as a single output.
So there has to be something which would do all the merging aspect and various other nifty gifty details
which are required behind the scenes.
So those are basically the storage drivers.
So storage drivers are the one that does all of these things together for you.
Now in Docker, there are multiple storage drivers which are available, which gives you a different
kind of a trade off.
Now whenever you are working with storage drivers, there is a concept of copy and write or also called
as a cow strategy which will come.
So it is important for us to understand this.
Now do remember that the cow strategy is not unique to Docker.
In fact, the Linux subsystem uses this extensively.
So you can say that this is something similar which Docker has taken.
Now a copy and write basically means that everyone has a single shared copy of the file until it is
written and then the copy is made.
So let's understand this with an example.
So let's say you have a common file over here and there are two programs which needs to access the common
file.
So in such cases, what will happen is both of these programs will make use of a single common file.
You will not have two files which will be created.
Both the programs will use a single common file.
Now let's say you have a program three Again, that program three wants to have access to the common
file.
Again, it will use that same common file.
So in this way you have resources which will be shared and disk space will be saved.
Now the question is what happens when a write is being made to this specific file?
And in this case there is a little different strategy.
So when a write operation happens within the cow strategy, what happens is that common file is being
copied to the layer where the write is happening.
So let's assume that program one is running on layer three of the Docker image, and Layer three is
making some write changes to the common file.
So now the common file which was shared over here, it will be copied to the layer three and then whatever
write which was supposed to happen, that would happen at the layer three approach.
Now do remember here that once a higher layer has made a change to this specific file, let's go a bit
up.
So let's say that you have this specific container image and let's say that there is a file at this
specific layer.
Let's call this as layer three.
Now if you make a change to the container layer, let's say you're logged into the container and you
made a change to this specific file, then this file, whatever file which was present over here, it
will be copied to this container layer completely.
So you have to be very careful.
Let's say this file in layer three is of 500 MB and you are making a change to this specific file.
So this file will be copied to your container layer completely.
So the entire 500 MB gets copied to this container layer and the change would be made at this specific
container layer.
Now whenever a read operation happens, Docker storage driver will ensure that if the file exists in
this container layer, it will not read the file in the below layer altogether.
It will read the file from the container layer, which is the read and write layer over here.
So I hope at a high level overview you understood what storage driver does.
Let's do one thing, let's jump into the practical and understand more about it.
So I'm in my Docker console and if we quickly do a Docker info over here, you will be able to see the
storage driver which is being used.
So during the earlier times FS was one of the standard storage driver, but it has recently changed.
So if you see over here you have a storage driver of overlay two and it uses a backing file system of
ZFS.
So this is the storage driver, which is.

of overlay two.

--- Block vs Object Storage ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome back to the Knowledge Portal Video series.
And in today's lecture, we will be speaking about block versus object storage.
So let's get started and have a high level overview regarding the difference between them.
Now, in a very simple terms, in block storage, the data is stored in terms of blocks.
So let's assume that this is a storage device, for example, a hard disk drive.
And in terms of block storage, what happens is whenever you install a file system like NTFS or Ext3
3 or 4, then the file system will actually divide the storage in terms of blocks.
So what really happens is the blocks are created.
So you see the blocks are getting created and once the block is created, then the storage is stored
in terms of blocks.
So I'll show you one of the examples.
Let me just log in to one of my server.
So this is the server and if you run the command, it will basically show you on what is the block size
of a device, which is dev.
SD So if you do a lsblk, I have two devices over here.
One is the SD, which is of size 25 GB and second is SD, which is a swap partition.
So in this SD, I have let me show you.
So I have a Ext4 based file system and when you do a block dev it will show you the block size of this
specific storage device.
So going back to the GPT, so this file system is divided in terms of 409, six based blocks.
So if you have a data of let's assume 809 6 or 8 KB, then you will have two blocks which will be allocated.
If you have a 12 KB of data, then there will be three blocks which will be allocated.
So it all works based on blocks.
Now one more important thing to remember is that data stored in blocks are normally read or written
entirely a whole block at the same time.
And most of the file systems that we speak about, like for USB or for Windows, we have NTFS or fat.
For Linux we have Ext4 or XFS.
Those file systems all are based on block storage.
Now when we speak about block storage, every block has a specific address and application can call
those specific blocks via a Scsi call via the block address.
Now one more important thing to remember is that there is no storage side metadata associated with a
block except the address.
So if there is an object or there is a file, let's assume there is a.
Image file which is being stored.
There is no metadata.
Like just by looking at the block, I cannot determine like what is the file being stored over here.
So that is called as a metadata and it does not also have a description or a owner.
So we'll look into what I really mean by a metadata when we talk about object storage.
So in an object storage.
It is a data storage architecture where the message or where the data is stored as object as opposed
to a block of storage.
So there are no blocks of data which has been divided.
So even if you have let's assume you have a ten MB of data, it will be directly stored as a object.
There are no blocks which are being present in the object storage one.
And a very important point to remember here.
That object is defined as a file, which is a data along with all of its metadata, which is combined
together as a specific object.
So let me show you this.
AWS S3 is type of a object based store.
So when I go to AWS S3 and let's open up any.
Bucket.
So I have a file which is dot PNG file over here.
And if I click over here within the properties, you find that there is a metadata which is attached
over here.
So let's click here.
And it will show you what is the type of metadata, which is content type, which is image dot PNG.
So this metadata basically tells on what exactly the object is all about, whether it is a text file
or it is a song or an MP3 file or it is a image file.
So this is the metadata which S3 automatically puts when you upload a file.
You can also create a custom metadata according to your requirement.
So this is also possible and this is the major difference.
So this is what this basically means.
What we call as an object in S3 is not only a file, but it is also the metadata which is connected
with the specific file.
So this file, along with all the metadata together, creates an object.
So that is the second point.
And the object ID, which is basically a unique ID associated, will be the combination of the content
of the object as well as the metadata.
So we'll be looking into this whenever the right time comes.
Otherwise it will become much complicated.
So difference between block storage and object storage.
One of the very famous difference I can tell is that object storage can be called via API.
So you see, you have a Https based interface so you can call the.
Object.
So if it if this was a text file, you could actually open this up in the browser.
Via Https.
You will see you are actually able to open this up from the browser itself.
However, you cannot really do that directly with the block storage.
So that is one of the major differences within the object storage as well as the block storage.
Now object storage are not as fast as block storage, but it does have its own importance in the industry.
So we will be speaking in more about the object and the block storage whenever the right time comes.
But this is a basic high level overview of object and block storage.
So this is it about this lecture.
I hope you got a very high level overview about both of them and if you have any doubts, suggestions,
questions, feel free to connect us at Twitter, Facebook or LinkedIn or mail us at instructors at KP
Labs dot in.

--- Changing Storage Drivers ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.

used within our workstation.

pointers that you need to remember.

on the distribution which you are using.
So for the Docker engine, which is of the community edition, the recommended storage driver for Ubuntu
is overlaid two or UFS.
Now for the Docker engine community on Debian, you have overlay two for CentOS, you have overlay two
for the fedora, you have overlay two.
Now again, if you see there are various other drivers which are available like device mapper or UFS.
Now these drivers are soon going to be deprecated and you will not see them in the future release.
So as a start, so as a best practice, if you are not sure what is the storage driver that you should
be using, Overlay two is the default one that you should be using.
And in fact whenever you install the Docker, the overlay two is the default driver that is being used.
Now before we go ahead and look into how we can change the storage driver, let's quickly look into
what is the storage driver which is being used currently.

multiple subdirectories within it.
I hope you have understood what these are.

Now, one very important part to remember is that if we are going to change the storage driver, then
whatever data that we might have for the previous storage driver would be inaccessible and whatever
containers which were launched would be inaccessible.
So let's take an example.
So let's say that our current storage driver is overlay two and you have launched ten containers with
the overlay two.

the containers would also be inaccessible.

called as Daemon dot Json.

Now since we do not want to overlay too for our demo, we'll make use of EFS.
All right, so now I'll go ahead and start the Docker.
Great.
Now if you quickly do a Docker info and if you go a bit up within the storage driver section, you see

to driver is no longer accessible.

I'll change the name to FS.

Now if you do a docker ps you should see the nginx image up and running.
However, this time the directory would be under the FS.
So you see there is a different approach in which stores the data.

be part of the FS directory here.

--- Overview of Docker Volumes ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about Docker volumes.
Now before we go ahead and understand more about what Docker volumes are, let's look into the challenges
when we are writing the files into the Containers writable layer.
Now we already discussed that whatever image that we have.
So this image is of read only.
So all of these layers are read only and whatever writes that you make, those writes happen at the
container layer.
So they are also referred as the read write layer.
Now whenever you create any file inside the container, that file gets created in this specific container
layer.
Now the problem with writing the file in this layer is that the layer is completely dependent upon the
container lifecycle.
So, for example, if this container is deleted, then all the files which have been written to the
container layer will also be removed.
So that is the first problem.
Now, the second problem is if there is some other process which is running maybe, let's say in the
container or inside the host itself, if they want to access the content inside this specific layer,
it is little difficult to do that.
Now, let's look into the second part of the challenges.
Now, the second part states that whenever you write the contents into this read write layer, we need
to make use of some kind of a storage driver so storage driver can be a UFS or overlay to any union
file system based driver.
Now, the problem with this kind of a approach is that as soon as we make use of a storage driver,
then the overall performance that you can achieve in storage driver is much more less when compared
to the host file system.
So the overall performance is also degraded.
Now in order to solve this, we basically make use of something called as Docker volumes.
Now what Docker volumes are.
So let's say that this is a container so you can mount the containers directory inside a file system
or you can mount it inside a specific area called as volume.
So now what happens is this container that you see over here, it is independent on the data which is
stored at the host level.
So irrespective of whether the container is running or container is deleted, this data that is there
within the file system or within the volume will continue to exist.
So this is one big advantage.
So even if the container is deleted, the data is still present.
And since the data is there within the host, if you have another process or if there are any other
containers which are running, those processes can also access this specific area.
Now there are three ways in which you can store the files in the host machine.
So one is through the help of volume.
So these are referred as Docker volume.
Then you have the bind mounts and for Linux you also have the temp mount.
So temp FS mount.

mount and you have the Docker volume.

exactly Bind Mount is all about.

--- Bind Mounts ---

Hey, Ron, and welcome back.
In today's video, we will be discussing about bind mounts.
Now, when you make use of bind, mount a file or a directory on the host machine is mounted into the
container.
Now, do remember that whenever you are doing a bind mount, you have to specify the path of file or
directory with the help of the full or relative path.
So when you compare the bind mounts with volumes, there are certain differences.
So when you use a volume, there is a specific area which is being created under the var lib Docker
volumes directory and that area is basically referenced to the container.
Now bind Mount basically uses the existing directory within the file system.
So let's say you already have a directory and there are certain contents inside that directory.
Now you want your container to be able to access the contents within the directory inside the host.
So in such cases you can make use of bind mounts.

the container is also deleted.

URL and it pushes to your internal storage.

--- Overview of Device Mapper ---

Hayden.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we will have a high level overview about the device mapper.
Now, the device mapper is a framework which is provided by the Linux kernel, which is generally used
for mapping the physical block devices into the higher level virtual devices.
Now this can be explained here where you have the physical block devices, here you have the virtual
block devices here, and device mapper is generally used to link them.
Now device mapper generally works by passing the data from the virtual block devices.
So any data which is stored here, it gets passed over to the underlying physical block devices, which
is provided by the device mapper itself.
Now this can be better understood with this diagram over here.
So as we were discussing, device mapper creates a logical devices on top of physical devices and provides
certain more features like Raid.
You have encryption, you have cache and various others.
So if you look into this diagram, there are multiple block pool.
So you can consider this as a physical device.
It can be like a hard disk or a SSDs and you have a logical device.
This is like a virtual device which is being created.
So this logical device is mapped to multiple blocks and this blocks is where the data would be stored.
So let's say you have a user user is creating a file in the logical device.
Now logical device in turn would be storing that file in the block pool.
So this is a block pool in the hard disk drive.
And similarly, if a file is being read, it is being initiated.
The read operation has been initiated from the block pool and it is being sent back to the user.
Now, this mapping, because let's say a single logical device can be connected to multiple blocks within
your disk.
So this pointers from the logical device to block pool are generally stored in the metadata device.
All right.
So this metadata device proves to be extremely important because it holds where exactly the data associated
with the logical devices.
All.
Now, as far as Docker is concerned, if you are using a device mapper, there are two major modes which
are being used.
One is loop mode and second is direct mode.
Now loop mode should only be used for the testing environment.
Now it basically makes use of the loopback mechanism, which is generally on the slower side.
Now talking about the direct mode production host Using direct device mapper storage must use the direct
mode.
So this is important part to remember.
Like if you are planning to use device mapper like you have concluded that device mapper should be used,
then you should make use of direct mode.
Now this mode is much, much more faster when compared to the loop mode.
So let me quickly show you on how you can enable a device mapper.
So I'm in my CLI and let's quickly do a nano on etsy Docker daemon dot Json.
All right, so within here we'll give a storage driver.
And let's call this as device mapper.
All right.
Now, before we go ahead and restart.
Let's do a Docker info.
So there are a lot of options.
In order to make things easy, you can do a grip on storage and currently you see the storage driver
is overlay two.
Now I'm hoping that you already know that whenever you change a storage driver, all of your containers,
all of your images would be inaccessible.
So let's do a Docker images.
So currently there are no images.
Let's pull a one sample image, I'll call it as BusyBox and I'll also create a new container from this.
I'll name the container as BusyBox itself, and we'll create it from the BusyBox image.
All right, So if you do a Docker, PS, you have the BusyBox container and you also have the BusyBox
image here.
Now, this container and this image is associated with the storage driver.
Now, we have already seen that currently the storage driver that is being used is overlay two.
Now, if the storage driver changes, then the container and the images would be inaccessible.
So now that we have added the entry in the daemon dot Json, let's go ahead and restart the Docker.
So I'll say systemctl, restart Docker.

And it also is giving a warning that a device mapper usage of loopback device is strongly discouraged

So basically when you enable device mapper directly, it makes use of the loopback device, which is
strongly discouraged.
Now you can also specify a custom block storage device in case you intend to.

--- Creating Volumes in Kubernetes ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in the earlier video we were discussing about the volumes in the perspective of Docker containers.
However, in today's video, we'll be discussing about volumes in perspective of Kubernetes.
Now, we already know about the two major challenges here.
The first is that on disk files within the containers are ephemeral.
So that basically means that if a container goes down, then the data becomes inaccessible or if a container
gets terminated, then the data is lost.
Now, generally, when the Cubelet finds that there is an unhealthy pod or if your application has crashed,
then your cubelet will basically create a new pod from a clean slate.
So then what happens is that whatever data that you might had within the pod is lost.
So that is the first challenge here.
The second challenge is that whenever there are multiple containers and you want to share the same data,
it becomes a challenge.
So within a pod there can be multiple containers.
And if all of them wants to share a common data, then you need a solution where all of the containers
can access a specific storage device.
Now, we already discussed about this diagram where you have a container and you can do a bind mount
or the file system.
So there is a Docker area which gets created where your container can go ahead and store the persistent
data which will not get lost even if the container is terminated.
You can also have a tmpfs mount, which is basically the data which can be stored in memory.
However, this is not persistent and if your system restarts, then your data is lost.
Now, speaking about volumes in Kubernetes, one of the great advantages of Kubernetes is that it supports
multiple types of volumes.
So let's say that you have a pod and you have connected a volume here.
So this volume need not necessarily be at the host level.
So you can have various other volumes like EBS, you can have Cinder, you have Glusterfs, you can
have local, you can have NFS and so on.
So this is just a small list.
However, the list becomes quite big.
So instead of creating a local volume within the host itself, you can even create a persistent volume
which is highly available.
So in case EBS, EBS also have the availability aspects to it here.
So there is also replication which happens behind the scenes so that your data does not get lost.
Now if you look into the official documentation associated with volumes in Kubernetes, if you look
into the types of volumes here, there are several types of volumes like the EBS, you have Azure disk,
you have Chef FS, you have Cinder CSI and so on.
The amount of flexibility you have in choosing a volume is great, and this is one of the great advantages
here.
So depending upon where you might want to store the data, you can use one of these as a volume which
can be mounted within your pod.

node's file system into your pod.
So what you do, you create a file or a directory within your host system and you mount that as a volume
within your pod.
So whenever a data in the pod is stored, it gets stored within the specific volume within the host.
