--- PersistentVolume and PersistentVolumeClaim ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about the persistent volume and persistent volume claim.
Now, generally in the development environment, staging environment, the developers will have to play
around and write their own Yaml files depending upon the pods that they want to create.
Now, in a scenario of volumes that we were discussing in the previous video, what the developer have
to do is that they have to first create a volume of a specific type.
Now we have already seen that there are a lot of type of volumes like you have EBS Center, Glusterfs,
NFS, etcetera.
So the first step is that the developer needs to be aware about a specific type of volume and its associated
configurations.
Now in the previous video we had taken a volume type of host path where we had a path of my data and
the type of directory.
So this is a simple example.
However, when you talk about glusterfs or center, et cetera.
Now developer will have to be aware about those things.
So once they create a volume of this specific type, then they can mount it on their pods.
So this is the basic approach when you directly make use of volumes within your specification files.
Now there are certain challenges here, so let's look into the challenge as far as the developer perspective
is concerned.
So the developer says that I am a developer, I just want to write my code and my pod dot yaml file.
I don't want to take care of storage provisioning and its associated configurations.
So now if let's say organization is making use of a center or a cluster, then developer, since he
has to mention these volumes, basically has to provision these volumes within the pod file.
He has to have a overview about what Glusterfs is, what Cinder is and its associated configurations.
So this is not what developer wants.
All right.
So that's the challenge of a developer.
So now you also have a storage administrator here and he says that he can take care of the provisioning
of storage and it's associated configurations.
So if a volume needs to be created in Glusterfs, then storage administrator will take care of creating
the volumes and its associated configurations.
Now the only thing that the developer has to do is that he has to create a reference to the storage
within the pod spec dot yaml file.
So the storage administrator takes care of creating these volumes part.
And all the developer has to do is he has to reference to that specific volume which the storage administrator
has created.
Now both of these things require a layer of abstraction in between because when you directly make use
of volumes, then the developer will have to do both the things within the Yaml file.
So this level of abstraction can be achieved with the help of persistent volume and persistent volume
claim.
Now a persistent volume also referred as a PV is a piece of storage in the cluster that has been provisioned
by an administrator or dynamic provisioned using the storage class.
So let's keep it simple that a persistent volume is basically an abstraction for the underlying volume
over here.
So currently within this diagram there are five volumes which are available.
You have volume one, two, three, four and five, and each volume has a certain specification.
So you have a volume one which has a size of small speed of fast.
Similarly, Volume two has a size of medium and speed of fast and so on, because many times what happens
is that within organizations, some developer might need a small volume, but they would need that.
The volume should have a speed of fast.
Some developer needs a very big volume because their application stores a huge amount of data.
So in that case they would need a huge volume.
They don't really care about the speed as long as they are able to write the data.
So typically a storage administrator can create a wide variety of volumes which a developer can use.
So this is referred as a persistent volume.
Now for each volume that is created can be of a different type.
Now we have already seen that within the Kubernetes volumes there are different type of volumes which
are available.
So you can have the first volume, which is basically from the type of EBS.
Second is NFS, third is Glusterfs, you have cinder, etcetera.
So each volume can be of a different type and different type provides a different kind of advantages.
So this provisioning of volumes of different types, different configurations can be taken care by the
storage administrator or the operations team developer do not really have to worry about this.
Now, the second important concept here is the persistent volume claim.
Now a persistent volume claim, also referred as a PVC, is a request for the storage by a user.
So once these volumes are created, a developer would have to request for those volumes.
So that request is referred as a.
Persistent volume claim.
Now, whenever you create a claim, user will have to specify various configurations like the size of
the volume that they need along with the access mode of that specific volume.
So a very simple example which can help you understand the concept is that developer says that I want
a volume of size ten GB, which has a speed of fast for my specific port.
Now if you look into this diagram, there are two volumes which has a speed of fast over here, alright?
And depending upon the storage size which is requested, which is ten GB, the appropriate volume can
be assigned to the port which is being created by the developer.
So this specific configuration where the developer says that I want a specific volume size is referenced
within the persistent volume claim.
So let's look into the overall working steps of PVC and PVC.
First is a storage administrator takes care of creating a persistent volume.
So you have a pool of persistent volumes over here.
So these pools are typically administered by DevOps team or a storage administrator team.
So you have administrator who creates multiple PVC.
So if you see first PVC is of Type NFS, you have iSCSI, you have again NFS, you have GCE.
Now developer needs some of the volumes for its ports, similar to what we were discussing here, that
a developer needs a volume of a specific size for his pod.
So what he'll do is he'll create a claim.
Creating a claim is the first part.
Claim is basically I want a specific type of a persistent volume.
So this is what a developer can raise a claim saying that I want a specific volume of size ten GB So
that can be referred as a claim.
So the second is a developer can raise a claim and depending upon the configurations that he has specified
within a claim like, like I want ten GB.
ET cetera.
A appropriate persistent volume is assigned to that specific claim.
Now, once the claim is created, all that a developer has to do is that he has to reference that claim
within the port spec dot yaml.
So within the pod spec dot yaml file he has to reference to this specific claim and that's about it.
So this is the three overview steps about the PV and PVCs.
So let's jump into the demo so that we have this concept better understood.
Now I'm in my terminal and for today's video we have three files which are available over here.
Now these three files are associated with the three steps that we were discussing in the PPD.

the storage class where your PV is associated with.

of your PVC, which is the persistent volume claim.

So currently it is 15 GB, So if you need ten GB of extra storage, you can modify it to 25 GB.

manifest if you have that available.

--- Reclaim Policy ---

Hey, everyone.
And welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about the reclaim policy.
Now, the reclaim policy in Kubernetes is responsible for what happens to the data within the persistent
volume when the associated Kubernetes persistent volume claim has been deleted.
So this can be better explained with this diagram where you have the PVC and from this PVC, there is
a PVC which has been initialized.
Now, what exactly happens when you go ahead and remove the PVC over here?
So if you remove the PVC, should the PVC stay?
Or maybe if you remove the PVC, should PV also be deleted?
And so on is something that is defined as part of the reclaim policy.
Now there are three primary types of reclaim policy that we need to be aware of.
First one is retain, second one is delete, and third one is recycle.
So in a retained reclaim policy, whenever the PVC is deleted, the associated PVC still exists and
the volume is considered to be released.
So the actual persistent volume is not deleted.
When you have a reclaim policy of retain, if you have a reclaim policy of delete, then whenever you
go ahead and delete the PVC, the associated persistent volume will also be deleted.
Third one is recycle.
So if this is supported by the overall volume plugin, the Recycle Reclaim policy will perform a basic
scrub.
That means it removes all of the data from a specific volume and makes it available again for a newer
claim.
So just from the basics of the Reclaim policy name, you can identify what exactly this does.
So let me quickly show you a demo on this.
So currently for today's demo we have a file called as PVC dot Yaml and this is basically a persistent
volume claim and the storage is five GB.
Now let's go ahead and do an apply on this specific file.
So it states that the persistent volume claim with the name of PVC has been created.
So if you quickly do a kubectl get PVC, oops, a small typo over here.
So this is the PVC that is available and associated with that.
They'll also be a persistent volume.
So this is the persistent volume.
Now, if you look into the volume, the size is five, and there is also an associated reclaim policy.
And the reclaim policy that is associated with it is delete.
So that basically means that if you go ahead and delete this PVC, the associated PVC will also be removed.
So let's go ahead and try it out.
So when you quickly do a cube, Ctl delete PVC followed by the name of the PVC, which is PVC.
So it states that our persistent volume claim has been deleted.
Now, if you quickly do a cube Ctl get PV, you will see that even the PV has been removed.
So with this, I hope you understood at a high level overview about the basics of reclaim policy.
In today's video, we looked into how if the reclaim policy is delete, then whenever the PVC is removed
associated with it, the PV will also be deleted.

--- Understanding Retain Reclaim Policy ---

Hey, Edwin, and welcome back.
Now, in the earlier video we were discussing about the basics of reclaim policy.
We looked at a high level overview about the three primary types of Reclaim policy with a small demo
on how a delete reclaim policy would work.
So in today's video, our primary focus would be towards the reclaim policy of Retain, and we'll understand
it in more detail.
So in a retain policy, whenever a persistent volume claim is deleted, the associated persistent volume
will still exist and the volume is considered as released.
So if you look into this diagram associated with this specific PV, under the Reclaim policy, it is
retained and under the status it is released.
So this would mean that the PVC associated with this PV was deleted.
So do note that in this status of release, it is not yet available for another claim because the previous
claims data remains to be within this volume.
So it can happen that the data which was associated with this is still existent.
So if you try to reclaim it with another PVC, you will not be able to do it directly.
So there are certain steps for reclamation that we need to know at a very high level.
Overview so an administrator can manually reclaim the volume with the following steps.
So when you discuss about reclaiming the volume, it is associated with the volume in the release state.
So first one is you can go ahead and delete the persistent volume.
So do note that if you delete this persistent volume, the associated storage asset in an external infrastructure
may be if it is a EBS or a GCE or even an Azure disk will continue to exist after the PV is deleted,
we'll just look into it in the demo perspective as well so that we have a better clarity.
Second is, once you have deleted the persistent volume, since we know that the underlying disk associated
with it still continue to exist, you can manually clean up the data on that associated storage accordingly
and then you can either manually delete the associated storage asset or if you want to reuse it, the
same storage, you can go ahead and create a persistent volume with the same asset definition.

So within here you have a persistent volume reclaim policy and the reclaim policy is delete.
So let's go ahead and modify this.
Let's change it to retain.
I'll go ahead and save this.
That's great.
So now if you do a kubectl get PV, you see the reclaim policy has been changed from delete to retain.
So now let's go ahead and try deleting the PVC through which our PV was associated with.
So our PVC is deleted.
Now, if you do a kubectl get PV, you will see that our PV is still existing and the reclaim policy

--- Storage Class ---

Hey, and welcome back.
Now, in today's video, we'll be discussing about the storage classes.
Now, a storage class provides a way for administrator to describe the class of storage they offer.
Now there can be different classes of storage, and each class might have a different level of quality
or different configuration by itself.
So if you look into this sample diagram, there are multiple classes of storage over here.
One is standard storage.
You can have premium storage and so on.
So depending upon this, a persistent volume might fetch the storage from a premium storage or it might
fetch it from the standard storage as well.
So the type of disk that is needed can be defined within the storage class.
Now if you look into the sample code associated with a persistent volume claim here, you can define
a storage class name.
So in this case, the storage class name is managed premium.
So in such case, depending upon the storage class configuration, the premium disk can be assigned
and whatever persistent volume that will be created, it would be much more faster when compared to
the standard storage.
So that is the basic idea behind this.
Now, when you discuss about the storage class resource by itself, do remember that every storage class
contains the field of provisioner, the parameter as well as the associated reclaim policy, which are
used by the persistent volume that are being associated with the storage class.
So if you look into this sample code, you have the kind of storage class.
Now here you have a provisioner of EBS.
Now here you also have a reclaim policy of retain.
You have certain options of allow volume expansion of true over here.
So depending upon the provisioner that is been associated with the storage class.
So in this case the provisioner is the EBS volume.
However, there can be multiple different type of volumes that can be associated under the provisional
like elastic block store.
You have Azure file, local storage OS, Glusterfs and so many others.
So depending upon the volume that you need, you can associate a appropriate volume plugin.
So let's quickly have a demo on this.
So currently if you quickly do a cube, Ctl get storage class.
Depending upon the environment, there will be a single or you can also have multiple storage classes.
So in a digital ocean based cluster you have one storage class and if you see it is a default one.
So what happens here is that within the PVC, if you do not specify a storage class, this one will
be used.
So let's quickly look into what exactly this has to offer.
Now, if you look into the configuration here, the kind here is storage class.
There are various configuration options like allow volume expansion as true.
Now if you go a bit down, you will see within the provisional you have the CSI or Digitalocean.
So this basically allows you to create the volumes in Digitalocean.
You also have a reclaim policy of delete and so on.
So within the PV, depending upon the configuration, these options would be applied.
So in case you have multiple storage class, let's assume you have one storage class for premium, one
storage class for standard, and for the PVC, you want to request the premium storage class.
So within the storage class name, you can specify the name of the storage class or tomorrow for a different
application, you need a standard disk so you can change the storage class name here from managed premium
to maybe manage standard.
So that's the high level overview about the storage classes and I hope this video has been informative
for you.

--- Important Pointers for Exams - Domain 1 ---

Hey Ryan, and welcome to the first part of the important pointers for the Docker Certified Associate
Certification.
So let's get started.
Now the first important pointer for today's video is that you should be very thorough on how you can
go ahead and create a swarm service.
So make sure that you remember this command, which is Docker service create, followed by the name
of the service, the amount of replicas and the image.
The next important point is that you should know the difference between a replicated and a global service.
So a replicated service will have n number of containers which are defined within the replica flag.
So the above command that we have discussed in point number one, this is a replica based service.
So here we are specifying the replica.
So that's the replicated service.
Second one is the global service, which will go ahead and create one container in every node of a cluster.
So one classic example is let's assume that you want to have a antivirus container in all the nodes.
So in such cases you will make use of a global service.
The next important point is that you need to be aware on the multiple approaches that are used to scale
a swarm service.
So first is Docker service scale, followed by the name of service and the amount of replicas.
Second is Docker service update, followed by the replicas and the name of the service.
So you should be very thorough with both of these.
And also you should know the difference between both of them.
Now the first one, which is the Docker service scale, allows you to specify multiple services in the
same command.
So here, apart from my web server, is equal to five.
You can also add one more service and many more.
However, for the second command, which is the Docker service update, it only allows you to specify
one service per command.
The next important point is associated with draining a swamp node.
So make sure you know how you can drain a swarm node.
So the command is Docker node update followed by the availability of drain, followed by the node name,
which is Swarm zero three.
Again, after draining once your maintenance has completed, if you want the containers to be scheduled
in that node, you can change that availability from drain to active and the containers can be scheduled
here.
Apart from that, make sure you understand the use case of Docker Stack CLI and where it can be used
and also the associated commands.
So you have Docker stack deploy followed by the compose file and the name of the compose file which
is Docker compose dot Yaml.
This is very important for you to remember.
Now the next important point is associated with the placement constraints.
So in placement constraints, depending upon the label that you associate it with a node, a task can
be scheduled or not.
So here you go ahead and you create a service which is Docker service create followed by the name of
the service, and you also add a constraint which is node label dot region is equal to is equal to Bangalore.
So what will happen is this specific service will be created on the node which has this specific label.
Now in the second scenario, you are also having node dot label dot region, which is not equal to Bangalore.
So in the second case the service will be created on a node which does not have a label of Bangalore.
Now also do note on how you can add a label to a node, so you have Docker node update followed by the
label hyphen add.
Then you specify the key value, which is region is equal to Mumbai and the worker node ID where you
want to associate the label with.
The next important point is associated with the overlay network and the associated security.
So do note that overlay networks allows containers which are across different servers to communicate
with each other.
Now the communication between the containers can be made secured with the help of IPsec Tunnel.
Now do note this specific command, which is Docker network, create hyphen hyphen, opt encrypted,
which basically is a secure overlay network followed by the driver of overlay.
And then you specify the name of overlay network that you intend to create.
So this opt encrypted is very important.
The next important point is associated with the quorum.
So in exams you can be given a specific cluster size and from here you should be able to identify on
what is the associated fault tolerance that can happen.
So remember the formula of N minus one by two, and that will allow you to quickly understand the associated
fault tolerance.
So for any cluster, be it DDR as well as UCP, the minimum amount of cluster size that you should start
with is three, because for three it allows you to have fault tolerance of one.
If you do a cluster size of two, you cannot have any fault.
Tolerance over here.
All right.
So make sure you are able to calculate the appropriate fault tolerance for the given cluster size by
applying the formula.
The next important point is associated with the troubleshooting aspects.
So at a high level overview, you should know the steps that are required to troubleshoot first.
And the very important one is to make use of the Docker System Events command, which provides you the
real time information from your server.
Second is that service deployment would be in pending state.
If Node is drained, then you have service.
Deployment can also be in pending state due to placement constraints if there are any.
And you can also further inspect a task to see the exact information on why it has failed.
The next important point is associated with the Docker stack commands.
Make sure you are familiar with the basic Docker stack commands.
So you have Docker Stack Deploy which deploys a new stack or updates an existing stack.
You have Docker Stack LS, which lists the stacks.
You have Docker Stack PS, which is list the task in a given stack and also Docker Stack services,
which lists the services in the stack.
So among all four of them, the third and the fourth one are very important.
So before sitting for the exams, make sure that you familiarize yourself with the third and the fourth
one specifically.
The next important one is associated with the child commands of a Docker service.
So here we have already discussed in the first slide on how you can go ahead and create a new service

Then, in order to list the task of one or more service, you can make use of Docker service PS command
to update a service.
You have Docker service update and to scale a service.
Again, you have Docker service scale.
So the third, fourth and fifth one are very important.
So make sure that you familiarize yourself with these commands before sitting for the exam.
The next important point is associated with the swarm routing mesh.
So routing mesh basically allows each node of a swarm to accept a connection on a published port for
any service that is running on the swarm, even if there is no container that is running.
So this can be better explained with this diagram where there are three nodes that are available.
However, a container is running only on Node one and Node two.
So you have a published port of 8080 in Node three, Node two and Node one.
So even if a client requests for a port of 880 on the Node three where there are no task running, Node
three will automatically redirect it to a specific node where your containers are running.
So that is what the swarm routing mesh is.
Now, here you have to be very familiar with this specific command, which is Docker service.
Create the name of service, the amount of replica this portion we already know by now, followed by
the published port which is 8080.
So this published port will be published to all the nodes that are part of the swarm and the target
which is 80 where containers are listening.
So you see you have 80 on the container level.
So what will happen is any request that is being made to the published port of a node will be redirected
to the target port of a given container.
So make sure that you are aware of this specific command.
The next important point is associated with updating a image of a swarm service.
This is very important.
So do note that if you want to update an image of a swarm service, you can make use of the following
command, which is Docker Swarm update followed by the image, which is my image, followed by the tag
and the service name.
So this will allow you to update the image of a service.
The next important point associated with Swarm is the concept of manager and worker node.
You should be familiar with this before you sit for the exams.
So the manager node handles the cluster management tasks like scheduling the services and others.
Now RAF implementation is used to maintain a consistent internal state.
So for all the manager you see a internal distributed state store is used for the consistent state.
Now the sole purpose of a worker node is to execute the containers which are being scheduled now.
You can also go ahead and prevent the manager node to schedule the containers.
So to prevent the scheduler from placing a task on the manager node, the availability of a manager
node can be set to drain.
The next important point is associated with Joint Token in Swarm.
So remember that joint tokens are secrets that allows a node to join a swarm.
Now, in terms of joint tokens, there are two primary types of tokens that are available.
One is for the worker role and second one is for the manager role.
So when you type Docker swarm, join token worker, then the swarm will give you the exact token that
a worker node should run and.
If you want a new manager node to join your swarm, you can run Docker Swarm, join hyphen token followed
by the manager.
A manager related command will be given to you, which you can add it as part of the new manager node.
The next important point is that you should know on how you can go ahead and initialize a swarm.
So this command, which is Docker swarm init, will go ahead and initialize the swarm and the location
where you execute this command becomes the manager in the newly created single node swarm.
After a manager is created in a single node swarm, you can then run Docker swarm, join token manager
or Docker swarm.
Join token worker to fetch the tokens associated with either a worker node or a manager node.
So let's discuss a few more miscellaneous pointers related to leaving a Docker swarm cluster.
So do note that if a worker intends to leave a swarm, he can run the Docker swarm Leave Command.
Now you can also make use of hyphen hyphen force option on a manager to remove the manager node from
a swarm.
Now also note that only use the force option in the situation where the swarm will no longer be used
after the manager leaves, such as a single node swarm.
The next important point is associated with the service discovery in Swarm.
Do note that Docker makes use of a embedded DNS to provide a service discovery for the containers running
on a single Docker engine and tasks which are part of Docker Swarm.
So this can be better explained where you have two tasks that are available.
Task one dot Myservice.
Task two dot my service and you have a client over here.
So if a client request for my service over here, then what will happen is the request will go to the
DNS server.
If you see the mapping is stored, you have task one dot.
My service has ten 004.
Then task to my service has ten 005 and so on.
So even the my service has a specific IP, so if a client requests for the DNS of task one dot my service,
then appropriate IP address will be sent back to the client.
So no longer need to remember the IP address.
You can just query by the DNS name.
All right.
So that's what the embedded DNS server means.
Along with that, when you also do a curl.
Now, since Curl is a command which is used with an external domain, which is docker.com, then it
will be forwarded to a external DNS server.
So this is also something that you should know.
Now apart from that, let's discuss few more miscellaneous pointers.
First one is associated with a Docker system DF command.
So do note that this command displays the information regarding the amount of disk space which is being
used by the Docker daemon.
This is very important for you to remember.
Second, miscellaneous pointer is associated with the Docker system events, which allows us to get
the real time events from the server.
Now these events differ per Docker object type, so you have Docker system events and you can also make
use of a filter option to filter events based on certain types.
So in the miscellaneous pointer three, we have various sub pointers here.
First important one is that do note that a Docker D is a persistent process that manages the containers.
Docker uses different binaries for the daemon as well as the client.
And to run a daemon you can make use of a Docker daemon.
Second is that for specifying the configuration option, you can make use of the daemon dot json file.
Now the location of the daemon dot Json file differs based on the operating system.
So for the Linux one it is Docker daemon dot Json and for the Windows one you have programdata docker
config followed by daemon dot Json.
So among your make sure you remember the default location for Linux.
So this specific location is very important.
The next important point is associated with inspecting a specific object.
So here you can make use of a docker container inspect to display a detailed information of one or more
container.
So for example, you get a question on how you can see the volumes that are associated with a given
container.
So in that case, you can make use of a Docker container inspect where you can see the exact volumes
that are associated with the container.
The last important pointer for today's video is the difference between a container and virtual machines.
And yes, this is important for the exams as well.
So make sure that you know, the fundamental difference between a virtual machine and container.
Primary difference is that the container makes use of the host resources, specifically the kernel over
here.
However, for the virtual machine, you will see that virtual machine has their own kernel.
So if you have ten different virtual machines, each virtual machine will have their own kernel and
primary.
For this, the entire size of the VM increases.
However, that is not the case of a container.
So in the case of container, if you see various things like the host operating system is shared among
all the containers that are running on a given host.

and a entrypoint instruction so a cmd can be overridden.
However, entrypoint cannot be overridden.
The third important pointer is the use case where you should use ad versus wget or curl.
Now, because image size matters using an ad instruction to fetch the packages from a remote URL is
strongly discouraged and you should make use of a curl or w get instead.
So in the first use case you are making use of AD, then you have to separate run instruction.
However, in the second approach you have a single run instruction and you are combining it with multiple
ends to not only use curl for downloading the archive packages, but also using a extract functionality.
So the second one is much more efficient command because here one layer will be created as opposed to
three of the first example.
The next important point is associated with the Workdir instruction.

So this basically curl command is used to make a Http request inside the container on localhost, which

Now it will exit with a zero echo status if a response is good or with a one which basically indicates
that things are not working fine.
So in such cases it will tell the docker that a container is unhealthy.
The next important point is associated with tagging a Docker image, so you should know the exact syntax

--- Important Pointers for Exams - K8s ---

Hey, everyone, and welcome to the Part seven of the important pointers for exams.
The primary focus for today's video would be associated with the topics of Kubernetes.
Now, the first important pointer is associated with the Kubernetes pod.
So a pod in Kubernetes represents a group of one or more application containers and some shared resources
for that specific container.
So not only a pod will have a container, but there can be multiple resources like volumes that can
be part of a single pod.
The second important point is associated with the deployments.
So deployment provides replication functionality with the help of replica set along with various additional
capabilities like rolling out changes, rolling back changes and others if required.
So during the deployments, you can go ahead and set a desired state and the deployment will ensure
that the desired number of containers are always running.
Now, when you discuss about deployment, understanding the rolling update related functionality is
very important.
So the deployment updates pods in a rolling update fashion.
So for example, in this diagram you have an image of Nginx.
So tomorrow you change this image of Nginx to image of Apache.
So the deployment will not go ahead and terminate all the pods at once.
It will do it at a rolling basis so you can further customize it by making use of Max unavailable and
max search to control the rolling update process.
The next important point is associated with the config maps.
Do note that config maps allows you to decouple the configuration artifacts from image content to containerize
the application to make it portable so you can have a central config maps which will have certain configuration
file.
And whenever you launch a pod you can associate a config map with them.
The next important point is associated with the Kubernetes secret.
So a secret is an object that contains small amount of sensitive data such as password, a token or
a key.
So whenever a pod gets launched, you can associate a Kubernetes secret with them.
The next important point is associated with a Kubernetes service.
So service is basically an abstraction which defines a logical set of pods and allows a policy through
which you can access them.
Now, when you discuss about Kubernetes service, there are two important service type that you need
to remember.
First one is the cluster IP.
So when a service type is of cluster IP and internal cluster IP address is assigned to that specific
service.
Now, since it is an internal cluster IP, it can only be reachable from the cluster.
Remember this you cannot reach a cluster IP from the internet.
Now this is also a default service type.
The next one is the nodeport.
So if a service type is Nodeport, then Kubernetes will allocate a port on every worker node.
So each node will proxy that port into your specific service.
So during exams, if you see a use case where a service needs access from the internet, then Nodeport
is the right one.
Cluster IP will not be the best choice there.
The next important point is associated with the volume in Kubernetes.
So do note that one of the benefits of Kubernetes is that it supports multiple volumes.
So you have EBS, cinder Glusterfs and so on.
So multiple volume types can be used for Kubernetes.
Now when it comes to volumes, you have to be very thorough for your concept of the persistent volume
and persistent volume claims.
So persistent volume is a piece of storage in a cluster that has been provisioned by an administrator
or dynamically provisioned using a specific storage class.
So when you discuss about the dynamic volume provisioning, do note that the dynamic volume provisioning
allows storage volumes to be created on demand.
So when you discuss about the volume types like EBS specifically, whenever you are using a Kubernetes
cluster in AWS, you can make use of dynamic volume provisioning.
So the dynamic volume provisioning feature eliminates the need for a cluster administrator to pre-provision
the storage.
Instead, it can automatically provision the storage when it is requested by the users.
The next important point is associated with the persistent volume claim.
So persistent volume claim is a request for a storage by a user.
So within the claim, a user specifies the size of the volume along with the access mode.
So, for example, a developer says that I want a volume of size ten GB which has a speed of fast for
my pod.
So accordingly a specific persistent volume will be associated with it.
The next important point is associated with the requests and limits.
So requests and limits are two ways in which you can control the amount of resources that can be assigned
to a pod.
So a request is something that is guaranteed to get for that pod and limit is.
Make sure that the container does not take notes resources above a specific value.
So again, let's assume that you have defined a request of 256 MB of Ram.
So any node where your pod is assigned, that pod is guaranteed to get this much amount of memory.
Now limit, on the other hand, is a maximum memory which will limit the maximum amount of resource
that a pod can use.
Now, depending upon the requests and limits that you set for a pod, a Kubernetes scheduler decides
the idle node to run the pod depending on the configuration here.
So, for example, if your pod requires eight GB of Ram, however, there are no nodes which are available
within your cluster that has eight GB of Ram, then your pod will never get scheduled.
This is one important part to remember.
The next important point is associated with the labels.
So labels are a key value pair that are attached to the objects such as pods.
So do note the commands that allow you to list a specific object based on labels.
So in the below command it will show you the list of all the nodes along with the labels that are associated
with the node.
So you have the hyphen hyphen hyphen labels, which will also show you the label associated with the
nodes here.
Now, along with labels, you should also be familiar with the selectors.
So selectors allows you to filter the object based on labels.
So for example, let's say you want to see all the pods, which has a label of envy is equal to production.
So in such cases you can do a kubectl get pods hyphen L, which stands for label followed by the label
which is envy is equal to production.
So the output of it will be all the pods which has this specific label.
The next important point is associated with the Daemonset.
So a daemon set can ensure that all the nodes runs a copy of a specific pod.
So this is very similar to the global service in Swarm.
The next important point is associated with the DNS for service and pods.
So in Kubernetes the communication can happen both with the IP address as well as DNS.
So understand this specific use case that we are discussing.
So let's say that you have a namespace one and under the namespace one you have one pod and you have
one service and you also have a namespace two under the namespace two you have pod three and pod four.
So if the pod four of a namespace referred as the Ns2 wants to communicate to the service A as part
of the namespace NS one, then the pod can simply reference to the object name which is a followed by
the namespace name which is NS one.
However, if the communication is happening inside the namespace, then you do not have to explicitly
specify the namespace.
You can just refer A and the appropriate DNS would work perfectly well.
All right, so make sure you remember this.
That for the cross namespace communication of name followed by namespace name is important.
The next important point is associated with taints and toleration.
Remember that taints are used to repel the pods from a specific node.
So in order to enter the taint worker node, you need to have a special path.
So that special path is referred as toleration.
Now when you discuss about taint, there are two important taint that you need to remember.
First one is the no schedule.
Second one is the no execute.
So when the no schedule is set, no pod will be allowed to schedule on to that specific node unless
and until it has a matching toleration.
So pods that are already running on that specific node will continue to run provided no schedule is
set at a later stage.
Now, compared to that, you also have a no execute which evicts the pods from the node even if the
pods are already running.
So in the examples, if you see a use case where you want to evict all the pods of a specific node,
then you can set a taint of no execute there.
The next important point is associated with the readiness probe.
Do note that it can happen, that the application is running but temporarily unavailable to serve the
traffic.
So for example, application is running, but it is still loading some of the large configuration files
from an external vendor.
So in such cases you do not want to kill the container, but we also do not want it to serve the traffic
so this can be better understood.
So a new container is being launched which is still updating itself.
So the status here would be not ready.
So in such cases the load balancer can serve the 100% of the traffic to a node which has the pod in
the ready state.
So this is where you can make use of the readiness probe to define the status of a given pod.
The next important point for today's video is associated with the Reclaim policy.
So persistent volume can have various reclaim policies which are.
Retain, recycle and delete.
Make sure that, you know, these reclaim policies.
So, for example, if a reclaim policy of retain is set, then if a user deletes a persistent volume
claim, then the corresponding persistent volume is not to be deleted.
Instead, it is moved to a release phase where all of its data can be manually recovered as opposed
to the retain.
You also have a delete.
So in such case, dynamically provisioned volumes is automatically deleted when the user deletes the
corresponding persistent volume claim.
So whenever a PVC is deleted associated with that PVC, whatever PV present, that PV will also be removed
in case if you do not want that to be removed, then you can set the reclaim policy to retain.
Now you also have a recycle which basically if supported by the underlying volume plugin, this will
go ahead and basically scrub your volume, which is hyphen RF your volume.
So all the files which are part of the volume will be removed and the volume then will be made available
for a new claim to happen.
The last important point for today's video is associated with the expansion of the persistent volume
claim so you can expand a PVC if its storage class has the allow volume expansion field to be true.
So in this specific diagram, you see that you have an explicit parameter of allow volume expansion
of true.
So to request a larger volume for a specific PVC, you can edit the PVC object and specify the larger
size here.
So this triggers the expansion of the volume that backs the underlying persistent volume here.
So that's the high level overview about some of the important pointers for Kubernetes.

Now, along with that, you should know in detail about the retain policy.

However, if it is within the release state, it is not yet available for another claim because the
previous claims data still remains to be within the volume.
So you should also know the basic steps which are required for reclamation of such PV.
The next important point is that you should know at a high level overview about the difference between
a volume and a persistent volume.
So there are two primary types of storage abstractions.
One is volume and persistent volume.
So Kubernetes volumes only exist while the containing pod exists.
So once the pod is deleted, the associated volume will also be deleted.
So the volume does not exist outside of the Pods lifecycle and it is used for storing the temporary
data.
As opposed to that the Kubernetes persistent volume remains available outside of the Pods lifecycle
as well.
